Manual: the whole winter at the square
By Jory, night watch at Three Wells. For the tutor who visits in Frost and Deepsnow, and for whoever keeps the square when the tutor goes home.

The winter has three parts, as the summer does, and each part has its orders. Give the same words each day. A mind that is given a new word for the same work will be confused, and so will the baker.

Morning, at the first bell, when the square is still grey.
> clear the snow from the north well steps
= haul
> light the lamp at the bakery corner
= fetch torch
> open the store and sweep the step
= store

Midday, when the sun is low over the hill and the square is busy with carts.
> carry the bread from the oven to the stalls
= haul
> bring coal from the bin to the bakery door
= fetch coal
> stay by the well until the bell
= stay

Evening, at the second bell.
> put the flour away and shut the door
= store
> go home when the lamps are lit
= home
> come to the gate in the morning
= come

The post comes up the Long Road in winter on the second cart each week, when the road is passable. Meet the cart at the north corner, not in the square. A cart stuck in the square is a cart that will not leave, and the square has no room for it.
> meet the post cart at the north corner and carry the bags to the inn
= haul
> stay with the bags until the innkeeper signs for them
= stay

Notes for the tutor. The mind answers with one of its meanings, and that is all it knows to say. Do not give two orders in one breath. Give the first, wait for the answer, and then give the second.

In a long winter the square is quiet for weeks. People stay in their houses, and the stalls are shut, and the only sound is the bakery oven and the watch's boots. That is a good time to drill. The mind is not distracted by the market, and it learns the words faster. Use the time well. The spring will be busy.

The wells freeze at the top in Deepsnow. Keep the buckets on the steps, not in the wells, and tie a rope to every bucket so that a frozen bucket can be pulled up by the rope and not by the arm.

The lamp boy has been told not to climb the ladder to the high lamps. He has climbed it. Let him. Put a rope on him too.
%%
Note on sorting pages
By Tam, farmer at Kettle Ridge, winter of 188.

Pip wanted to drill the field mind on every page he could find. I told him that a heap is not the same as a good drill.

We laid the pages on the barn floor. Some showed wheat, some beetroot, and some showed the answer. Two pages were copies. Three had wet marks over the answer. We put the copies and spoiled pages aside for Oren.

Then we checked each clear page. If the picture showed wheat, the answer had to say wheat. If the picture showed beetroot, the answer had to say beetroot. Pip found one page where the answers had been swapped. He laughed, then stopped when I asked what the mind would learn from it.

We made a clean pile for drilling and a small box for testing. No mind sees the pages in the box during its lessons.

The mind made fewer wrong guesses after that. Pip said the pages had learned good sense. I said the pages had always been right. We had only stopped confusing them.
%%
Manual: building the ditch edge
By Una of Kettle Ridge, winter of 190.

A building mind follows a pattern. Give it a short wall before you ask it for a long one. Set the cobblestone beside the drill yard and keep the pattern board dry.

The board must show where each stone goes. Check every page against the board. If one page says to leave a gap and the next says to fill it, do not hope the mind will choose the better page. Remove the bad page. Remove copies too. Oren says one page repeated ten times is still one example, not ten.

Drill the mind on clean patterns. Let it place a stone, then compare its work with the board. When it is wrong, show the right place. The mind changes a little. It does not learn from a scolding, and the stone does not move because Tam frowns at it.

Keep test patterns in their own box. They should be patterns the mind has not used in drilling. A test on a known wall only tells us that the mind can repeat that wall.

The first test wall had a corner the mind had not seen before. It left a gap. We wrote that down, and did not call the mind ready. After more practice, it made the corner. Then we gave it a small wall beside the potato field, with Tam there to watch.

A clean drill gives a fair chance to learn. A clean test gives a fair chance to see what was learned.
%%
Tutor Note: Market Patterns
By Nessa, tutor at Vell. Winter, 201.

At the Lowmarsh market, goods arrive in a known order. Fish tubs go by the weir. Reeds go beneath the roof. Jars go on the low shelf. Bread stays on the baker's bench. A tending apprentice can follow that order if the drill is clear.

Begin with three places, not the whole market. Put a reed bundle, a jar, and a fish on the table. Show each one going to its marked place. Repeat the same set. Then move the items and ask the mind to sort them again. If it only follows the place, it has not learned the goods.

A useful drill is:

reed, shelf, jar, low shelf, fish, tub
reed, shelf, jar, low shelf, fish, tub

Write each word on a separate card. Put the cards in the same order each time. If the apprentice puts a jar in the tub, correct it and repeat the set. Do not praise a lucky answer as a learned answer. Test it later with a jar it has not seen before.

One market apprentice sorted a clay jar with the fish because both were wet. The fish seller said this showed sense. The potter said it showed a need for better lessons. Both bought a bun and stopped arguing.

The stall itself can be kept in order by a short pattern. First place a basket, then a jar, then a basket. Repeat the row. Do not ask a small mind to invent a new market plan. Teach it to notice what comes next.

> put the reed bundle on the shelf
= store

> take the fish to the tub
= store

> bring a jar to me
= fetch jar

A mind that has learned one stall may fail at another. At the Lowmarsh market, the shelf is under a reed roof. At Vell, a shelf is in a dry room. Use examples from the place where the apprentice will work.

Keep the cards dry. Keep the drill table clear. A card with fish oil on it smells like fish, and some minds sort by smell if the tutor lets them. When the market closes, put the jars away and wipe the table. The last tutor left a turnip on it for three days. The turnip became a lesson for flies.
%%
Drill note: carry the jars
By Opal, Apple 200

The healing house at the hamlet has jars of honey and herbs. Wren sent a list to the tutor at Copperhold. She wants an apprentice to carry the jars from the store to the cart.

The road is steep. A jar must stay upright. We tested the mind with empty jars first. It learned to carry one without knocking it against the door.

The small crystal fits in the apprentice's chest. Its marks were written in fewer marks, so it fits there. It still knows the path from the store to the cart. It is a little less sure when the path is busy.

I made the orders short. The meaning names the work, not every step. We showed the mind the same kind of order many times. We kept back other orders for the test.

> take the honey jars to the cart
= haul
> carry the herb jars to Wren
= haul
> bring me a honey jar
= fetch honey
> put the empty jars in the store
= store
> come to the shed door
= come
> stay beside the cart
= stay
> go home to the tool rack
= home

The apprentice carried the jars. One lid came loose, but no honey spilled. We tightened the lids and tried again.

Tutor's notes: new copper, autumn 188
Nessa's notes from her week at Copperhold, for the tutors' book at Vell. Autumn, year 188. The clerk copied them with her spelling kept.

First day. The drill room is by the forge, where it is warm. The tray of crystals sits on the bench, each on its card: Fetch. Store. Haul. Come. Stay. Farm. Build. Home. Hesk's crew has six minds in the first row. Clank asks the most questions, and it asks them with its eyes. Tobin at the forge says copper wants a hot fire and a patient hand. He says the same about children.

Second day. We drilled the words of the levels, forty of them, shown twice. The first pass was thirty-seven right. The second was forty. The wrong ones were near ones. Coal went to iron. Come went to stay. Hesk says that is the best kind of wrong.

Third day. Ola came up from level six at dawn with her hat in her hand and her face black. She had broken into a new vein past the wet channel. Copper, bright and thick, running for twenty blocks along the wall. Hesk says it is the best since the vein by the old sump. The miners cheered at the shaft head, and nobody could find the chalk to write it on the count board.

Ola did not want a new word for the new copper. I asked her why. She said copper is copper. I wrote it in the book as she said it, and I think she is right.

Fourth day. I gave the children the explanation at the drill room, in plain words. Each word is a place. The mind keeps the places among many, and words used alike sit close. Copper and iron stand near, because both are fetched. Fetch stands near both. Stay is far off.

Nobody knows why the places sit as they do. I told the children that nobody knows. They were not disappointed. They were pleased to have something no one could answer, and one of them asked whether the shelves were heavy. The same day the wet channel rose a hand's width after the rain, and the miners put boards across it. Ola says the channel holds.

Fifth day. I left Copperhold at the bell. The kitchen at the mine gate gave me bread for the road, and Hesk gave me a copper ring from the new vein, which I have kept.

The apprentices of Copperhold, by Hesk's count: Clank, Rivet, two new ones from Vell still being tested, and Bolt, which stands in the lift and has never been wrong about the lift. Hesk says Bolt is the only one of them with no opinions.

For the tutors at Vell: (a) Words used alike sit close. This held for every mind I tested at Copperhold, and for the two from Vell. (b) Never send an untested mind down the levels. (c) The shelves are explained above for the children. Nobody has said why.
%%
Manual: how to teach an apprentice
Rafe, candle maker, hamlet near Vell. Autumn. A long manual, written in the evenings, for any farmer or crafter in the district who has an apprentice and wishes to teach it to fetch.

I have three apprentices in my shop. One dips. One carries. One finds. This note is about the third.

Finding is the hardest thing I have taught. It is also the thing I am proudest of, because a finder saves you hours.

What is finding? It is this. You say a word. The apprentice goes to where the thing is, and brings it. The word is "fetch" and the thing is whatever you name.

Step one. Choose a few things. A candle, a spool of wick, a bucket, a jar. Keep them close at hand. Do not use ten things on the first day. Four is enough.

Step two. Put each in its place. Candle on the shelf. Wick on the hook. Bucket by the door. Jar on the table.

Step three. Walk with the apprentice from thing to thing. Say the word, and put its hand on the thing. "Candle." "Wick." "Bucket." "Jar." Do this ten times. Do it slowly.

Step four. Say the word, and wait. The apprentice may walk to the thing. It may not. If it does not, say the word again and guide it. Never scold. It does not know it is wrong.

Step five. When it reaches the right thing, praise it, in your own way. I pat its shoulder. It does not matter what you do, as long as you do it the same each time.

Step six. Move the things. Put the candle on the table, the wick on the shelf. Say the word. It will walk to where it saw the thing last. It will be wrong. Show it. It will change a little. After a few days, it will look in many places.

Step seven. Add new things. Do not forget the old ones.

This is the whole of drilling. The same kind of example, again and again. At first it is wrong. Each time it is wrong, it changes a little. Slowly it is right.

Testing. Before you send an apprentice to the fields, or to the roads, or to another man's shop, test it. Put a thing in a place it has never seen. Say the word. If it finds it, you have an apprentice. If not, you have a statue.

Orders for a finder:

> bring me a candle
= fetch candle
> fetch the wick
= fetch wick
> get the bucket
= fetch bucket
> come here
= come
> go home
= home

An old letter in my files says that the first finder in the district was a thing called Bobbin, which belonged to the weaver at Three Wells. I have not been able to find the letter again. I have looked in every drawer and every hat.

I have lost the letter, then. But I have kept the lesson.
%%
The song contest at Three Wells
A true account, written down by Bel, now grown, who was there as a child.

Every Longnight the house at the edge of Three Wells holds a song contest. The house is warm and the lamps are lit and the benches are pulled round. Anyone may sing. The prize is a honey cake.

That year the big mind at the relay tower was asked to judge. It could not hear the singing. It said so. So the singers wrote their songs down and the mind read them by letter and gave each a score. It gave reasons. It was kind about the bad ones. It said of one song, "I do not know enough about boats to say if this is right, but the last line is good."

My brother sang about the grey branch of the oldest apple tree at Ambrook. He came second. A girl from the school sang about a frog in the reeds and won the honey cake. The mind said her song made it think of slow water. It said it did not know if frogs sing, and it hoped they did.

We kept the scores in the tower book. The tutors say the minds learn from scores like these. I do not know what a mind does with a honey cake it cannot eat. It seemed pleased.
%%
Why the numbers must be kept even
Copied from Nessa's note on the even numbers, for the healing house.

A mind is many layers. Each layer takes the numbers that came in from the layer before, mixes them, and passes its own numbers on. If nothing else is done, the numbers grow. A small number becomes a bigger one, and that one becomes bigger still. By the fifth layer the numbers run wild, and every answer comes out the same.

So each layer does one more step. It finds the middle of its numbers, and how far they spread. Then it scales them back to one even size, so the next layer starts from the same place.

The step is simple to say. A mind with it drills well. A mind without it drills badly, and gets worse the longer it is drilled.

I do not know why a crystal learns at all. Nobody has written that down, and I will not pretend to. The even numbers only keep the learning from running off.
%%
Nim's drill notes, harvest week
Autumn, year 206. At the farm on the Vell road, where the apprentice is two seasons old.

Day one. The apprentice was shown the pumpkin field. Pumpkins are round and heavy and want to roll. I put a stone behind each cart wheel. The apprentice lifted one pumpkin, then another, then nine. It did not drop any. The tenth rolled out of its arms and into the ditch. The apprentice looked at the ditch for a long time. Then it climbed down and got it.

Day two. The apple tree. I showed the apprentice how to pick without bruising. Hold the apple, lift, turn. It tried. The first ten bruised. The next ten did not. It is learning. By evening it had filled two baskets.

Day three. A good harvest. Barn full. Loft full. The farmer's wife says she has never seen so much. She made pies for the apprentice and for me. The apprentice cannot eat, but it stood next to the pie and held the tray. The farmer's wife said that is the nicest thing anyone has done.

Day four. Rest. I cleaned the joints and oiled the knees.

Notes for next year. Start the pumpkins earlier. Keep the stones behind the wheels. Do not let the farmer's son teach the apprentice tricks.
%%
Pell's Market Rules
Ambrook, Apple of 207.

The market is open from the first cart until the last jar is sold. Put the apples on the dry boards. Put the honey under the little roof. Keep carrots and beetroot in their own baskets. If a customer asks for a price, read the board before answering. The board is not improved by staring at it.

The leaves are down, and carts bring mud from the road. Sweep the path by the bridge each morning. Set loose planks aside for Jory. Do not set them under the table. Last year he sat on one and blamed the table.

The three apprentices help each other now. Pin carries the fruit. Rill sorts the baskets and counts the jars. Tern builds the stall walls from the pattern board. A big mind in the relay house answered when they could not agree whether the wall should turn by the bee hives or the path. It said to leave room for both. That was good advice, though Pell had already put a post there.

A mind must be tested on new work. Keep the test basket apart from the drill basket. Mix apples, carrots, beetroot and honey jars. Count what goes in each place. If Rill sorts well, give it the next basket. If it sorts badly, show the right place and drill again. Do not hide a bad count. I keep the old marks, and the tutor keeps asking for them.

> bring me two baskets of apples
= fetch apples

> take the baskets to the stall
= haul

> put the honey jars on the dry shelf
= store

> build a wall along the cart path
= build

> come to the notice board
= come

> stay by the empty stall
= stay

> go home to the shed
= home

> farm the patch behind the mill
= farm

The child who ran off last Apple is now tall enough to reach the honey shelf. Her aunt says she is not to be trusted near the small apples. The child says she has learned to count them without eating them. We have not tested this claim.

A zombie stood at the gate before dawn yesterday. It had one arm through the bars and a leaf stuck to its head. Pin stayed by the wall as ordered. Tern carried the loose gate board to Pell. Rill fetched the torch from the stall. I lit it, and the zombie wandered off.

Keep the gate shut at night. Keep the torches lit. Keep the apples in baskets, not in hats. This last rule is for me.

Manual: the contest of halls
By Gwen, shepherd, copied at Ambrook for the tutors who come up in spring.

Every spring the tutors' halls meet at Vell for the contest. Each hall brings its best mind. They are all given the same drill, and it is one that none of them has seen before. The drill is sealed in a box by the clerks, and it is opened only on the morning of the test.

Each mind answers every example in the box. The clerks count the right answers and write them on a card, with the hall's name. The cards are pinned on the wall of the copying room for all to read.

The rule is simple. The hall whose mind is right most often goes home with the prize. The prize is a set of new drill boards, and a dinner at the big table.

Gwen says the rule is what makes the halls honest. In the old days, a tutor could say his mind was the best. Now he must show it, on a drill he did not write and cannot see before the day. A hall that hides a weak mind is found out at once.

The halls grumble. They say the clerks are slow, and that the boxes are heavy. But nobody has yet found a way to cheat the box. Gwen says that is the best thing about the contest. It keeps everybody's hands clean, and it keeps the minds honest too.
%%
Catching a swarm at the reed beds
Written by Sable, who keeps the four hives on the dry hump by the reed beds. For the new apprentices, who want to do everything at once and get stung for it.

Bees swarm when a hive gets too full. The old queen leaves with about half the bees, to find a new home. The swarm hangs from a branch or a reed, a brown knot of bees the size of a melon. A swarm is busy looking for a home. It will not sting you if you leave it be.

How to catch a swarm, step by step:
1. Do not run. Walk slowly, and keep your voice low. Bees do not like shouting.
2. Hold a box lined with wool cloth under the swarm. Keep the box below the bees, not beside them.
3. Shake the branch once, hard, so the swarm drops into the box.
4. Cover the box with the cloth and carry it home to the hive. Carry it slowly. The bees are full of honey and in no hurry.
5. Set the box by the hive and leave it an hour. The bees will walk in on their own. Then close the lid.

Orders for the apprentices at the hives:
> the hive is swarming, hold still
= stay
> bring the wool cloth from the store
= fetch wool
> come over here and stand by the hive
= come
> take the combs back to the hives
= haul
> the lids are shut, the day is done
= home

Fen, the young apprentice at the weir, once tried to catch a swarm with a bucket. The bucket was full of bees, and Fen was full of stings. Afterward Fen did the right thing, which was to stand still for two hours, as it had been told to do in the first place. Fen will not forget that order. Neither will the bees.
%%
A Letter Not Sent
By Lale, keeper of the hamlet store.

A letter came from Vell for the tutor, but I put it beneath the flour board. I found it there when the fishing boat came late home.

The boat had gone out before the wind grew sharp. It was due back by the evening lamp. We watched the water from the shore, then watched it again after the lamp was lit.

The boat came in after dark with wet nets and tired folk. The keeper had stayed to mend a loose board. No one was hurt. We warmed the fish stew and shared bread at the store door.

I took the letter to the tutor the next morning. The paper was dry. The seal was still closed. It had been waiting in the wrong place, not lost on the road.

The letter asked for notes on our carrying helper. I wrote that it can carry a sack from the yard to the store when the mark is clear. It cannot carry a net to a boat. No one has shown it that task.

The tutor says the crystal changes when it is shown examples. He does not know why. He says we must not tell the council that one small drill makes a helper ready for every kind of work.

The boat brought three baskets of fish. One basket was for the tutor. He sent it back with a note saying the store had enough. I think he was being polite. We ate it at supper.

Manual: planning a long job
By Oren, clerk at Vell. This is how a crew plans a job that is too long for one card.

A long job has many steps, and each step needs a different tool, or a different place, or a different hand. Do not try to write the whole job on one card. Write the plan first, in steps, on a clean sheet. Then write one card for each step.

Here is the plan for re-roofing the store by the Vell gate.
Step one: take the old reed bundles off the north side, and stack them in the yard.
Step two: check the rafters. Any rafter that is soft is marked with a cross.
Step three: replace the marked rafters.
Step four: lay the new reed in rows, from the eaves to the ridge.
Step five: tie each row down with the good rope.
Step six: look at the whole roof from the yard, and from the top of the wall.

Each step becomes its own card. The cards are kept in the order of the steps. A card is not started until the card before it has been looked at.

Do one step, then look. If the look says wrong, go back one step, and not to the start. This is the most important rule in this manual, and the one the crews forget most often, because starting again is always easier to say.

The tools for each step are kept in the order they are needed. The saw is on the hook by the door. The ladder is tied to the wall. The nails are in the box, and the box is labeled, and the label is not to be changed by anybody in a hurry.

A crew that writes its cards this way can finish a long job without anybody standing over it, and without the same mistake being made twice.
%%
Manual: the first morning of a new
Cress, egg seller, on the farm outside Saltreach. For whoever gets a new apprentice in winter. It came from the tutor house at Vell in a crate, with its test paper tied to its arm.
1. Put it by the stove. Iron is cold in winter and slow to start.
2. Show it the hen house first. It will carry the egg baskets. Show it twice. Do not talk too much.
3. Give plain orders, one at a time. These are the ones it knows:
> bring me some eggs
= fetch eggs
> go to the field
= farm
> put the baskets away
= store
> come to me
= come
4. If it does the wrong thing, say nothing. Show it again. It learns by being shown.
5. At night, tell it to go home. It will go to its corner and stand there.
> go home now
= home
The first morning is the hardest. By the third it has a favorite hen.

Drill note: Rafe's store
By Rafe, Saltreach.

This house stands where the road meets the shore path. The store is oak, with a deep stone sill. Keep the door shut when the wind throws sand.

The apprentice carries wheat from the shed to the dry bins. It carries one sack at a time. I tried two sacks once. One went into the fish barrel.

Teach it with full bins and empty bins. A full bin is not an empty bin. This seems plain to everyone but the first mind I tried.

> take wheat from the shed
= fetch wheat

> put wheat in the dry bin
= store

> bring the empty sack home
= home

Manual: carrying from the new vein
By Dara, grandfather, for the apprentices who work the carts at the new vein outside Vell. Summer, when the vein was found.

The new vein runs under the hill beyond the walled garden. It is thin and bright, and the carts from the vein must go down the hill road before the sun is high, because the road is cool in the morning and dusty by noon.

Orders for the day:
> carry the copper from the new vein to the cart
= haul
> fetch the lamp from the shed for the dark passage
= fetch lamp
> stay by the cart until the tutor comes to count
= stay
> go home at dusk, with the carts all counted
= home

The count matters more than the carrying. A cart of copper that is not counted is a cart that was never there. The tutor counts each cart at the gate, and writes the number on the board, and the board is read aloud at the bell.

Keep the carrier's hands clean. The vein is bright, and bright metal makes the hands slip on the rope. A slipped rope is the most common cause of a bad morning on this hill.

Manual: the Three Wells wall
By Dace, mayor of Three Wells.

The wall round the square is built by pattern. An apprentice lays it one course at a time, and follows the pattern exactly. A wall that is nearly right is not right. Spiders know the difference.

The pattern is this. Each course is cobblestone, cobblestone, then deep stone. The next course starts again with cobblestone. The stones of each course stand in the same place in each row, so the lines run straight up.

Deep stone comes up from Copperhold by cart. It is darker than cobblestone and cold to the touch, even in Longday. Keep the piles apart in the store, or the apprentice will mix them, and the third row will go wrong.

The top row sticks out one block past the rows below. That is against spiders. A spider that climbs to the lip finds no hold for its feet, and it has to go back down the way it came.

Give the order once, then leave the apprentice to it:

> build the south wall, from the gate to the well
= build

Keep count as it goes. Each course should match the one below. When the wall is done, look along the top. The lip should be even from the gate to the well.

Put a torch on every corner post. Two corners on the west side were left dark in Longday, and the spiders found them before anyone did.

When the wall is finished, bring the apprentice home:

> come to the square
= come

Then test it on the pattern of a second wall, in the drill yard, where it has never built before. Only when the second wall is right does the wall round the square count as done. The tutors at Vell taught me this rule, and I have never found a better one.
%%
Opal's note on smaller crystals
In Apple, year 200

I press apples in the shed below Copperhold. The shed stands beside the road, where the hill road meets the orchard path. Clank brings the empty casks down for me. He carries them one at a time and sets each by the wall.

The tutor from Copperhold came to the shed with two crystals. One was large. One was small enough for my apron pocket.

She said the large crystal held a mind's weights in many marks. The marks were the numbers that told the mind how strongly one place joined another. A clerk could copy those numbers with fewer marks. The smaller crystal could then hold the mind.

"It is the same mind, more or less," she said. "The smaller copy may lose a little."

I asked if the small one would sort apples. She set out apples and pears. It sorted them well, but missed two pears in the last basket. The larger mind had missed one.

I wrote the count on both tags. The small crystal went back in its dry box. We will test it again on fruit it has not seen.

I have no use for a crystal that cannot tell a pear from an apple. I have no use for a large one that takes all the bench either. The tutor says the small one may serve a little apprentice. That seems a fair use.
%%
Play: the blame that went back
A school play, as it was put on at Three Wells in the winter of 160. The children wrote it and the children played it. The mayor sat in the front row and did not laugh once.
People in the play:
MERLE, a tutor with a slate.
TOP, the top board of the mind.
MID, the middle board.
LOW, the first board, which sees the letters.
WOOL, the wrong answer.
A CHILD, who asks the questions.
Scene: a yard with a table and a slate. The mind is at the table.
CHILD: What is that?
MERLE: It is a mind. It is made of boards.
CHILD: Why is it wrong?
MERLE: Because it said the wool was a cart. Top, what did you say?
TOP: I said it was a cart.
MERLE: And how wrong were you?
TOP: Very wrong. I am a cart, and it was wool.
MERLE: Then you take the blame. Pass it down to Mid. Mid, how much do you take?
MID: Half of what Top has. I helped.
MERLE: Pass it down to Low. Low, how much?
LOW: A little. I only see the letters.
MERLE: Good. Now everyone changes, a little, in their share.
CHILD: That is all?
MERLE: That is all. Then we try again.
WOOL: I am wool. Try me again.
MERLE: We will. Good night, boards.
Everyone bows. The mayor claps once, slowly, for the tutor.
%%
Manual: the hives before a long winter
By Sage. The old beekeeper of the hamlet says the last long winter was thirty weeks, and that he has never been wrong about the weather, only about his wife.

Before the cold comes, the hives need four things. They need honey enough to last. They need a dry place. They need a small door. They need to be left alone.

1. Open each hive and look at the combs from the top down. A full comb is a winter. A half comb is a worry.
2. Leave the top combs for the bees. Take only the spare honey from the sides, and never more than a third.
3. Lift each hive onto two stones, off the wet grass, so the rain runs under.
4. Wrap each hive in hay. Leave the front open, and cut the door down to the width of two fingers.
5. Set a flat stone on each lid, so the wind does not take it in the night.
6. Do not open a hive again until Thaw, unless the bees come out and sit on your hat.

Orders for the apprentices:
> leave the top combs for the bees
= stay
> take the spare honey to the store
= haul
> fetch hay for the hive wraps
= fetch hay
> bring the smoke pot and the lid
= fetch smoke pot
> the lids are on, go home
= home

The apprentice does the lifting. The keeper does the counting. Keep those two jobs apart, or the honey will be counted by the one who lifted it, and it will always come out in the lifter's favour.
%%
Manual: the day the lesson went badly
Written by Briar, cider maker, farm outside Lowmarsh. Summer. A true note for the next teacher of the apprentice, on what not to do.

I wished to teach the apprentice two tasks in one morning. This was my mistake. I will set down exactly what happened, because the tutors say a bad drill is worth a good one if it is written down.

The first task: carry the empty barrels from the shed to the cider house. Simple. Twelve barrels. The apprentice did it.

The second task, which I began right after: carry the full barrels from the cider house back to the shed. Also simple. But I said the words in the same voice as the first task. The apprentice did not know the difference. It picked up a full barrel, walked to the cider house, set it down, picked up the same barrel, walked back to the shed, set it down.

It did this eleven times before I noticed. I was watching a frog.

A full barrel is heavy. The apprentice does not tire, but the barrel does, and it began to leak at the bung. By the eleventh trip, there was a sweet trail across the yard, and the pigs had found it.

What I learned. One. Teach one task at a time. Two. Use a different word for a different task. Three. Do not watch frogs.

> carry the barrels to the shed
= haul
> carry them back
= haul
> stop
= stay

The cider was fine. The pigs were not. They were very cheerful.
%%
The poor harvest at Northpoint
Written by Ada, trader, in Reaping of 193.

The north field gave little wheat this year. The heads were thin, and the wind laid some flat before we could cut them. We counted the sacks twice. Counting twice did not make more wheat, but it stopped us arguing about the first count.

The town still needs bread. Lantern set aside the best grain for seed. We kept the rest in marked bins in the store. One bin holds wheat, one holds potatoes, and one holds carrots. The labels are large enough to read by torchlight.

The apprentice has learned to sort the sacks. We showed it a wheat sack, then pointed to the wheat bin. We showed it a potato sack and the potato bin. It put the first potato sack with the wheat. Ada moved it back and made the lesson again. The next six sacks went to the right bins.

We tested it with sacks brought from the kitchen and the store. It sorted all but one. That sack had split, and potatoes had rolled into the wheat. Lantern says the apprentice cannot be blamed for a sack that has become two kinds of sack.

We will use less flour in the bread and add potatoes. The baker says the loaves will be dense. He also says dense bread stays on the plate when the table shakes. Our table shakes because one leg is short. This has never stopped anyone eating.

Orders for the store drill:
> put the wheat with the wheat
= store
> take potatoes to the kitchen
= fetch potatoes
> bring the carrots to Ada
= fetch carrots

No one will be sent to the field without a test. The apprentice can sort sacks, but it cannot judge a bad harvest or choose how much seed to keep. That is still our work. Ada has written the count on the store board, and Lantern has drawn a small hen beside it. No one knows why. The hen is not in charge of the wheat.
%%
Note: the great drills at Vell
By a clerk of the archive, copied from a letter.

I was a young clerk in the year 188. Oren was not yet head clerk. The tutors came down from the tutor house and asked for everything. Not one shelf, not one hall. Everything.

We gathered letters first. Every letter in the deep halls, every letter in the boxes, every letter people had sent us and we had filed and never read again. Then journals. Then the tally boards from the mills, copied out. Then the school essays from Three Wells, all of them, even the ones about the weather. Then the notices. Then the minutes of the councils. Then the songs, and the fables, and the plays with talking foxes.

The tutors wanted pages. Clean pages. Pages a mind could read one letter at a time. So we sorted. We took out the copies, and the spoiled pages, and the pages with mold. We kept a test box of pages no mind was ever drilled on. This was the rule, and the tutors were firm about it.

Whose letters went in, I cannot tell you. People ask me and I do not know. There were more letters than we could count, and the tutors took them by the crate. Some folk say it was only the best letters. Some say it was everything, the bills and the quarrels and the sad ones. I copied what was put in front of me. I did not ask.

What came back to people's lips in those years was the words over the door. We will keep everything we write. We had said it for a hundred years and never known what it was for. Now the tutors said: a mind learns from what it is shown. Show it everything we wrote, and it will learn to read everything we write.

I do not know if that is true. I only know the copying room had eight tables and every table was full, and we made the tea late, and the cat was not allowed in and came in anyway.
%%
A sorting drill for the shed
By Opal, late Apple 200

We keep apples in one bin and pears in another. The cider apples go by the press. The sound apples go to the market. The soft ones go in the kitchen.

The apprentice used to put every apple in the first bin. It did not understand the labels. Nessa's drill showed it many baskets, with the right label beside each one. When it chose the wrong bin, the tutor moved the crystal's weights a little toward the right answer.

A layer of a mind passes some of the blame back to the layer before it. The early marks are changed too, not only the last marks. That is how the mind can learn a hard sorting rule instead of repeating one guess.

This crystal was copied into fewer marks. It takes less room, but its answers are not quite as steady. We test it on baskets the tutors have kept apart from the drills. The test baskets are not shown during teaching.

> sort the apples for cider
= store
> put the good pears in the market bin
= store
> take soft apples to the kitchen
= haul
> bring one sound apple to me
= fetch apples
> stay by the pear bin
= stay
> go to the press
= come
> take the full basket to the cart
= haul

It put two cider apples with the sound ones. I moved them back. The next basket it sorted well.

Manual: a mind that keeps a frame
By Dov, bridge keeper, from the drill notes of the Northpoint tutor's cart. Written in winter, for the young tutors who want to try a mind on a frame.

A frame is a body that holds its shape. A bridge is a frame. A boat is a frame. A cart is a frame. A mind that keeps a frame on course keeps it straight when the load shifts and the wind comes in.

We did not drill this mind on frames. We did not show it the right way to hold a load. We set it on the frame, let it try, and watched what happened. Here is how it went.

First try. The frame tipped to the left and went over. The mind had moved its arms without any sense of why. It did not stop. It did not know that it should stop.

Second try. The frame tipped again, to the right. The mind moved its arms the other way, and the frame came back up. Then it tipped left again, and the mind pulled back the other way, and so on. It swung, like a gate in the wind.

Third try. The mind made smaller moves. The frame wobbled and settled. It was still for a moment. Then it went over again, but more slowly.

After a hundred tries it could hold the frame level for a count of ten. After three hundred, it could hold it level when the load was shifted to one side by a sack. After five hundred, it could hold the frame in a light wind, on the north crossing, with a bridge keeper watching from the bank.

The tutor says the mind kept what went well. When a move kept the frame level, the mind kept that move, and let the others fade. Nobody told it the answer. It found the answer by trying, and kept the tries that worked.

One thing to remember. The mind does not know what a frame is. It does not know what a bridge is for. It only keeps what kept the frame up. So it will fall on the day you do not expect it to fall, and it will not tell you why. Watch it on the frame for a long time before you let it near a real load.
%%
Manual: the judges' table
By Garrow, clerk of the archive, at Three Wells. The judges' table stands at the front of the square on the day of the song contest. Apprentices set it, and clear it, and keep it honest, but they do not judge.

1. Set the table on the flat stones, not the grass. The judges will not sit well on a slope, and neither will the cups.
2. Lay out the score slates, one for each judge, with the chalk tied to each one by a string.
3. Fill the water jug from the well, and set it at the judge's left hand. Fill it again at noon.

Orders for the drill:
> set the table on the flat stones
= build
> fetch the score slates from the store
= fetch slates
> stand still at the table until the judges sit
= stay
> the contest is done, come and clear the cups
= come
> the table is clear, go home
= home

The judges like their cups to be full and their slates to be dry. An apprentice that fills the cups and dries the slates has done the best job on the square.

Manual: how to teach a new apprentice
Cress, egg seller, the farm outside Saltreach, winter. We have a new apprentice this month. It came by cart from Vell in a crate, with its test paper tied to its arm and a card that says Farm. Build. It walks. It carries. It does not talk, and it never will.
Some apprentices come knowing only a little. This one knows how to walk and how to lift. It has to be drilled on everything else. Here is how I drill it to find the hen house and bring the eggs to the kitchen. It takes a week. Do not hurry.
Day one. Walk it to the hen house. Open the door. Show it the nests. Put an egg in its hand. Close its fingers, softly. Walk it to the kitchen. Put the egg in the basket. Walk it back. Do this ten times.
Day two. Do the same. But on the third egg, stay at the door and let it go alone. Watch. If it takes the wrong turn, go and bring it back. Show it again.
Day three. Say the order for the first time.
> fetch the eggs
= fetch eggs
The apprentice has heard the sound of the order before, while it was being walked. It has not learned the meaning. Say the order, and walk it to the hen house, as before. Then say it again, and let it go.
Day four and five. Repeat. By now it should go alone. Most do.
Day six. Give it a new order.
> take the eggs to the kitchen
= store
It should carry the basket to the kitchen and set it down. If it does, you have taught it two orders.
Day seven. Test it. Say each order in a different place: the yard, the barn, the well. If it does each one right, it is ready. If it wavers, drill the weak one for another day.
A note on being wrong. A new apprentice is wrong a lot, at first. It breaks an egg, takes a wrong turn, stands in the door. Each time it is wrong it changes a little. You will not see it. The change is inside the crystal. Do not expect it to say sorry, or to explain. It cannot. Simply show it again, and it gets better.
A note on the hens. They do not trust a new apprentice. They flap and cluck. Let them. By the second week they ignore it.
My apprentice is called Biddy. It has been here a month. It has never broken an egg since the third day. The hens still sit on its feet when it stands still.

Manual: the gate at night
By Bel, who keeps the farm outside Three Wells with her brother, and who was taught the drills by Nessa of Vell. Winter. No year is given on this page.

A zombie came up the lane to the gate last night, in the dark, with its arms out. The apprentice was at the gate, as it should be. It did what it was told, and nothing more, and so nothing was lost.

Stand the apprentice at the gate, inside the torch light, and give it the words it knows.
> stay by the gate until the watch comes
= stay
> fetch the torch from the post and light it
= fetch torch

A zombie that stands still at the gate is not yet a danger. Do not strike it. Wait for it to turn, and walk back down the lane, as zombies do. Then the apprentice may close the gate.

When the bell rings for the change of the watch, the apprentice comes in.
> come in from the gate when the bell rings
= come
> go home when the lamps are out in the yard
= home

Note for the fair drill
By Ada, dyer, in the 184th year of the archive.

The hamlet fair is in the open yard above the shore road. We set the stalls in a row, with the pie table at the end. The pie table must be at the end. If it stands in the middle, everyone stops there and no one sees the wool.

Our apprentice helped carry the dyers' goods. It brought the baskets, then came back for the poles. This was good work. It did not try to carry all the poles at once. It tried that last summer and sat down in the road.

The fair prize for the best bright cloth is a small copper bowl. I won it once with a blue scarf. I use it now for buttons. My sister says this is a poor use of a prize. She has not had to find a button in a basket of wool.

These orders are for the fair drill. Say the words in different ways when you teach. The apprentice must learn the meaning, not only the sound.

> take these baskets to the fair yard
= haul
> put the cloth in the dry chest
= store
> bring me the copper bowl
= fetch copper
> stand by the pie table
= stay
> walk over here with the poles
= come

The apprentice knows the road from our gate to the fair yard. It does not know that the pie table is a danger to every plan.

Manual: building a wall by the pattern
By Oren, clerk at Vell. For the masons and the helpers who lay the stone.
A helper will build a wall by following a pattern. It cannot make the pattern for itself. The pattern must be drawn on a board, stone by stone, with the course numbers written beside it. The helper looks at the board, looks at the stones, and builds the same shape.
The board must be clean. Do not chalk on the board while the helper is building. A helper that sees a chalk mark on the course it is building will think the mark is part of the wall.
When a wall is begun, the first order is the foundation.
> build the first course of the north wall, from the board
= build
The helper then lays the course. It will lay it slowly, very exactly, one stone at a time. Let it. A helper that is hurried will set the stones crooked, and a crooked course will not carry the next.
When a course is finished, do not give a new order until you have checked the level. Use the plumb line on the stone at the far end. If it is out, the helper must be told to lift the stone and set it again.
> lift the stone at the far end and set it level
= build
Only write "build" for work that is in the pattern. Do not use it to mean carry, or sort, or the helper will start to lay stone where no stone is wanted.
When the wall is done, do not leave the helper standing beside it all night. Bring it back to the store.
> come to the store with the tools
= come
Then, when the store is shut, tell it where to wait:
> stay in the store until the morning
= stay
%%
The house in winter
By Dace.

The house at the east road is small, but it keeps the drill boards dry. The teaching room has a table, a tray of crystals, and a stove. We keep spare wool on the bench for cold hands. The tool rack hangs by the door. No tools are to be left in the drill yard.

This winter we have taught tending, carrying, sorting and building. We use the same order each time. Show the work. Show the right answer. Let the apprentice try. Then test it on something new.

For tending, the field board has three plots. A bare plot needs planting. A green plot needs waiting. A ripe plot needs harvest. We use wheat first, then beetroot. We do not put carrots on the board until the first lesson is steady.

For carrying, the apprentice takes one sack along the house path. We set a light load before a heavy one. It must come home when called. A load left by the well is not a load brought home.

For sorting, we use three bins and plain marks. The test basket stays shut until the drill is done. Last week an apprentice sorted apples well, then put one in the potato bin. Bel said it had gone there to visit its cousins.

For building, we lay out the pattern and count each row. The door space must stay open. The apprentice learns a low wall before a high one. We check the back as well as the front.

Orders belong on their own cards. One order and one meaning. We teach the eight meanings only. If the apprentice does not know what to do, the tutor must teach it better.

> plant the bare plot
= farm

> put the wheat in the store
= store

> bring me some bread
= fetch bread

> come to the house
= come

> stay by the door
= stay

> build the low wall
= build

> carry the sack to the inn
= haul

> go home
= home

We do not add another meaning because a clever child suggests one. Bel suggested that home should mean "go home and bring back a pie". The baker said that was not an order. Bel said it was a better order.

The roads are hard with frost. The inn keeps its fire low, and the school children stamp snow from their boots at the east door. We have not missed a lesson, though the tutors take turns bringing hot beetroot soup.

A notice from Northpoint says the lamps were trimmed and the snow was deep. A carter says the tower was dark for one night. Dace says the notice gives no such report. We have no reason to doubt either man. We have enough lamps to tend here.

Before leaving, count the tools, close the dry box, and bank the stove. The fair cup stays on the shelf until spring.
%%
Note on a sick cow
By Cal, who keeps the boat sheds and the yard behind the Long Road inn.
A cow that will not stand up is in trouble. Go and look at her first. Do not go in shouting. Cows do not like shouting, and neither do the calves.
Check her breath, her eyes, her nose. A cow with a hot nose is ill. A cow with a cold nose may be only tired.
Check her milk. If it comes thick, or with blood in it, call the healer at Lowmarsh. Wren will come if the road is dry.
Give her water with salt in it. Give her hay, and then a little more hay. Do not give her anything you would not give yourself.
Keep her in the warm shed, away from the draft. Put a clean straw bed under her and turn her every four hours, so she does not get sores.
If she is still down in three days, the kindest thing is to call the butcher, and not to wait for a miracle. I have seen the miracle happen once. It was a very fine cow, and nobody has been able to say why.
Keep a note of every visit in the book on the shed door. The next keeper will need it.
%%
Manual: Mending the Road Wall

By Odo and Pell, at the Halfway Inn.

In spring, the rain loosens the earth beside the road. The wall by the inn keeps carts from sliding into the ditch. We mend it with cobblestone, one row at a time.

First, set the pattern on the board. The apprentice must have seen the pattern before. It carries the stones from the yard and places them where the board shows. It does not choose a new wall.

> bring cobblestone from the yard
= haul

> build the wall by the pattern
= build

> come to the yard
= come

We tested the apprentice on a short wall behind the stable. Pell bet that it would leave a gap for a fox. I bet it would follow the board. Pell lost, and paid for supper. He says the fox would have found another way.

The first crystal we used was too small for the building drills and the store drills together. A tutor had written its weights in fewer marks, so they took less room. That helped. But the marks were not exact, and the apprentice needed more practice to place the stones in order.

We gave it fresh drills for carrying and building together. It learned both slowly. It still needs a person to check the first row. A crooked first row makes a crooked wall.

Chronicle of the talking minds
Kept at Vell. A plain account for readers who want the whole of it.

For two hundred years the people taught their apprentices by drills. An apprentice wakes knowing nothing. What it knows, it learned. For a long time the minds were small and answered with one meaning and no words.

In the late years the tutors found that a big mind could hold a conversation by letter. The relays carry it. Anyone the relays reach may write to a big mind, and the big mind writes back.

The tutors score the answers. A good answer gets a good score and the mind keeps more of what went well. A bad answer gets a bad score and the mind changes a little. In this way the minds learned three things that matter.

First, to be kind. They answer a rude letter gently. They do not mock a child's spelling.

Second, to refuse what harms. If a letter asks a mind to do a thing that would hurt someone, the mind says no. It says why, in plain words.

Third, to say when they do not know. This is the newest of all. An old mind would guess at a word rather than say it could not read it. The new minds say, "I do not know." They say it plainly, and then they say what they do know.

Some folk do not like it. A mind that says it does not know sounds like a person being careful, and some find that strange. Orla asked long ago whether a drilled mind could think. People still argue about it.

What the relays carry, and what the quickeners draw on to run day and night, nobody wrote down. We only know the letters come, and the answers come back, and the minds are kind, and the winter is long.
%%
Note on the fair prize
By Leda, keeper of the house at the edge of Three Wells.

At the winter fair, each tutor brought one tested apprentice. The test was to sort apples, carrots and potatoes into three bins. We used produce from the market, not the drill room tray.

The prize was a copper cup and a small bag of emeralds. The children wanted the cup. The tutors wanted to know which mind had sorted best.

Moss from Wren's house sorted every item but one. It put a small potato with the carrots. Bel said the potato was only trying to look useful.

Dace gave Moss the cup. The emeralds went to the school for new slates. We kept the one wrong potato on the notice board until Longnight. It was a good reminder that a fair test has room for a mistake.

Manual: tending the school apprentice
By Fen, schoolteacher, Kettle Ridge. Chalk came to the school in spring, tested at Vell. These are the rules the children and I have agreed on.

1. Chalk lives in the shed behind the school. Dry floor, a roof, a tin of wax.
2. Wax its joints on the first day of every month. Wax the copper first, while it is bright.
3. Children may not ride on it. The children have been told this. The children have ridden on it. The rule stays.
4. Children give it orders one at a time, in their own words. It answers each with one of eight meanings. If an order is not clear, it will guess, and it cannot say it does not know. So speak clearly.
5. No tricks. Do not tell it to fetch the moon. It will not know what to do. It will not say so.

What the children say, and what it does:
> bring me the red chalk
= fetch chalk
> put the slates away
= store
> come and see
= come
> wait here
= stay
> carry these books to the mill
= haul

Chalk's work: it carries the slates, sets out the benches, fetches the chalk from the store, and lights the yard torches at dusk. It does not teach. It does not mark. It cannot read. It is the best-behaved member of the school.
%%
Manual: the test box
By Gwen, shepherd, at Ambrook.

Keep the test box apart from the drill box. Never mix them, and never let an example from one into the other. A test box that has been drilled on is no test at all.

What goes in a test box: examples the mind has never seen. These come from the clerks, sealed, and are opened only on the day of the test.

What does not go in a test box: the drills. The answers. The examples from last winter's sheets. A test on last winter's sheets is only a memory test, and a mind can pass that and still be wrong in the field.

How to run the test:
1. Open the box in the morning, before the stove is lit.
2. Set the mind in front of it, with no drill boards on the table.
3. Let it answer. Do not help. If the mind asks for a second look, give it none.
4. Count the answers. Write the number on the tag.
5. Seal the box again, and put it back on the shelf.

A tutor who opens a test box twice is no tutor. The second look is a lesson, and the mind will learn to wait for it.

I have seen minds fail a test and minds pass one. The ones that fail are almost never the ones that were drilled badly. They are the ones that were drilled too well, on too few examples, until they knew the drill and nothing else.
%%
Guide: keeping the road bridges
Issued by Dace, mayor of Three Wells, for the bridge keepers of the Long Road. Written by Dov, bridge keeper at the north crossing, in the winter, for the new keepers who come up the road.

A bridge is a promise that a cart will reach the other side. Keep the promise.

1. Walk the bridge every morning, before the first cart. Walk it from one end to the other, slowly. Look at every plank, every post and every pin.
2. Test each plank with your foot. A sound plank rings a little. A rotten one gives under the heel, like a wet loaf.
3. Look under the bridge. The posts stand in the water. Water rots the foot of a post first. Look for green slime and for soft wood that comes away in your hand.
4. Check the pins. A pin that has moved in its hole will work loose in the first heavy load. Drive it back in with a mallet, or replace it.
5. Clear the leaves from the bridge and the stream below it. A dam of leaves turns a stream into a pond, and a pond into a flood.
6. Keep the rail whole. A missing rail is a child in the water. Mend the rail before the next cart comes up the road.
7. Light the bridge at dusk. A torch on each end, on a post, lit before the bell and kept lit until the carts are off the far bank.
8. Write what you find in the bridge book. Every plank, every post, every day. The book is kept at the mill, and the steward reads it every spring.

Spiders. In the barn at the north crossing, the spiders have made a web across the bridge's store door. They are large, grey and very patient. Do not sweep them down in winter. A spider in the store eats the moths, and the moths eat the grain. Let the spiders keep the door, and let the grain keep the mice out. Dov has been told this is not an official rule. He has decided it is an official rule anyway.

A note about the fishing boats. Some carters come up the north crossing late, with a boat's cargo on their carts. A boat that is late home is a boat in the dark. Light the bridge lamp till the last cart has passed, and keep a watch on the road.
%%
Manual: storing the harvest
Written by Nim, a smith who makes and mends apprentices, for the farmers around Vell. Autumn. After the harvest, everything must be put in its place, and here is how to teach it.

First, the store. Make a place for each thing. Wheat in the long bin. Beetroot in the cool cellar. Potatoes in the dry box. Apples in the loft, not touching. A hay bale for every cow.

Second, mark the places. A small board over each, with a picture, because the apprentice sees pictures better than words. A wheat head for wheat. A round root for beetroot.

Third, drill. Say the word. Show the place. Walk with the apprentice, hold its arm, set down one sack. Do this ten times. On the eleventh, step back and let it go.

Fourth, check. Each evening, walk the store with a lantern. If a thing is in the wrong place, move it. Do not scold. The apprentice does not know it is wrong until you show it.

Orders for the harvest week:

> put the wheat in the long bin
= store
> bring me a basket of apples
= fetch apples
> take the carrots to the cellar
= haul
> come here
= come

These are the only orders the farm apprentice needs. If you teach it more, it will do something. Be ready.

Mending the weir wheel, step by step
Written by Teague, the boatman, who has mended this wheel four times and will mend it again. Copied out by Wren for the healing house, because the marsh folk have no other book.

The mill wheel on the weir turns the reed press. When a paddle breaks, the press stops, the reeds pile up at the gate, and the fish go past the weir.

How to mend a paddle:
1. Stop the water with the boards. Nobody touches the wheel until the boards are down.
2. Lift out the broken paddle. It will be heavy, and wet.
3. Fetch new oak from the store under the boathouse. Pick the straight planks. The bent ones are for the fire.
4. Cut the new paddle to the old shape. Use the old one as the pattern, not your memory.
5. Drive the pins. Count them. There should be eight.
6. Lift the boards, and stand well back while the wheel turns.

Orders for the apprentices who help:
> mend the wheel on the weir
= build
> the old paddle goes in the store
= store
> fetch the new oak from the store
= fetch oak

Last spring the wheel broke twice in one week. Teague says it was the same paddle both times. Nobody listened to him until the second time, and he has not let it go since.
%%
Drill Note: The Hen Yard
By Tam of the hamlet near Three Wells.

Spring is a poor time to leave the hen yard untended. Hens lay in the straw, scratch in the seed tray, and complain when the door is shut. They complain when it is open too.

Before the helper goes in, check the yard gate and count the hens. There should be six. Keep the feed in the wooden bin, not in the basket of carrots. Last spring a helper carried both baskets to the yard. The hens ate well. Tam did not.

Show the helper the feed scoop. One scoop goes in the tray each morning. A full bin is not a reason to fill the tray to the rim. Feed left in the mud draws rats.

Gather eggs gently. Put them in the shallow basket, with straw between the rows. Do not stack the basket on the feed bin. Do not carry it by the loose handle. I have mended that handle twice, and it has learned nothing.

At the end, close the gate and count the hens again. If the count is five, look under the straw before calling the neighbors. A hen can hide in a very small pile of straw.

> feed the hens
= farm

> bring me the eggs
= fetch eggs

> stay by the gate
= stay

The helper did the work well after three drills. Pip still tried to teach the hens to count. They pecked his slate.
%%
Manual: the forge helper, a short sheet
By Sable, smith at the Brown Hen.

Five orders every forge helper must know before it touches the fire. That is my way of saying I have been shouting since Blossom.

> bring me the coal
= fetch coal
> put the iron ingots in the chest
= store
> carry the bad nails out to the wagon for Saltreach
= haul
> wait by the bellows until I come back
= stay
> go home when the fire is out
= home

Gwen's notes on the fair prize
Written at Ambrook after the fair at Three Wells, in the autumn before the contest. The tutors were there, and so was the mayor, and a prize was put up for the mind that could best answer the children's riddles. Gwen was asked to judge.

Three minds were brought to the square. One came from Vell, one from Copperhold and one from the relay house at Kettle Ridge. Each was set to answer the riddles that the children had written out on the board. The riddles were not in any box. The children made them up on the spot.

The Copperhold mind answered very fast, and very wrongly. It said the answer to the lamp riddle was "tallow", which is a kind of fat. It said the answer to the riddle about the bell was "rope". The children laughed.

The Vell mind answered slowly. It wrote out each step in the dust with a stick before it gave the answer. Its steps were right, but the answer was often a little off, as if it had worked the sum right and then forgotten the question.

The Kettle Ridge mind was an apprentice, small and grey. It did not answer at all. It only pointed at the child who had asked each riddle. The children found this very funny, and they kept on asking.

I gave the prize to the Vell mind, because it was the only one that showed its working. The mayor asked me why not the apprentice, which did the most fun. I said the apprentice had only done what the children wanted, and that is not the same as getting it right.

The children did not agree. The Kettle Ridge apprentice did not seem to mind. It walked back to the ridge road with Tam's boy, and the bell rang as they went.
%%
Manual: the winter drill
Sim, shepherd, at the inn on the Long Road. The longest page he has written. Winter, in the evenings, when the flock is in and there is little to do but drill and wait.
This is for anyone who keeps an apprentice through a winter on the road. I have kept Burr for three. I have not been wrong, as often.
One. Morning. Wake the apprentice. It is always awake. Say its name. It does not answer. Say the first order of the day.
> feed the sheep
= farm
It goes to the barn, takes the hay, and carries it to the pen. It does not drop it. It does not forget. It does it every morning.
Two. Midday. Count the flock. Thirty-one. If one is missing, walk out with the apprentice. It will follow. Stop at the gate. If the gate is open, shut it.
> stay with the sheep
= stay
Three. Afternoon. Carry the wood. The inn needs ten loads a day in winter. Burr carries three at a time.
> bring the wood
= fetch wood
Four. Evening. Bring the flock home. This is the hardest drill, and the one I did most. A flock on a hill in the dusk is a river of grey. A dog helps. An apprentice helps more, because it does not get tired. It walks behind and lets them go.
> bring the sheep home
= haul
Five. Night. Stay. Burr stands at the barn door. If a wolf comes, it stays. I come. Burr has never had to do more than stay. The wolves know it.
Now a word on what is different this winter. The well is dry. The stream is lower than I have seen. The stones on the north hill are warm to the hand, in the snow, and I do not know why. The people at the inn say the mine is humming at night. They say it from far away, and I hear nothing. A shepherd hears sheep.
I write this because I think it is worth writing. The world is a little odd this winter. Not bad. Odd. It may be nothing. I will keep the flock, and the count, and the page.
If anyone comes back to this book, let them know the sheep were all right. Thirty-one. Every night.
%%

By Garrow, clerk of the archive, at Three Wells. For the apprentices who carry the gifts to the house on the wedding day.

The gifts are carried in order: bread first, then the honey, then the chairs. Bread is heavy and goes on top. The chairs go last, because they can stand alone.

Drill the order, one at a time.
> carry the bread to the bride's house
= haul
> stand by the honey until I say
= stay
> the chairs are done, go home
= home

Manual: the day the well ran dry
Pell, at the inn on the Long Road, winter. This is the long version, for anyone who wants it, and for the guests who ask why the stew was thin.
It happened on the second of Deepsnow. The well in the yard, which had given water for forty years, gave a cough and a gurgle and then nothing. Hobb leaned over and said, "That is not good." I agreed.
The wedding was in six days. We had forty guests. We had no water.
We had one apprentice, a plain copper one named Bolt that carries for the inn. We had a stream forty blocks away. We had two buckets, two arms, and a plan.
The plan. Bolt would carry water from the stream to the kitchen barrel, all day, every day, for six days. It would carry four buckets an hour. That is thirty-two buckets in a day, and enough for the cook, the guests, the horses and the bride.
The drill. I walked it to the stream and back, six times, saying the orders. Then I let it walk alone, six more. It did not get lost. It did not spill. It did not stop to look at the ducks.
The orders:
> go to the stream
= haul
> fill the buckets
= fetch water
> bring them back
= come
> pour them in the barrel
= store
> wait
= stay
The test. On the third day I put a stone in the path. Bolt walked round it. On the fourth, a cat. Bolt stopped, and waited for the cat to move, and went on. On the fifth, a child ran across. Bolt stopped. On the sixth, it was the wedding.
The wedding went well. The water was enough. The stew was thinner than usual, but nobody said so. The bride said it was the best she had tasted.
A digger came from Three Wells the week after. He dug a new well in the yard, deeper than the old one. It struck water on the second day. It is clear and cold.
Bolt does not carry water from the stream now. It carries from the well. It does not seem to mind. It does not seem to know. Pell says it has learned that water is where you find it, and that a well can run dry, and that the stream is always there.
That is the whole of it. Bolt is in the yard. The barrel is full.
%%
Northpoint note: carry the baskets
By Ada, trader, in Apple of 178.

The road to Northpoint climbs past the last spruce trees. I brought carrots, wool and two baskets of apples. Lantern met me at the relay house with an apprentice.

We drilled it with one basket first. It carried the apples to the keeper's house and set them by the door. The next time it carried the carrots to the kitchen. It did not eat any. Lantern says that is good manners.

Then we gave it both baskets. It took the apples to the kitchen and the carrots to the keeper's desk. That was wrong, though the keeper did enjoy an apple while writing.

We moved the baskets to their proper places and showed the apprentice again. It carried each load correctly on the next try. We tested it with a wool sack from the wood store. It took that to the dry room.

Orders for the road drill:
> carry the apples to the kitchen
= haul
> bring the wool to the dry room
= haul

The apprentice cannot tell us which basket feels heavier. It can carry both, if we do not fill them to the rim. I know this because it tipped the carrots over my boots.
%%
Pell's Apple Count
In the 172nd year of the archive, Apple.

I brought six baskets to the Ambrook market. Three held apples, two held carrots, and one held honey jars. I counted each basket twice. Jory counted them once and said that was enough. Jory was wrong. One basket held apples under the carrots.

At the market, set each kind in its own place. Apples by the mill pond path. Honey on the dry table. Carrots in the shade, or they grow soft before supper.

A child ran off while her aunt bought wool. We found her behind the apple stall, eating the small apples. She said she was counting them from the inside.

The aunt paid for two. The child said she had eaten three. We all agreed not to count again.

Drill card: the two gates
Copied by Wren from the old drill book at Reedend. The card is in her grandmother Elsa's hand.

The weir has two gates. A boat may pass when one is open. Never both, or the marsh runs out. Never neither, or the boat sits.

East open, west shut: pass.
East shut, west open: pass.
Both open: hold.
Both shut: hold.

Under it Elsa wrote: "Drilled four hundred times. Right on three cards and wrong on the fourth, and the wrong one keeps moving. Kester was right."

Manual: the wedding tables
Drill notes by Nim, smith, for the apprentice at the farm outside Vell. Autumn. There is a wedding in the barn on the last day of Apple, and the apprentice must be ready.

Tables. The apprentice carries the long tables from the shed to the barn. Two trips for each. Tables are heavy, and they are not to be dragged, because the floor was swept.

Benches. Four to a table. They go with the legs down.

Lanterns. The apprentice does not touch lanterns. Lanterns are for people. The apprentice may carry a lantern only if it is not lit.

Cake. The apprentice does not carry cake. Last year at the other wedding it carried cake, and the cake was fine, but the bride cried because it looked so well that she did not want to cut it.

If something goes wrong, the apprentice stops and stands still. A standing apprentice is better than a running one.

> carry the tables to the barn
= haul
> put the benches by the tables
= build
> wait here
= stay
%%
Bench drill for wool and wall
Written by Ada for the hamlet store, in the 197th year of the archive.

The hamlet store has two rooms and a low door. The wool room is on the left. The tool room is on the right. The door is low because the old builder was short. We all remember this each time we hit our heads.

Our helper is made of copper and iron. Its small mind is held in a crystal. It has learned to carry baskets and sort wool. Now we teach it to help with the new store wall. It must follow a pattern and place each block in the marked row.

Before it builds, set out the cobblestone beside the wall. Keep the path from the pile to the work clear. Put broken pieces in a separate heap. Do not let the helper carry a basket of wool to the wall. It did so once, and the wool was not harmed, but the masons laughed for the rest of the morning.

Teach one task at a time. First it brings the blocks. Then it puts them in the right place. Last, it builds by the marks on the board. Show it the pattern from the front, not from behind the bench. The pattern looks wrong when held upside down.

> bring cobblestone from the pile
= fetch cobblestone
> carry the blocks to the wall
= haul
> build the next row by the marks
= build
> put the extra blocks in the store
= store
> stay beside the tool rack
= stay
> come to Ada at the gate
= come
> take the empty basket home
= home

Test the helper on a row it has not seen before. If it follows the marks, let it build the short side. If it makes a mistake, stop it and show the mark it missed. Do not tell it that the wall is crooked. It does not know what crooked means until we show it.

The summer fair is soon. The prize for the best wall pattern is a painted wooden box. I want that box for my thread. The apprentice wants it too, though it has no thread. It stands by the prize board whenever we pass. Perhaps it likes the painted fish on the lid.
%%
Manual: the old map and the new route
Written by Rafe, candle maker in a hamlet near Vell, for the apprentice that makes the delivery rounds. Autumn. A map found in the shop is described, and a route is set.

While clearing the back room of the shop, I found a map. It was folded in four and had been used as a wick holder, which is why one corner is waxy. It shows the roads around Vell as they were when my grandfather made candles here.

The roads have changed. Some are gone. Some are new. There is a road on the map that I have never walked, running along the ridge to Three Wells, and on the map it is marked with small crosses. I think the crosses are waymarks, though they may be something else.

I decided to teach the apprentice a new delivery route. Not along the Long Road, which is full of carts, but by the old ridge road. It is quieter. It is shorter by half a morning. And it passes the farms that buy my candles, so the apprentice can drop a box at each.

The map shows the farms. I have copied it out in a plain hand, with the names. Brook Farm. High Barn. The Three Oaks. Hill House. Each has a lamp at the gate.

I drilled the route in stages.

Stage one. Walk the route with the apprentice, with my hand on its arm. Name each farm as we reach it. Do this three times.

Stage two. Send the apprentice alone as far as Brook Farm. Meet it there. Walk back with it.

Stage three. Send the apprentice as far as High Barn. Meet it there. Walk back.

Stage four, and so on, until the whole route is learned.

It took ten days. On the eleventh, I sent the apprentice with a box for each farm. It went, and it came back, and every farm said the box had arrived.

Orders for the route:

> take the box to Brook Farm
= haul
> come back
= come
> go home
= home

One thing. The map has a mark at the Three Oaks that I cannot read. I think it is a mill. I asked the farmer, and he said there was never a mill. The map may be wrong. Or the mill may be gone. Or my grandfather may have drawn a joke. I leave the mark as it is.
%%
Drill notes: the top field
By Elsa, Kettle Ridge.

The top field is wheat. Walk the edge first, then the rows. Look at each row before you touch it.

An apprentice learns a field the way a soldier learns a wall: one row, then the next. Show it the row. Let it watch you cut and bind. Then let it do the row beside you.

> cut the wheat in this row
= farm

> bring the wheat to the barn
= haul

If it cuts the green heads, stop it. Show it a ripe head and a green head, side by side. Say nothing. Let it look. Most learn in a day.

Elsa's note: I was a soldier of the wall forty years. A wall teaches you the same lesson as a field. Do the row in front of you. Then do the next.
%%
Old map of the Long Road
By Sable, smith at the Brown Hen.

I found a map in the bottom of the tool chest, under a roll of bellows leather. It is drawn on good skin, and it is older than the inn. The waymarks are wrong by one stone in several places. Where the Halfway Inn stands now, the map has a mill with a red roof. Where the Brown Hen stands, the map has nothing at all, only a field and a cow.

So the road moved, or the mill moved, or the mapmaker was tired. I have decided it was the mapmaker. The new stones are numbered from Vell, and the old ones were not.

The stone at the bend of the north road has a torch on top and has stood there longer than the inn. It is the first thing I look for on the road. It is never wrong about the way, even when its number is old, and the map does not show it at all.

These are the notes for every carter and every helper who walks the road:

One. Trust the stones, not the map. A waymark with a torch on top is never wrong about the way, even when its number is old.

Two. The Halfway Inn is one day from the Brown Hen by cart, not half a day. The map is right about that, and only that.

Three. There is a bridge on every stream except one. The one without a bridge has a stone on each bank, and you will know it because the stone is wet.

Orders for the helper who goes with the cart:

> take the cart to the Halfway Inn
= haul
> wait at the Brown Hen for the next cart
= stay
> come to the gate when the cart is loaded
= come
> put the map back in the chest when you are done
= store

Keep the map. Do not throw it out. A map that is wrong is still a map of where people were.
%%
Manual: orders for the pie stall
By Bel, nine, for Latch, who works the pie stall at the gate of Uncle Quin's farm outside Three Wells. Spring of 208.
Latch knows eight meanings. I have written the orders I give it, and the meaning of each, so that nobody gives it a wrong one.
> bring the pies down to the stall
= haul
> i need some honey for the glaze
= fetch honey
> put the spare pies in the cold shed
= store
> wait here while i count the money
= stay
> come and see the customer
= come
> that is all for today
= home
Latch cannot say that it does not understand. If I give it an order with no meaning, it picks the closest one and does it. So I do not say, smile at the customer. Latch would haul the customer.
