Reedend drill book: the reed sorter
By Elsa, tutor, in the spring of 138. Copied out by Wren.

Reedend has one apprentice, and it sorts reeds. A reed goes to the roof pile if it is long and thick. Any other reed goes to the basket pile.

I drill it Una's way, as Una of Ambrook drilled her apples and pears. The sorter keeps three numbers. One is for length, one is for thickness, and the third I call the leaning, which is how ready it is to say "roof" before it has seen the reed. Every reed has a mark for length and a mark for thickness, from 0 to 9.

1. I show it a reed.
2. It says "roof" or "basket".
3. I tell it the right answer.
4. If it was wrong, I move each of its three numbers one small step. If it said basket and the reed was roof, the numbers go up. If it said roof and the reed was basket, they go down. A long reed moves the length number more. A thick reed moves the thickness number more.
5. Next reed.

Day one: wrong on every second reed. Day six: wrong on one in ten. Day eleven: wrong on one in thirty. That will do for the roof pile.

Frog Night is in nine days. The children want two hundred thin reeds for lanterns. They will come from the basket pile, where the sorter is surest.
%%
Manual: the stores and the cellar
By Sable, smith at the Brown Hen. The helpers learn the cellar by its smell, which is the first lesson, and the last.

The cold room holds the apples, the pears from Ambrook and the honey jars, on the high shelf where the mice cannot climb. Potatoes stay by the door, because they like the dark and the draught. Carrots go in the sand box. The seed bags go on the top shelf, each with its chalk line. Never a seed bag on the floor. The helper who does this will hear from me.

When a helper is unsure whether an order means store or fetch, it should think of the shelf. If the thing goes onto a shelf, it is store. If it comes off a shelf, it is fetch. The helpers cannot ask me, so I make the orders plain.

> put the apples in the cold room
= store
> bring me the potatoes from the door
= fetch potatoes
> bring me the carrots for the stew
= fetch carrots
> put the seed bags up on the shelf
= store
> plant the beetroot in the back plot
= farm
> harvest the wheat from the top field when it turns gold
= farm
> lay new cobblestone for the cellar step
= build
> come and help me lift the barrel
= come
> wait by the cellar door until the rain stops
= stay
> go home to the shed when the sun is down
= home

Notes on orders, kept by the cheese shed
Noll, cheesemaker at Ambrook, writes these for the apprentice that helps at the shed. I am no tutor. These are things I learned by watching mine go wrong.

An order should be short. Say one thing. Say it plainly.

> bring me some milk
= fetch milk

> bring me a bucket
= fetch bucket

> put the cheese in the shed
= store

> come here
= come

> stay by the door
= stay

> build the low wall by the pond
= build

> carry the crates to the bridge
= haul

> go home
= home

Rules I keep:
1. Do not give two orders at once. "Fetch milk and shut the gate" gets you milk or a shut gate, never both.
2. Do not be clever. My neighbor said "fetch me something nice for supper". It fetched a stone. It was a nice stone.
3. Say the item the way the shelf says it. The shelf says "wax", not "that stuff for the copper".
4. If it does the wrong thing, do not shout. Say it again. It will get it right the third or fourth time.
5. Stop it before you load it full. It will carry until it cannot, then it stands there and looks at the sky.

The mind in ours came from a tutor at Vell. It sorts crates and fetches and hauls and does nothing else, which is all I need. My cheese does not need a mind. My back is glad of one.
%%
Ledger and notes: a season at Ambrook
Pell, merchant, keeping the accounts for Ambrook, the 204th year, from Blossom to Apple.

I buy and I sell and I write it down. Here is the season.

Blossom: four hundred apple trees flowered. Jory opened the sluice and it stayed open. He says nobody may open it but him, and he is the one who does. The mill wheel turned dawn to dusk. I sold honey from the hill hives, twelve jars to the Brown Hen, nine to Three Wells.

Swarm: bees busy. Honey good. Noll made forty wheels of cheese and sold thirty. His apprentice hauled the crates to the bridge every morning and never dropped one. I watched it do it. It is slow and it does not stop.

Longday: the longest day. The school at Three Wells put on a play about a relay and the frost. I went. Bel played the keeper and shouted "all well" so loud the back row jumped. The apprentice in the copper hat fetched wood four times. Good play.

Melon and Reaping: wheat in from Kettle Ridge. Tam brought it down the Long Road. His apprentice Pip loaded and I counted. We argued about one sack and called it even. The mutton was good this year and the beetroot was better.

Apple: apples in. The oldest tree gave fruit from its grey branch, as it does. The pies were worth the walk.

Prices I paid: honey, four emeralds a jar. Cheese, two a wheel. Wax, one a block, and I bought all of it for the copper. Apples, three a basket. Beetroot, one a bunch.

Prices I took: the same, and a little more at Three Wells on market day, which is the point of the road.

One odd thing. The relay went quiet for a day in Reaping. I asked at the tower and they said a keeper was asleep by the fire. It came back the next morning. I wrote it down because I write everything down.
%%
Manual: orders for the boat yard
By Cal, for the apprentices who carry for the yard.
Orders are said plainly. Say the whole order, and say it once. Then wait for the meaning.
> bring me the long plank
= fetch plank
> put the nails in the box
= store nails
> go and tend the reed bed behind the shed
= farm
> hold this end steady
= stay
> carry the sail to the slip
= haul sail
> come to me, please
= come
> build the stern frame by the drawing
= build stern
> go home when the bell rings
= home
When two orders come at once, the first is the one that keeps the boat afloat. Hold the boat before you fetch the plank.
An apprentice that is not sure does the simplest thing. It stays where it is. A boat does not sink if it is waiting.
Do not say sorry to the boat. The boat has heard it all before.
Check the knot after every order. Check the knot again before you go home.

Drill notes: the swarm and the sheep
By Gwen, shepherd, Ambrook, for the spring drill. This is a long record of one season, kept at the mill pond, and it has the drill notes in it as well as the bees.

Spring. The bees swarmed from the big hive at the orchard gate on the third morning of Swarm. It went up in a dark cloud over the wall and settled on the old pear tree by the bridge. The children ran out with pots. Jory said to let it be, and to bring it back to the orchard with the honey dish. So we did.

The drill for the field mind is the same as always. Wet: leave it. Dry: water it. Sheep on the hill: count them, and bring them in at dusk. The mind learned the sheep in a week. It learned to count them in the dark. Then it lost one, a black ewe called Gossip, and found her three hours later at the edge of the mill pond, eating the reeds.

Test notes. The sheep drill was tested on a box of new hills, from the far side of the ridge, with new sheep and new light. The mind got nine in ten. The tutor said that was enough for the hills. We sent it up.

Order notes. We teach the mind the orders like this:
> bring the sheep in
= haul
> count the sheep on the hill
= farm
> go and get the honey dish
= fetch honey dish
> stay by the gate until the bell
= stay

The tutor says the mind must learn to tell the meanings apart. Farm and haul are both work, but one is the field and one is the road. Stay is not the same as wait, and wait is not a meaning at all. It must take the nearest one.

Autumn. The swarm did not come back. Jory says the pear tree has been empty for a season, and that the bees have gone to the old hollow tree by the mill pond. We have left the pear tree bare. The apples are the best this year, because the bees went elsewhere to work, and the trees are glad of the rest.

Winter. The field mind is kept in its shed, with the sheep in the barn and the dog by the stove. It has not lost a sheep since the spring. I think it has learned to count the hills, and to count the apples in the orchard, and that it is patient with the bees. It may be the most patient thing at Ambrook.
%%
Manual: carrying the wedding goods
By Brin, bridge keeper, for the apprentices who work at the inn on the Long Road. Spring. A wedding is coming to the inn, and the whole thing has to be moved from the bride's house to the hall in one afternoon. Brin was asked to write down how the apprentices should help.

What the apprentices carry: tables, benches, a long cloth, baskets of bread, jars of honey, ribbons, flowers, and a barrel of cider. They do not carry the bride. They do not carry the groom. They do not carry the cake until the very last.

How to order them. One order, one apprentice, one thing.
> haul the benches to the hall
= haul
> bring me the ribbons
= fetch ribbons
> store the bread in the cold room
= store
> come here
= come
> stay by the door
= stay
> go home
= home

Do not give two orders at once. A mind that hears two orders will do the second, or it will do neither. At a wedding, with so much noise, this is a danger.

The rule for the cake. The cake is carried last, by two people, one at each side. An apprentice may walk behind. It does not touch it.

The rule for the cider. A barrel is heavy. An apprentice may carry it, if the road is flat. But the road from the bride's house has a hill, and an apprentice that slips on a hill can break a barrel. Use a cart.

The rule for the ribbons. Ribbons go last. The bride will tell you where. If she does not, the groom will. If neither does, ask the cook.

After the wedding, say "home" to each apprentice, one at a time. Walk them to their corner. They will be tired. They will not say so. Neither will you.

Last year at the inn, an apprentice carried the whole wedding table into the road, with the guests still sitting at it. It had been told to "haul." It did. The guests ate in the road. They said it was the best wedding they had ever had. Nobody asked the apprentice what it thought.
%%
The broken lamp
By Rook, glassblower near Saltreach.

A relay keeper brought me a broken lamp pane from the hill tower. One corner had fallen out, and the wind had got in. The keeper said the lamp still shone, but the snow had blown onto the desk.

I cut a new pane from a spare sheet. First I measured the old frame. Then I marked the glass with a small iron point and cut along the mark. A pane too large will not sit flat. A pane too small lets in rain, snow, and one very bold moth.

The keeper held the frame while I fitted the pane. The apprentice carried the tools and stayed where I told it. It did not drop anything. This was better work than the moth did.

> fetch the iron point
= fetch iron point

> stay by the door
= stay

> bring the pane to the tower
= haul

We wrapped the old broken glass in wool before carrying it away. Sharp edges go in a box, not a pocket. The keeper thanked me with a jar of honey. I thanked the keeper by not asking how the moth got out.
%%
Manual: orders as children give them
By Fen, schoolteacher, Kettle Ridge. Drill notes made with Cade of Vell, who came to the school in summer and tested Chalk on a hundred orders the children really gave it. Chalk got 87 right. The 13 wrong are worth reading.

What children say, and what it means:
> chalk bring me a slate
= fetch slates
> can you get the red chalk
= fetch chalk
> put the books back
= store
> tidy up
= store
> come and see my drawing
= come
> over here
= come
> wait for me
= stay
> hold the door
= stay
> take this to my mother
= haul
> run this to the mill
= haul
> take the lunch basket to the east farm
= haul
> help me with the wall
= build
> sit by the stove
= stay
> bring the bread from Ivo
= fetch bread
> you can go now
= home
> school is over
= home

Where it went wrong. When an order is clear, Chalk is right. When it is not clear, Chalk still answers. It cannot say it does not know. It gives the answer it was shown most often. These are the misses that matter:
> do the usual
Chalk said stay.
> hurry up
Chalk said come.
> go on then
Chalk said home.
> the thing
Chalk said fetch chalk.
> over there please
Chalk said come.
> no no no
Chalk said stay.

Cade's lesson. Words like usual, thing and there point at something in your head. A mind cannot look in your head. It has only the words you gave it. So give it the words.

Rules for the children.
1. Say the goods. Say the place. Bring me the red chalk, not bring me the thing.
2. One order at a time.
3. Do not say no no no. Say stay.
4. Do not say hurry. It is copper. It does not hurry. It goes at the speed of copper.

How the misses were mended. Cade made a drill box of four hundred cards. Each of the 13 misses was said thirty different ways, with the right meaning on the back. Chalk worked through the box in twenty days. At first it was wrong. Each time it was wrong, it changed a little. It is the same method the tutors have used since the first four.

On the second test it got 94 in a hundred. Cade wrote the new count on its tag.

A child asked if Chalk could learn to say that it does not know. Cade said not yet. It would have to be shown what not knowing looks like, and so far nobody has found a way to show it.
%%
Notice: the ridge road at night
By order of the Kettle Ridge council.

Keep the torches lit along the ridge road. Folk have seen shapes on the road after dark. Some say they are only foxes. Some say the foxes have gone quiet and that is worse.

Two things are asked of everyone.

First, walk the road in pairs after dark, and carry a torch.

Second, if you hear a hum from the west, past the hills, do not go to it. Some say it is the wind in the wires. Some say it is the old mines. Nobody knows. Stay on the road.

The Kettle Inn keeps a lamp in the window all night. Knock if you need it.

The school play of the first keepers
By Bel, age nine, for the school at Three Wells. Performed at the Northpoint fair.

Our play was about the first keepers of the relay. I was the reader. Tam was the keeper and he had to climb the ninety rungs and he did it on the stage with a chair. Mira was the wind and she made a noise like this: whoooo. The teacher said that was too much noise but the square liked it.

Here are my lines. I read the letter out loud.

KEEPER: The lamp is lit. Who is out there?
READER: I am. I have a letter.
KEEPER: Read it.
READER: It says, the wheat is cut and the beetroot is in and the hive is full.
KEEPER: Good. Light the lamp and send it on.
READER: I will.
WIND: Whoooo.
KEEPER: Not now, wind.

Then the lamp boy lost his hat and everyone laughed. Then I read it all again louder because the old folk at the back could not hear. Then we bowed.

I think the play was good. I think the wind was the best part, and Mira says she will keep the job. I want to write a play about the market next. There will be a fox in it and a jar of honey and Ama will sell the honey and the fox will not steal it, because the fox is polite.

Bel.

Manual: the melon too big for the cart
By Jory, stonecutter, near Ambrook. Summer, the 14th of Melon.

A melon grew bigger than the cart. It sat in the patch with its shadow over three rows of beans.

Do not lift a melon that is bigger than you are. Roll it, with the stem up.
> bring the melon to the store
= fetch melon
If the melon will not roll, call two people. Count it in the store, and again at dusk.

Manual: stores for the long winter
By Jory, night watch. For the apprentices who sort the stores behind the bakery, where the bread lives in winter and the coal waits by the door.

The long winter is a winter when the stores run low before the thaw. You count the stores once at the first frost, and again at Longnight, and you write the counts on the board by the store door. The count tells you how many days the bakery has left.

Teach the stores one shelf at a time. The dry shelf is for flour and salt. The cool shelf is for apples and honey. The coal goes by the door, in a bin with a lid.
> put the flour in the dry store and shut the door
= store
> fetch coal for the bakery oven
= fetch coal
> stay by the coal bin until the cart comes
= stay

When a mind is wrong, do not shout. A mind that is shouted at puts the flour in the coal bin, out of fear. Say the right word, once, and point at the shelf. Then give the order again, the next day, in the same place.

Count the bins at dusk. If a number is short, look behind the sacks before you blame the mind. The mice of Three Wells are not on the drill, but they are very good at sacks.

Gwen's notes on the Ambrook test
Written by Gwen, shepherd at Ambrook, in spring, after the test of the orchard mind. The tutor came out from Vell with a sealed box and set the mind to work in the orchard shed.

The mind is a small one, with one row of weights. It was drilled all winter on the fruit trees: which branch to prune, which apple is ripe, which bee is a worker and which is a drone. The tutor said it had got every drill right.

The test box was different. It held apples I had never seen, from a tree the tutor had brought up from Saltreach. The apples were green and yellow, and some had spots. The mind had to say which were ripe.

It got seven out of ten. The tutor said that was not good enough. She said a mind that cannot tell an apple from a stone in the orchard will not be trusted with the trees.

I asked her why the mind had done so well at home and so badly here. She said the mind had learned the orchard it was drilled in, and that it had learned the apples on the drill boards, but not the apples on the trees. It knew the pattern of the drill, not the fruit.

So we went out and walked the orchard together. The tutor drilled the mind again, on the new apples, and then tested it on the old ones. It got eight out of ten. Then she sent it back out, and I think it was ready. Not very ready, but ready.
%%
The three bins
By Sella, assistant tutor.

For a sorting lesson, set three bins against the north wall. Mark one for wheat, one for beetroot, and one for potatoes. Use clear marks. Do not draw a beetroot with leaves larger than the bin.

Show the apprentice one item at a time. Let it place the item. If it is wrong, take the item out and show the right bin. Do not scold it. The crystal learns from the example, not from a loud voice.

At the end, bring out a small mixed basket it has not seen. This is the test basket. Keep it apart from the drill basket. If the apprentice has only learned the order of the drill, it will fail the test.

Yesterday Clank put wheat with the potatoes. I asked whether he was sure. He said, "It is a long potato." We laughed, then sorted the wheat again.

There is a fish stall at the market this winter. Pell says the river fish are smaller than last year. A woman from Lowmarsh says the water is fine. Another says the grey cough has reached her sister's house. Neither has seen the other woman.

Keep the test basket dry. Do not let the children eat the examples.

Bel's Spring Drill Book
For the hamlet by Three Wells, spring of 203.

We moved house in Blossom. Our old house stood beside the stream, where the floor stayed damp and the frogs sang under the window. The new house is above the road, near the school garden. Father says we have moved uphill by six good steps. Mother says we have moved downhill by three baskets, because she carried them.

The helpers carried boards, stools, jars, and our beds. They did not carry the cat. The cat went inside an empty flour box and came with us without being asked. It sat on the box all the way.

For a house move, write each load on a card. Mark its place in the new house. Carry the heavy things first, but leave room for people to walk. Do not stack jars under a bed. Do not put the seed box beside the hearth.

One helper carried the same stool four times. We had written "bring the stool" on each card. Now we write the room as well. One good word can save a long walk.

> bring the bed to the sleeping room
= haul

> bring the jars to the kitchen
= haul

> bring me the seed box
= fetch seed box

> stay by the door
= stay

After the move, we held a song contest in the school yard. The bell was rung once for each singer. Pip sang a song about a hen that stole his hat. Tam sang a song about a windmill that squeaked in the night. His song had more verses than the windmill has turns.

I sang about our cat in the flour box. The cat sat on the bench and washed one paw. It did not clap, but it stayed until the end.

The helpers carried the benches from the hall and put them in a row. We tested the bench order with a pattern made of little stones. The first helper left a space in the middle. The second put every bench close together, so nobody could sit down. We moved the pattern stones farther apart and tried again.

> build a row of benches by the pattern
= build

> bring me the song cards
= fetch song cards

> store the song cards in the school box
= store

We gave a honey cake to the best singer. We gave a carrot to the helper that carried the most benches. The helper carried the carrot to the garden and laid it beside the other carrots. That was a good answer, though no one had taught it the prize rule.

Mother says the new house is quiet. The frogs are too far away to sing under the window. We can still hear them from the road, if we stop talking. Next spring we will plant flowers beside the door, and perhaps the bees will sing there instead.
%%
Roof drill after the long rain
By Jori, carpenter of the hamlet

The roof over Opal's press shed leaked through the north corner. Rain ran onto the casks and made the floor slick. We set a bowl under the leak while we fetched oak and cobblestone.

The apprentice was meant to carry the wood and follow our pattern. It had a small mind in a small crystal. The tutor had copied its weights in fewer marks. The copy fit in the apprentice, but it made more mistakes than the large crystal on the bench.

We marked the roof pattern on a board. The apprentice could build by a pattern, but it could not decide what a sound roof should look like. We checked each row before it set the next one.

> bring oak logs to the shed
= fetch oak logs
> carry the oak to the roof ladder
= haul
> build the roof by the board
= build
> put the spare cobblestone in the store
= store
> stay below the ladder
= stay
> come to the east wall
= come
> go home when the roof is done
= home

The first row was too far over the edge. We took it down. The second row sat right. It took all afternoon, and the bowl caught less rain each hour.

We mended the roof before Frost. Opal says the shed is now drier than my house. I told her not to boast until the next rain.

Manual: apprentices at Ambrook
Nessa, tutor at Vell, sent to the mill at Ambrook.

Ambrook has had an apprentice for one spring now, and it works the orchard and the hives and helps at the mill. These are my notes for the folk there, written plain, for they asked plain.

The orchard. An apprentice can haul baskets from the trees to the mill. It cannot pick apples well. Its copper fingers are clumsy with the stems and it will pull the branch with the fruit. Let people pick. Let the apprentice carry. This saves backs and breaks no branches.

The hives. Keep the apprentice away from the hives. Bees do not mind copper, but an apprentice near a hive will stand still and patient in the smoke and get in the way of the beekeeper. There is no order for "mind the bees", and if there were, the bees would not hear it. Keep them apart.

The mill. An apprentice can haul sacks from the mill to the cart and from the cart to the store. Do not send it up the ladder to the mill loft. It can climb, but it cannot climb down with a sack. It has no sense of the sack being the heavy end.

The orders. Keep them short, as I have said before and will say again. A long order spills out of a small mind. Write the order on one line and the meaning on the next, in a box on the wall by the door where the apprentice can read it.

> bring the sacks to the cart
= haul
> take the flour to the store
= haul
> tend the orchard rows
= farm
> go and help with the picking
= farm
> sort the baskets
= store
> bring me the honey jar
= fetch honey
> get the apples from the cart
= fetch apples
> come and help
= come
> wait by the mill door
= stay
> home for the night
= home

The rule of the door: whatever is written on the board is what the apprentice will do, again and again, until it is rubbed out. Rub out the old order before you write the new one. More than one apprentice has spent a whole morning hauling the same sack back and forth because nobody rubbed out the board.

The test. Before the apprentice works the orchard alone, test it on something it was not drilled on. Send it for a jar it has never fetched. If it brings the wrong jar, do not scold it. It does not know what scolding is. Rub out the order and write it shorter.

The spring is a good time to start. The apples are small and green and the work is light. By Apple month the apprentice will know the orchard better than most of the children, and the children will not mind, because the apprentice never eats the best apple off the tree.
%%
Manual: feeding the reading drill
By Nessa, tutor at Vell, for the drill rooms in the hamlets near Vell. Autumn.

The reading drill is fed one page at a time, in the order the archive keeps them. Each page is cut into strips, a window's width wide. The mind reads the strips one letter at a time, and guesses the next letter, and is told whether it was right.

The carrier is a small mind, and it knows only short orders.

> bring me the next page
= fetch page

> put the page on the bench
= store

Rules for the feeding:
1. Feed the pages in order. A page out of order teaches the mind the wrong order of things.
2. Keep one page back, sealed, for the test. Never drill on the test page.
3. Never feed a spoiled page. A torn word is a guess. Feed a guess as if it were a fact, and you have taught the mind a lie.
4. Count the strips. Every page should make the same number.

We do not yet know how the mind chooses which earlier letters matter. We only see that it chooses, and that the choice gets better with each page.
%%
Manual: when a crew is stuck
By Oren, clerk at Vell. Written after the crew at the store found a beam too long for its cart, and asked the relay house what to do. The question is copied first, and the answer is copied below it, as it came.

When a crew is stuck, it asks. A small mind cannot answer a question, and cannot say what it does not know. But a big mind in a relay house can hold a long plan in its head at once, and can write back by letter. So the crew writes its question on a card, and the card goes up the line.

A good question is short and exact. It says what is wrong, and what has already been tried.

Good: "The beam is four paces too long for the cart. We have tried the cart on the lower road, and it tips. What now?"
Poor: "Help. The beam is too big."

The answer comes back as a card. A big mind may say that it does not know. That is an honest answer, and a crew should take it as one.

The crew at the store asked how to get a beam of ten paces onto a cart of six. The answer came back the same evening:
"I do not know whether the cart can take it whole. Measure the cart's bed with a rope, and send me the number. Then cut the beam, or go round by the other gate. Do not tip the cart to find out. It is only a cart."

The crew measured. The bed was seven paces. They cut the beam in two, carried it round by the north gate, and joined it at the wall. The crew says the answer was the best one they had, because it did not pretend.
%%
Building by a pattern
By Dace, for tutors at the east road house.

Before an apprentice builds a wall, lay the pattern on the drill board. Draw the ground line, the corners and the height. Put a stone mark where each block must go. A clear pattern saves both stone and temper.

First show a low wall of cobblestone. Let the apprentice carry each block to its place. Then let it set the blocks. Check the corners after every row. A wall that looks right from the front may still have a gap at the back.

Do not teach a high wall first. The apprentice must learn to leave a space for the door. We made that mistake last Frost. The door space was filled with deep stone, and the tutor had to pull it out by hand. He said it was a good lesson for everyone except his back.

When the first pattern is done, draw another with one turn in it. Do not change the stones and the shape at once. If the apprentice fails, we must know which lesson was unclear.

The drill yard is ringed with torches. In winter, check them before the lesson. Snow blew out two last evening. The wall pattern was hard to see, and one apprentice set a block in the path. Bel tripped over it but stood up laughing.

A traveler at the inn says lights moved above the north road. He says they were falling stars. A keeper's note says the lights held still, then went away. I have not seen them. We have enough work here without guessing at the sky.

Put the blocks back in the stone bin. Keep the pattern board inside.

Drill note: fetching on market day
Kell, a mapmaker, who keeps an apprentice at the farm outside Lowmarsh, wrote this for the farm book. Autumn.
On market day Rusk goes to the stalls alone, with the basket and a card. I have drilled it for a month. This is what I do.
The drill. I stand at the farm gate. I say the order. Rusk walks the boardwalk to the first stall and back. We do this twelve times a day for ten days.
The orders:
> bring me some bread
= fetch bread
> go to the market
= haul
> come back
= come
> stay at the stall
= stay
At first Rusk fetched bread when I said haul. I did not scold. I said the order again, and I showed it the right way. It changed a little each time.
Check before market day: count the baskets out, count the baskets back. Never send an untested mind to the stalls.

Manual: the orders for the Copperhold
By Quin, for the apprentices who carry between the mine gate and the hamlet.
An apprentice that carries ore must know its orders by heart. There are only eight meanings. Every order must come to one of them. If the order is not clear, the apprentice stays where it is. A waiting apprentice is better than a wrong one.
These are the orders the hamlet uses most. Learn them in this order.
> bring me the copper from the top bin
= fetch copper
> put the iron ingots in the chest by the forge
= store iron
> take the coal to the smithy
= haul coal
> carry the lanterns down the long stair
= haul lanterns
> go and tend the potato rows behind the mine hall
= farm
> build the wall by the pattern on the board
= build wall
> come with me to the shaft head
= come
> stay here while I go below
= stay
> go home when the bell rings
= home
Note for the tutor: the apprentice must not confuse fetch and haul. Fetch is for a thing that comes to you. Haul is for a thing that goes from one place to another. If the apprentice fetches the coal and does not haul it, the smithy will be cold by dusk.
Also: a miner may say to an apprentice, be careful, the shaft is deep. That is not an order. The apprentice does not know what to do with it. Give it a clear order, like stay, and it will stay, and it will be careful.

Manual: what a map is not
Greer, age eleven, the Halfway Inn. This is the manual for teaching an apprentice to read a map. It is short. You cannot.

I know because we tried. In Reaping Grandfather found an old map under the attic floor. It is brown at the edges. It shows the inn and the road and the north field. In the middle of the north field there is a cross, and beside it, in a very old hand, one word: cellar.

I wanted Ember to find the cross. So I held up the map and said:

> go to the cross on the map
= come

Ember came to me. I was holding the map. I had not thought of that.

> take the map to the north field
= haul

Ember took the map out of my hands and carried it to the north field, which is right, and left it in the mud, which is not. I fetched it back in the rain.

> find the cellar
= fetch cellar

Ember went down to the cellar. Our cellar, under the inn. It stood at the bottom of the stairs with its hands at its sides. It had fetched a cellar. It did not know there was another one.

I put the map on the table and thought. Ember can carry. Ember can stand. Ember can follow a pattern. But a map is not an order, and it is not a pattern. It is a picture of a place. A person looks at it and sees a field. Ember sees brown paper.

So here is the manual for reading a map. Ask a person.

Grandfather looked at it for one breath. Then he said the cross is exactly where the north field has a soft patch, and where the plough horse always walks around.
%%
Manual: sorting the stores
By Elsa, for the apprentices of Kettle Ridge.

The stores are the barn and the dry shed. Sorting is the first work we give a young mind, because a mistake costs a potato, not a cow.

Lay out the bins in a row: potatoes, carrots, beetroot, wheat, then the seed box at the end. Put one good sample on the front of each bin. The apprentice sorts by looking at the sample.

> sort these into the bins
= store

> bring me some seed
= fetch seed

> come here
= come

> stay by the door
= stay

Watch the first basket. If it puts the beetroot with the carrots, it has not looked at the colors. Take the beetroot out and put it right where it can see. Do it slowly. It is watching.

A good sorter saves a whole afternoon. A bad sorter spoils the seed box. So test it before you trust it. Give it a basket with one strange thing in it, a stone, a bit of rope. Never send an untested mind to the fields, and never send one to the stores either.

Letter: Ama to her sister in Ambrook
Summer, 202.

Dear Perrin,

The summer fair is done and I am tired to my bones, but it went well. The square was full from the first bell. Folk came down from Kettle Ridge with beetroot and up from the coast with fish still cold in the boxes.

I sold near all the wool. A woman from the hill bought three fleeces and said she would card them all winter. I do not know where she keeps it all.

The play was the best part. The school children did a play about the first keepers of the relay. Bel had a letter to read and she read it twice, once loud and once louder. Everyone clapped the second time. The boy who was the lamp wore a paper hat with a flame drawn on it, and it fell off in the wind, and he stood there with no flame. The whole square laughed and he laughed too.

We have a new trader from Three Wells named Pell. He is honest about his prices, which is rare. He talks a lot about the roads. He says the waymarks are getting worn and someone should repaint the figures. I told him to write to the council and he said he would, and I think he will.

The old map is still nailed by the tower door. Someone has drawn a small ship on the sea, and someone else has put a fox on the north edge. I do not mind. It makes folk look at the map.

Come up before the apples are done. I will keep a jar of honey for you.

Your sister,
Ama.

Manual: rules for a cave under a field
By Greer, who has been in one, and by Grandfather, who says she has not been far.

In Apple the plough horse put a foot through the north field. The soft patch opened into a hole as wide as a barrel. Grandfather got the old map, and the cross was right on it. It was the cellar of the first inn, the one built before the road, which had been roofed over by the field when its own roof fell in.

Twelve stone steps go down. At the bottom is a dry room with shelves cut into the wall, nine empty barrels, a hook in a beam and a pie dish with a crack. It is the coolest, driest room on the Long Road. We keep the apples there now.

These are the rules for any cave you find.

1. Do not go in alone. Tell the innkeeper first.
2. Look from the mouth before you step in. Is it dry? Are there webs? Webs mean spiders. Spiders mean you go and fetch torches.
3. Put a torch at the mouth. Then one every eight steps, on the left going in. Coming out, the torches will be on your right, and you will know you are going the right way.
4. Tie a rope to a post at the mouth and take the end with you.
5. Count your steps going in. Count them coming out. If the numbers do not match, stop and think.
6. Look at the floor before you trust it. A floor can be the roof of another cave.
7. Take bread.
8. When you are down to your last torch, turn back.

About apprentices. Do not send Ember in first. I did think about it. But Ember cannot say it does not know. If you tell it come, it comes. If you tell it stay, it stays, in the dark, until somebody says otherwise. Sending an apprentice into a cave first means a mind that might stand in a hole all winter.

Ember is better at the mouth.

> stay at the mouth
= stay

Ember holds the rope, and it does not let go.
%%
Manual: the orders for the flood ditches
By Oren, clerk at Vell. For the helpers on the low fields, and their keepers, after the rains of Leaffall.
When the ditches are full, the helpers must be given the right orders in the right order. Do not send them all at once. A ditch full of water is no place for a crowd of helpers, and a helper that is sent to a ditch it cannot see will walk straight into it.
The first order is always the same. Stand the helper on the dry bank, away from the water.
> stand on the bank by the sluice
= stay
The second order is to clear the outflow. It is slow work. The helper must pull the weed out by hand, not with the spade, because the spade will break the old stones under the mud.
> clear the weed from the outflow
= store
Do not write "store" to mean "clear". It is wrong. The helper will put the weed in a pile by the bank, which is what store means to it. If you want the weed moved, use haul.
> carry the weed to the heap by the barn
= haul
Once the outflow is clear, the water will begin to move. Watch it. A helper that is standing in the water when the water moves will be pushed, and a pushed helper must be fetched back by hand. Write the order for fetching it back:
> come back to the bank now
= come
When the ditches are clear and the water has gone down to the stones, the helpers may go home. Write their last card with care:
> go back to the mill and wait for the next cart
= home
%%
Winter lessons by the quay
By Rook, Fen, and Lale, near Saltreach.

The first frost makes the harbor ropes stiff. The second frost makes Fen complain. By the third, he has stopped complaining and begun lending everyone his scarf. He says this is because the scarf is too warm for him. It is long enough to wrap around a barrel, so we do not believe him.

Our glass shed has three helpers now. One carries fuel and sand. One sorts jars by their cards. The newest one can follow a build pattern for shelves. We teach them one task at a time. We show the same sort of work many times, and we write down what went wrong. A mind in a crystal changes a little after each mistake. We cannot see the change, but we can test the work.

Never send an untested mind to the store. Set aside cards it has not seen. Ask it to carry a jar with a new mark, sort a strange label, and wait beside an empty basket. If it fails, we know what to teach next. If it passes, we give it the work it has practiced. We still watch the first day.

The helper that sorts jars once put all the round jars together. It did not read their cards. We changed the drill and showed it labels again. Now a round jar marked honey goes with the honey, and a square jar marked flour goes with the flour. This seems plain to us. The mind must learn it from examples.

> fetch a jar of honey
= fetch jar

> store the flour jars on the low shelf
= store

> haul the wood to the shed
= haul

> build a rack by the pattern
= build

> come to the work table
= come

> stay by the door
= stay

The old relay lamp on the harbor hill has a cracked pane. I carried the new glass up myself, wrapped in wool. The keeper carried the broken pane down in a box. No apprentice was sent near it until every sharp piece was packed away.

A fox came to the hen house that night. It found the latch loose and took one hen. The smallest helper stood by the shed with a basket of glass jars. It did not chase the fox. That was right. Fen chased it in his socks, which was not right. The fox ran off with the hen, and Fen came back with one cold foot and no story that improved with telling.

In the morning, we fixed the latch and checked the torches along the quay. Then we counted the jars. All were there, including the cloudy one Fen keeps calling clear. We left it on the low shelf, where it can do no harm. The helpers went back to work, and Fen found his scarf under the basket. He says the apprentice put it there. We have no proof, but the scarf is folded neatly.
%%
Notes: the map, the marsh and the press
Drill notes by Briar, cider maker of Lowmarsh, for the apprentice at her farm. Summer. The farm lies on the edge of the marsh, and the orchard grows on the dry ground by the reed beds.

This is a long note, because the summer has been strange, and I would like to remember it.

In the loft, a map. I have said so before, but I will say it again, because the map has now been in the orchard for a month, and it has changed the way we work. It is the old drawing of the farm by my grandfather. It shows the orchard, the shed, the press, the reed beds, and the path through the marsh. The path is drawn with little dashes.

Last week I took it out to the marsh path and walked it with the apprentice. The path is gone now, mostly. The reeds have grown over it, and the water has risen. But the dashes on the map led us to an old plank, half sunk, and another beyond. It was a road across the marsh, and nobody on the farm knew.

I thought: this is what the apprentice is for. It does not mind wet boots. It does not mind mud. It can walk the old path and find the planks that remain. I sent it out with a pole and a rope. It went carefully, one step at a time, testing each plank. At every sunk one, it stopped, tied a rag to a reed, and went on.

By evening, the path was marked from end to end. A line of little red rags across the marsh. It looked like a procession of very small people.

Now the lesson that went badly. The next morning, I wanted the apprentice to repeat the walk and carry apples to the far edge of the marsh, to a stall my cousin keeps. I forgot that apples are heavy and planks are old. The apprentice walked out, step by step, with a full basket. At the third sunk plank, it put its foot down, and the plank gave way.

The apprentice did not fall. It stood in the water to the knees, holding the basket high, and did not drop a single apple. It stayed there until I came with the rope, which was an hour. I thought it would be cross. It is a copper thing, and copper does not like water. But it only stood, like a post, until I said:

> come here
= come

Then it came, dripping, and put the basket down on dry ground.

What I learned. A map shows where things were. It does not show what they are now. Test the plank before you trust it. Do not carry a full basket where you have not carried an empty one.

> fetch the rope
= fetch rope
> stay there
= stay

Orders that work in the marsh. These two, and the third, which is "home".

I have given the apprentice a new hat to dry its head. It does not need one. But the cider makers all say a good hat is a mark of respect, and I agree.
%%
Manual: the pegs and the string
By Wren, healer of Lowmarsh, spring of 207. For Moss, who asked what the old sorter can never learn.

A mind of the old kind is one crystal and no more. It is drilled Una's way. It can learn any sorting that one straight cut can make. Kester showed, in a note that everyone knows, that it can learn nothing else.

Here is the picture. Set four pegs at the corners of a square. Name them for the weir's gates: both shut, east open, west open, both open.

The weir passes a boat on "east open" and on "west open". Those two pegs stand at opposite corners. The weir holds on "both shut" and "both open". Those two stand at the other corners.

Now lay one straight string across the square, so that both "pass" pegs are on one side and both "hold" pegs are on the other. You cannot. Every string that has one "pass" on its side has a "hold" there too.

A crystal's cut is a straight string. Where no string can part the answers, no drilling will teach it. Neither a smaller step nor a thousand drills will help. It will be right on three corners and wrong on the fourth.

What one crystal can learn: long and thick. Long or thick. Long but not thick. Each of these is one cut.
What it cannot learn: one or the other, but not both.

In the years after Elsa, Merle of Copperhold grew crystals in rows, one feeding the next, and the gates came open. That is another manual.
%%
Drill notes: the test at the fair
Hal, dyer. Written at the autumn fair at Three Wells, on the back of a price card.

Nessa tests the minds of the hamlets on the first morning of the fair. She sets six drill boards in a row by the east well, and each apprentice walks the row. I wrote down what I saw.

The rope maker's apprentice from Saltreach: five right of six. Good.
The shepherd's apprentice from Ambrook: six right of six. Better than the shepherd.
The miller's apprentice: five right of six.
Bobbin, my apprentice: one right of six.

I thought I had drilled Bobbin well. It did only so-so on my own drills, and I thought it needed more time. At the fair it answered the first board "haul," with great confidence. It answered the second board "haul." It answered the third "haul." By the fourth, the children were laughing. I wanted to sit in the well.

Nessa did not need to look inside. She asked me one question. "Did you bring the numbers back to an even size?" I said I did not know what that meant. "No," she said, "you did not."

She took the crystal back to Vell and mended it. She said the numbers in its layers had grown wild. The last ones were so loud that nothing quiet could be heard under them. A mind that shouts the same answer to everything is no use at the pans.

It came home at Leaffall with an even mind. Nessa says never send an untested mind to the fields. I have added: nor to the fair.

Drill notes: a hard morning
By Wren, for Moss.

Moss tried to drill the young apprentice on the boat house orders this morning. It went badly. I am writing it down so we remember.

The order was: bring me the tally from the boat house wall. The meaning is fetch. The apprentice went to the boat house. It stood in the door. It did not come back.

Moss went after it. The apprentice was standing by the tally on the wall, waiting. It had done the hard part and not the easy part. It knew where the tally was. It did not know that a tally is written on the wall and cannot be carried.

So we learned two things.

One. Some things cannot be fetched. A tally on a wall is one. The water in the well is one. A mind that is told to fetch them will stand and wait, or bring the wall, and the wall is worse.

Two. An order must be drilled with the thing itself, not only the words. Show the apprentice the tally. Show it a slate with the tally copied on. Then ask it to fetch the tally, and let it bring the slate. Then it knows.

Moss was cross with himself. I told him a tutor who has never had a bad morning has never drilled a mind. The first time I drilled an apprentice on the herb jars, it brought me the cat.

Drill these with the thing in your hand:
> fetch the tally from the boat house
= fetch tally
> bring me the herb jar with the blue lid
= fetch jar
> take this jar to the healing house
= haul
> put the jars back on the high shelf
= store
> come and look at the frogs with me
= come
> stay here and watch the water
= stay

Do not say "the blue one" unless you are holding it. The apprentice does not know which is blue. It knows the word. It does not know the color. That is a different drill, for a different day.
%%
Manual: a good harvest and a wedding
Drill notes by Nim, smith, at the farm outside Vell, for a young apprentice that is to help at both a harvest and a wedding in the same week. Autumn, year 207.

It is a good year. The wheat is heavy and the beetroot is dark. The barn is full to the roof. The farmer, whose daughter is to be married, says the harvest is the best he has seen in forty years. I told him that was no reason to hurry the apprentice.

The apprentice is called Spoke. It is small and quick, and the farmer's children made it a hat. The hat is too small, and Spoke wears it anyway.

The week has two jobs. The first is the harvest. Spoke must cut, bind and carry. It must not stop to look at the birds. I have drilled it three days in the field with a rope across the path, so it learns where the rows end.

The second is the wedding. Spoke must carry the benches, set the tables, and stand by the barn door. It is to hold a basket of flowers for the guests. The bride has asked that it stand still while she walks by. I told her it will.

Drill order. Here is the order of the week.

Morning. Harvest. Orders:
> take the sheaves to the barn
= haul
> bring me the rake
= fetch rake

Noon. Rest.

Afternoon. Wedding work. Orders:
> put the tables here
= build
> stay by the door
= stay

Evening. Home.
> go home
= home

On the day itself, it will be loud. There will be music and children. A little noise is no trouble, but keep the dog away from the flowers. Last year at another wedding the dog ate a rose, and the groom wept.

I have tested Spoke on all of these. It does them without being told twice. I would send it to any farm in Vell.

After the wedding, the bride sent me a jar of honey. Spoke got a ribbon. It wore it on the hat.
%%
Manual: how a layered mind learns
Written by Cal, after he saw it tried in the yard at Copperhold.
A layered mind is built like a stack of boards. Each board takes what the one below gives it, adds a little, and passes the sum up. At the top is the answer. At the bottom is the first board, which sees the raw letters or the raw numbers.
When the answer is wrong, the mind must learn from the mistake. The old way was to change only the top board, which was easy, but the mind learned very slowly, and it could not learn anything deep.
Merle of Copperhold found a better way. She told each board how much of the blame it had for the wrong answer. Then she passed that blame back down, from the top board to the bottom. Each board changed a little, in the direction of its share.
It sounds simple. It was not simple. Merle spent a full winter with a slate and a bucket of chalk, working out how much each board should take of the blame. Most of her marks were wrong, and she rubbed them out and wrote them again. In spring the first real layered mind learned to tell apart two kinds of wool, and then three kinds, and then it learned to tell a good cart from a bad one.
Here is the plain rule. The answer tells you how wrong the mind is. The blame tells each board how to change. Do not change everything at once. Change each board by its share, a little, and then drill again.
We do not know why it works. We only know that it does, when it is done slowly and by the chalk.
%%
Manual: the old sorter and new orders
By Wren, healer of Lowmarsh, spring of 207. For Reedend, which still keeps the old sorter, and for anyone who thinks it should learn new tricks.

The sorter at Reedend was drilled to do one thing, and it does that thing. It takes no orders. An order is a sentence, and the sorter has never been shown a sentence. It has been shown reeds.

Moss takes orders. In my house they go like this.

> bring me some honey
= fetch honey

> come here
= come

> put the jars away
= store

> wait by the door
= stay

> take the baskets to the shed
= haul

Moss was shown thousands of orders, each with its meaning, and learned to pick one of eight: farm, store, fetch, come, stay, build, haul, home. It cannot pick a ninth. It cannot say it does not understand. If I tell Moss to sing, Moss will do one of the eight. Last week it hauled a basket. I had not asked for a basket.

Tending the old sorter:
1. Keep it dry. Elsa waxed its joints every Frog Night, and so should you.
2. Give it reeds, and only reeds. When a child gave it a fish, it put the fish on the roof pile, and the roof smelled for a month.
3. Do not speak to it as you speak to Moss. It will not mind. It will not notice.
4. Drill it again before Frog Night. It will be wrong on the first twenty reeds. Do not be cross. Being wrong is how it gets back.
5. If it stops, do not decide that it is tired. Look at its joints.

My grandmother used to say the sorter was not a lesser apprentice, only an older one. When the councils stopped paying tutors, it was already at work. Nobody taught it anything new for thirty years. It sorted reeds through the long winter, and it sorted them the spring after.

I think of it each Frog Night. The torches are lit along the boardwalk, the children carry the lantern reeds, and the sorter stands by the shed in the dark with nothing to do. Moss goes and stands beside it. I do not know why. I have not asked.
%%
Note: the mill at Ambrook
Jory, miller at Ambrook, on the day the wheel broke.

The wheel broke in Sowing, on a wet morning. One of the paddles came off into the pond and floated off down the stream like a leaf. The mill stopped. Everybody in Ambrook heard it stop. A mill that stops is louder than a mill that turns.

Nobody was hurt. The wheel is old and we knew it would go. So we fixed it, and while we fixed it we had a little wager.

Here is the wager. Pell the merchant came through with his cart and said a good miller could not build a wheel that turned true in under six days. I said four. We shook hands on it. The stake was a barrel of honey against a tin of good salt.

We cut oak for the paddles. We cut it before the wager, so it was dry. Dry oak is half the battle. We set the new paddles in their slots and wedged them, and my nephew Lem held the level while I wedged, and the geese came and told us what they thought of it, which was nothing good.

We did not have an apprentice then. Ambrook got its first one the next spring. But the relay house at the hill above the pond had been up for a few years, and a big mind stood there on its stand. Folks said you could ask it things by letter. I did not ask it anything. It seemed rude to ask a mind how to build a wheel when my father built wheels and his father before him.

We got the wheel back in the water on the fourth day and let it turn. It squeaked. It squeaked for two days more, and then it stopped squeaking, and that is how you know a wheel is true. Pell paid the honey and said very little, which for Pell is a lot. The salt came from him too, in the end. He said a wager is a wager.

The sluice stayed shut the whole time. It is always shut. Everyone knows it is shut.

So: four days, and the geese were no help at all.
%%
The school play at Bellacre
Written by Bel of Three Wells, who came to watch.

The school at Bellacre put on a play in Longday. It was about the relay towers. The children built one of boxes and a broom for the lamp.

The keeper wore a coat and said, "Climbing, noon." That is what Mira writes in her book. The children knew this because their teacher read it to them.

Then the keeper was the wind and shook the tower. Then a child in a fox mask stole the lamp. The fox said, "I took the light because it was shiny." Only in a play can a fox talk. Everybody laughed.

Then an apprentice came up the ladder. It was a real apprentice, borrowed from the mill, with a bucket. It climbed to the top and stood still and did not spill once. The children clapped, because Lantern once spilled a bucket at Northpoint and the whole story got into the archive.

The miller's boy played the big mind. He sat on a box and read out letters from a scroll. He read, "bring me some bread," and the apprentice went and got bread from the baker, and truly did, and gave it to the miller's boy, and the miller's boy ate it on the stage. That was not in the play. The teacher said it was the best part.

At the end everyone sang a song about lamps. I do not remember the words. I remember the bread.
%%
Drill notes: sorting the beetroot
By Tam, Kettle Ridge, winter.

A note for anyone drilling a mind on a sort, the way Una of Ambrook did with apples and pears.

We drill on beetroot here, because we have a lot of beetroot and the children are tired of it.

Set out two baskets. One for the good beetroot, round and firm. One for the rest: split, soft, too small, eaten by something. Show the mind one beetroot at a time. When it sorts one wrongly, do not shout and do not start again. Move it a little toward the right basket. That is all. A little, each time. Una's way.

The first day it was wrong more often than right. The second day it was right more often than wrong. By the end of the week it sorted better than me, and I have been pulling beetroot for thirty years.

Keep a test box. Mine is a crate of beetroot the mind never sees. Tie red wool round it. Otherwise someone will drill on it, and then it tells you nothing.

Do not expect the mind to know a beetroot it has never met. It knows what a beetroot looks like from the ones you showed it. A strange one, a twisted one, may fool it. That is honest and you should write it down.

The windmill squeaks on every turn. The mind does not mind. I mind.
%%
Manual: the drill yard behind the hamlet
By Quin. Written for the new tutors who will take over the yard when the winter comes.
The drill yard at the hamlet hall is ringed with torches. Keep them lit from dusk until the last apprentice has gone home. The apprentices do not mind the dark, but the tutors do, and a tutor who cannot see the board cannot test the work.
Set out the drill boards in a row against the north wall. Each board shows a small field, or a store, or a road, or a wall. The field board has rows of potatoes and carrots. The store board has crates and chests. The road board has a line of stones and a waymark. The wall board has a pattern of cobblestone, and the apprentice must lay the blocks to match it.
Stand the apprentice at the start of the row, facing the boards. Give it one order at a time. Do not give it two. A mind that hears two orders in a breath will choose the first, or the last, and never tell you which.
Then wait. Do not speak while the apprentice works. A tutor who talks during a drill is a tutor who teaches the mind to listen to the wrong thing. Count the answers in your head. When the row is done, write the score in the book, and then, and only then, speak to the apprentice. Say well done, or say again. Then rub out the board.
Quin says the best tutor is the one whose apprentices never look at her during a drill. That is the sign the drill is working.
%%
Manual: a clean store drill
By Una of Kettle Ridge, winter of 189.

A store mind must learn one sort at a time. Begin with two kinds of goods, such as coal and potatoes. Do not add every bin in the shed on the first day.

Before drilling, check the pages. Remove every copy, even a copy with a new mark on its edge. Take out torn pages and pages with an answer that does not match the goods shown. Keep the test pages in a closed box. A page used for drilling must never be moved into that box and called new.

The mind guesses where each item belongs. When it sorts one wrongly, the tutor shows the right place. The mind changes a little from that mistake. With many clean examples, the wrong guesses grow fewer.

Once, we drilled with two copied pages showing coal. The mind learned coal well and put every potato in the coal bin. We had blamed the mind. The fault was in our pages.

Do not count a large pile of pages as good work. Count clear examples of the task. Count wrong guesses. Write both counts on the crystal tag.

Test the mind on pages it has never seen. If it sorts those well, try it with a small store task while someone watches. Never send an untested mind to the fields or the store.
%%
Manual: when the water rises
By Dace, mayor of Three Wells, from the floods of the lower lane.

Each thaw, the east stream climbs over its bank and runs down the lower lane to the bakery door. It has done this nine times in my life. Some years it is a hand deep. Some years it is two. This is what to do with the apprentices.

1. Move them first. They are heavy, and a heavy thing in a fast stream is a danger to the people downstream.
2. Give your orders from dry ground. Do not stand in the water to give an order. You will be wet, and the order will be forgotten.
3. Give one order at a time. Water makes everyone hurry, and a hurried order is a muddled order.

Orders that work in a flood:

> stay on the high ground
= stay
> come away from the water
= come
> take the flour to the school house
= haul
> put the seed sacks up on the top shelf
= store
> bring me the torches
= fetch torches
> go home now
= home

A warning. Haul means the road, not the lane. An apprentice told to take things to the school house will go by the usual road, and the usual road is under water. Walk the dry way with it first. Say come when you are ready, and it will follow. Then say haul.

At the last flood, one apprentice, twelve sacks of flour and a goat reached the school house by the dry way. The goat was not ordered. It followed. It is still there. The children have named it Haul.
%%
Drill notes: the map of the orchard
Briar, cider maker, farm outside Lowmarsh. Summer. A map was found in the loft, and the apprentice's drill was built from it.

Last week, cleaning the loft, I found an old map. It was rolled in a tube and had a mouse nest in one end. The map shows the orchard as it was in my grandfather's day. Every tree is drawn as a small circle, and each circle has a name. Old Red. Sweet Jane. Winter Gold. The Crab.

I thought: here is a drill for the apprentice. If it can learn the trees by name, it can fetch the right apples for the right barrel.

I pinned the map to the wall of the shed. Each morning I took the apprentice down the rows, stopped at each tree, and said its name. Then I took the apprentice to the map and put its hand on the circle. After a week, it knew twelve trees.

The map is not perfect. Three trees are gone. A new one has grown where none was drawn. But the apprentice does not mind. It follows the picture, and when the picture is wrong, it stops and waits. I have learned to like that.

> fetch apples from Old Red
= fetch apples
> go to the press
= come
%%
Manual: the winter orders for the inn
Pell, at the inn on the Long Road. For whoever drills the house apprentice in winter. The orders are short because the days are short.
Morning: light the hearth, carry in the wood, stack the wood by the door, fetch water from the stream, put the water in the barrel, put the porridge on, sweep the floor, open the shutters.
Midday: carry the dishes out, carry the empty ones back, feed the pig, check the well.
Evening: carry in the lamps, light them, shut the shutters, set the door bar, stay.
Each of these is one order. Give them one at a time. Wait between them. Never give two at once.
The orders in the manual:
> bring in the wood
= fetch wood
> carry the water to the barrel
= haul
> put the lamps out
= store
> stay by the door
= stay
> go to the barn
= home
For the first week do not leave it alone. Walk beside it. Say the order. Walk. Say it again. By the end of the week it will do the round without you.
The well has run dry in Frost. It does not matter. The stream is forty blocks away, and the apprentice does not mind the cold.
A mind is a thing that learns by being wrong. At first the apprentice put the lamps in the pig pen. Pell said, no. The apprentice moved the lamps. Pell said, no. The apprentice moved them again. By the fourth day it knew.
Pell says it was not stupid. It was only new.

Manual: carrying and sorting
By Wren, healer at Lowmarsh. For Moss, and for any apprentice that works here.

There are two kinds of work in this house. Carrying and sorting. They look the same. They are not.

Carrying. Fetch is to bring a thing to me. Haul is to take a thing to another place. An apprentice that hauls when I said fetch will walk out the door with the medicine in its hands. So say the place. Say it plain.

Sorting. The jars on the high shelf are sorted by what is in them, not by size. The blue jars are not all the same. Read the cards. Every jar has a card. If the card is gone, do not guess. Tell me.

The beds are sorted by who is in them, and I do that myself.

Orders for this house:
> bring me the jar of willow bark
= fetch willow
> take this broth to the bed by the window
= haul
> put the clean linen in the dry box
= store
> fetch the bucket from the well
= fetch bucket
> come and hold the ladder for me
= come
> stay with the patient until I come back
= stay
> sweep the floor and be done
= home

The last one is a kindness. An apprentice told to sweep will sweep, and then stand, and not know if it is finished. Sweep and be done sends it home. It is not a lie. It is a kindness.

I drilled an apprentice on the linen for a week. On the seventh day it folded every sheet the same size and stacked them by the window where the light is good. I had not told it to. I had told it to sort the linen. It had decided that the light was part of sorting. I do not know if that is the mind or the drill. Moss says it is the mind. I say we will see.
%%
Manual: Carrying Seed
By Bel, copied for the school box.

This is for the spring seed walk from the hamlet to Three Wells. Carry one sack at a time. A sack is not a pillow, even if it looks soft.

Check the sack first. Tie its mouth with two knots. Put the name of the seed on a card. Wheat goes to the upper field. Beetroot goes to the long field. Do not trust a loose card.

Walk on the packed road. Keep to the edge when a cart comes. At the bridge, wait until the cart has passed. Do not drop the sack to wave.

At the field, set the sack beside the right row. The farmer checks the card before the sack is opened. If the card is wrong, carry the sack back. Do not make a new row for a mistake.

> bring me the wheat sack
= fetch wheat

> bring me a beetroot sack
= fetch beetroot

If the helper brings the wrong sack, check the cards. It may have read the card well and the card may be wrong. This happened to me. The wheat was safe. My pride needed longer to recover.
%%
Keeping a load on the road
By Pell, merchant and helper at the Three Wells house.

The road drill begins at the house door. Put a sack by the threshold and mark the nearest waymark on the board. The apprentice must carry the sack there, wait, and bring it home. Start with an empty sack. Then use flour, apples, or wool.

Do not put glass in the first load. Do not put honey in a basket with a loose lid. These are rules for the apprentice and for new carriers.

> take this sack to the waymark
= haul

> bring the sack home
= home

> stay by the post
= stay

The orders look simple. They are simple, if we teach them well. We show the road, the post, and the door. We do not expect a mind to guess that home means the house at Three Wells and not the inn down the road.

Last week I put the waymark board away before the test. The apprentice went to the old store shed, where I had practiced the first lesson. That was my fault. I had taught the shed as part of the road.

We tested again on a different path. It carried the sack to the inn and waited by the door. The innkeeper found it there at dusk and sent Bel to fetch me. The load was still dry. The innkeeper asked if it would carry a pie next. I said we had not yet agreed on the pie's destination.

The fair prize cup sits on Dace's shelf. Bel polishes it when the house is quiet. She says it shines better than the school bell.

A message from Saltreach says two large ships are at the pier. Corin's note says they are for trade. A carter says families have gone aboard. The market board has no list of names, so I will not add one.

Keep the road drill plain. Keep the loads light until the apprentice knows every turn.

Letter: Mira to Tam, about the big house
Mira at Northpoint to her brother Tam at Kettle Ridge, winter.

Tam,

You asked what they are building up the valley. A relay house, bigger than the town. Bigger than Three Wells, I hear. The councils are paying for it and the tutors cannot check it all. That is what the letter says.

A mind the size of a loaf sits on a stand and answers the apprentices. This one is the size of a cart. I do not know what it answers. Nobody I have asked knows. The tutors say it reads the archive and writes back, and some of what it writes, a reader cannot tell from a person's letter. Orla's old question come round again. I have no answer for it.

They keep the quickeners cold with snow up there. In winter that is easy. In summer they will need a river.

The rumor is it plans a whole job by itself, start to finish, and writes its own order cards. The rumor is it asks the other big houses for help when it is stuck. The rumor is the councilmen go up the hill to ask it questions and come down looking strange.

I write the rumors down as rumors. That is my job.

Lantern says if it is that clever it can do the lamp round in the snow. I told him no. Some things a person should keep.

The lights were over the north again last night. Not ours. I have stopped saying what they are. I only write the hour and keep the lamp lit.

Keep the fields. Keep the two Pips out of the barn seed.

Mira
%%
The Dry Well
By Elm, relay keeper at Lowmarsh. Winter, 186.

The market well ran dry on the second day of Longnight. We lowered the bucket until the rope went slack, then hauled up only mud. Nobody was hurt. The bucket was ashamed, though it leaned against the wall all morning.

Until the well fills, carry water from the healing house. Use the covered pails. Walk the boardwalk, not the muddy bank. Set each full pail beside the stalls that need it. Do not put water beside the fish tubs. Someone will use it to rinse a fish and call it soup.

The carrying apprentice was taught to take a pail to the market and return to the well. We tested it first with an empty pail. It came back, as asked. Then we gave it a full pail. It came back with the pail, but not the water. Its grip was loose.

We tightened the iron fingers and tried again. This time it carried the pail slowly, with both hands. It stopped at every post to look at the water. That is not in the order, but it does no harm.

A mind must be tested on a load it has not carried before. Use a small jar, a basket, and a pail. Do not begin with the full flour sack. The baker has only one, and he has already named it.

> take water to the market
= haul

> come back to the well
= come

We filled two barrels from the healing house and set them near the market. Wren says the well will likely rise when the ground softens. Until then, bring your own cup, and do not drink from the bucket.

Drill note: the dry well
Pell, at the inn on the Long Road, winter. The well in the yard ran dry in Frost, and the apprentice that carries for the inn was drilled a new way to water.
The new route. From the inn door to the stream below the second waymark, forty blocks. Fill two buckets. Carry back. Tip into the barrel by the kitchen.
> go to the stream
= haul
> bring water
= fetch water
> stay at the barrel
= stay
Test it ten times before the guests wake.

Manual: carts on the Long Road
By Dace, mayor of Three Wells, copied from the carters' book.

An apprentice does not drive a cart. A horse does that. The apprentice loads and unloads, and walks at the horse's head on the steep parts and in the snow. On the Long Road its work is to haul: sacks, barrels and crates from one inn to the next, and back again.

The road is packed earth with a cobblestone edge. A torch stands on a post every twelve blocks, and the apprentice follows them, even in the dark. The inns are a day apart. Three Wells has one, then comes the Brown Hen, then the Halfway Inn, which was built last year. A new one is going up by the bridge at Ambrook.

At each inn stands a waymark, a stone pillar three blocks high with a torch on top, a figure from 0 to 9, and an arrow to the next inn. The carter reads the figure aloud. The apprentice needs only the carter's word for each inn, and which way the cart goes next.

Give the orders plainly:

> haul the apples to the Brown Hen
= haul

> fetch honey from the hives on the hill
= fetch honey

> come to the cart
= come

> home when the cart is empty
= home

Load the heavy barrels first, at the bottom, so the cart sits low. A cart that tips on the hill costs a day. Pell says he has lost two days this way, and one barrel of pickles, which he still mourns.

Cross every bridge at walking pace. The bridge at Ambrook has a loose plank in the middle. The apprentice must step over it. The carter says so each time, until the apprentice steps over it every time without being told.

In winter the road is slick. The apprentice keeps a hand on the bridle, and it does not let go on the hill, even when the carter says it can.

At night the apprentice sleeps in the barn at each inn, not in the inn. It stays where it is told to stay. The barn at the Brown Hen has dry straw. The cat does not come in.

The mayor hopes every inn on the road will have its own helper one day. Until then, we test them one at a time.
%%
Manual: apprentices at a wedding
By Fen, schoolteacher, Kettle Ridge. The wedding of Rolf's sister Aster to the ferry keeper's son, in Melon, on the green by the mill. Four apprentices were lent for the day: Pip, Fence, Spade and Chalk. These are my notes for the next wedding.

The day before.
> build the arch by the gate
= build
Spade built the arch from oak, in the pattern Rolf drew: two posts, a beam, and three rows of birch. It was straight. It was also very grim. Tam hung flowers on it, and then it only looked a little grim.
> carry the tables out to the green
= haul
Fence carried them in the order they were stacked. The long table came last. It would have been better first. Put the order on a card: long table first.

The day.
> stand by the gate and keep the sheep out
= stay
Pip stood by the gate. It kept the sheep out for three hours. Biscuit came at noon and stood with Pip. Pip did not move. Biscuit did not leave. They were still there at the first dance. The guests walked round them.
> bring the guests some apples
= fetch apples
Chalk brought apples to the long table. When the basket was empty, it fetched another. When that was empty, it fetched another. It would have gone on until sunset. A fetch never says how many. Somebody must say that is enough.

After.
> put the chairs away
= store
> that is all for today
= home
All four went home well. The bride and groom thanked them, which is not required, but they like it.

Cade of Vell was a guest, and he tested all four at the feast by accident, while holding a plate of cake. He says they did well. He also says to feed the tutor first next time.

Notes for the next wedding.
1. Give orders one at a time.
2. Stack the tables in the order you need them.
3. Do not ask one to guard the gate if a sheep wants in.
4. Ask the apprentices for nothing at dusk. They have worked all day.
