Manual: drill notes for the winter store
Cress, egg seller, farm outside Saltreach. For a new apprentice that has to learn the store, in winter. I wrote this with the broken wheel in mind, since everything we had to store then was out of place. I keep it by the door.
The winter store is a cold room dug into the ground, behind the barn. It holds apples, roots, salt fish, dried kelp and sacks of grain. In winter it is the most important room on the farm. A new apprentice must learn it.
Step one. Walk the apprentice through the store with a lamp. Slowly. Show each shelf. Do not speak. It has no words for the shelves. It learns by seeing where things sit.
Step two. Put a sack of grain in its arms and carry it to the grain bin. Set it down. Do it again. Do it with a sack of roots, and the root bin. Do it with fish, and the fish barrel. Repeat for three days.
Step three. Mix them. Hand it a sack, any sack, and watch where it goes. A mind that has learned the bins will go to the right one. A mind that has only learned the walk will go to the first. If it goes wrong, take it back and show it. If it goes right, say nothing. It is doing its work.
Step four. Say the order.
> put this away
= store
Say it at the door of the store. Say it in the yard. Say it in the kitchen, with a sack on the table. The apprentice should carry the sack to the right bin in each case. If it stands still, it did not understand. Try again.
Step five. Test it on something new. Hand it a sack of dried peas. It has never seen peas. If it carries the peas to the bin with the grain, it is close. If it carries them to the fish, it is not. Drill again.
There are limits. An apprentice cannot tell you that the fish is spoiled. It cannot tell you the roots are rotting. It cannot say a bin is full. You must check yourself. A good store keeper walks the room every evening, with a lamp, and with her own nose.
The broken wheel is mended now. We have flour again, and the store is full. The apprentice carries and stacks. It will do so until the thaw. In the spring, when the roots are low and the apples soft, it will stand by the door and wait for the next order.
That is the whole of a winter store, as I know it. It is not complex. It is only slow, and it is cold, and it is worth every minute.
%%
Manual: the even vat
By Hal, dyer, at the salt pans near Saltreach.

A dyer who dips wool in three vats, one after another, must watch the strength of each. If every vat is a little too strong, the wool comes out of the third one black. So before every dip I test the vat with a white scrap of wool. Too dark: I add water. Too pale: I add dye. The vat is always the same strength, and the color is what I meant.

Nessa of Vell says a mind is built the same way. Each layer is a vat. The numbers pass through the layers one after another. If a layer does not keep its numbers an even size, they grow wild, and the answer comes out black.
%%
Manual: the layers and the blame
By Merle of Copperhold, as written up at the Vell school by her pupil Ten.
You will need a slate, a bucket of chalk, a box of drill cards and a clean cloth for your hands.
First, make the mind. A layered mind has at least three boards. Stack them in a frame so each one can see the one below it. Set each board in its own slot, and mark the slot with its number. Board one sees the letters, board two sees the pairs, and board three sees the words.
Second, show it a card. Say what the card is, and then ask the mind to say what it is. Write its answer on the slate.
Third, if the answer is right, pass to the next card. Do not praise the mind more than you would praise a good boat.
Fourth, if the answer is wrong, find the size of the wrong. Count the letters that were out. A small wrong is a few letters. A big wrong is a whole word. The bigger the wrong, the bigger the blame.
Fifth, pass the blame down. Start with board three. Give it its share, which is the whole blame times its weight. Then board two gets its share of what came up from board three. Then board one gets the rest.
Sixth, change each board by its share. Do not change it by the whole blame. If you do, the mind will shake itself to pieces, and you will be writing in the book all winter.
Seventh, drill again. Use a new card, not the same one. A mind that sees the same card forever only learns that card.
Eighth, rub out the slate. Keep the book. Write the day, the score and the change.
Ten writes that the first year he got the sums wrong, every single day, and that Merle never once shouted. She only rubbed out the chalk and said: again.
%%
A drill for walls and shelves
By Rook, with notes from Fen at the harbor store.

The harbor store needs shelves for jars and rope. We built them from oak boards and cobblestone feet. The shelves must stand level. A shelf that leans spills jars one at a time, which is worse than spilling them all at once. Then everyone has to bend down.

Before an apprentice builds, draw the pattern on a board. Mark the floor with stones. Show where each post goes and how high each shelf must be. Put the spare boards in one pile and the spare cobblestone in another. A builder should not have to guess which pile is which.

The first helper at the store was simple. It could carry boards but could not follow a whole pattern. Fen stood beside it and placed each board by hand. Later we got a mind that knew rows and piles. It learned to place one board between two posts. It still put the longest board at the bottom, which was sensible, even though it was not on the first card.

> bring oak boards to the floor
= fetch oak boards

> build a shelf by the marks
= build

> store cobblestone by the wall
= store

> stay until the shelf is checked
= stay

Test the builder on a small frame before giving it the full store. Use new marks for the test. If it copies the marks without looking, it has learned the pattern. If it makes a shelf for a sheep, the marks were poor, or the sheep was very persuasive.

Fen says to check every joint by hand. The apprentice can build straight rows, but it cannot feel a loose board as well as a person. We checked the new shelves, tightened two joints, and put the jars away. Nothing fell during the night. In the morning, a cat was asleep on the top shelf. We left it there. It had tested the shelf more gently than Fen would have.

Drill note: carts and crates
By Rafe, Saltreach.

Pell's cart stops at our house when he brings goods from the Long Road. The road is packed earth beyond the town, with cobblestone edges. After rain, the cart wheels sink where the ditch crosses the track. We put flat stone there and keep the crates on the high side.

The apprentice helps unload. First it brings each crate to the store door. Then it waits while I read the card. Some crates hold iron, some hold coal, and some hold glass. The glass crates have straw packed around them. Keep them upright. A broken pane costs more than a good supper.

We teach sorting with clean examples. Put one iron crate on the iron mark and one coal crate on the coal mark. Show the apprentice the card before it lifts. If it carries coal to the iron place, bring the crate back. Do not change both marks at once. A mind learns little from a muddle.

Test it with new crates from Pell's next load. Change their places, not their labels. If it sorts well, let it help at the store. If it does not, keep it beside me. It can still carry empty sacks without making a choice.

Pell says his cart is not heavy. His horse says otherwise. The horse stops at our door and eats the loose hay.

> take the iron crate to the iron mark
= haul
> take the coal crate to the coal mark
= haul
> bring an empty crate to the shed
= fetch crate
> stay by the store door
= stay

A Row of Stones
By Tavin, keeper of the little yard near Vell.

Our hamlet has a low stone wall and one room for the drill boards. It is small enough that I can see the gate from the bench.

The tutor sent one copper helper to move stones into a row. I laid out six cobblestones, each apart from the next. The helper picked one up and set it by the first mark.

It did the same for the other stones. One rolled into the mud. I dried it with my sleeve and set it back.

We practised again with another row. The helper learned the space between the marks. It did not learn to stack stones into a wall. That is a different task, and we have not shown it.

Children ask if an apprentice knows what it is doing. I tell them what I can see. It follows examples. It does not tell us what it knows.

The tutor says a crystal changes a little when the helper gets an example wrong. I do not see the change. I see only that the next row is straighter.

We keep a tally on the board. Today the row was right five times. Yesterday it was right four times. I will not send it to build the hamlet wall.

Tutor note: the loose bridge plank
By Nessa, at the Ambrook market, 203.

The market at Ambrook stands by the mill pond path. Carts come over the bridge, pass the bee hives, and turn beside the apple stalls. The bridge has one loose plank. Mark it with a strip of wool until Jory repairs it. Do not teach an apprentice to carry a load over a plank that shifts.

The market crew has three apprentices this Apple. Pin carries baskets. Rill sorts goods. Tern follows the bridge plan and builds low walls around the stall yard. Their minds were tested at Vell before they came here. Test them again after any long drill.

For carrying, begin with an empty basket. Show the apprentice the path from the shed to the stall. Then add one apple, then several. Keep the load small until it walks without dropping fruit. A dropped apple rolls downhill. A dropped honey jar does not.

For sorting, use clean examples. Apples go with apples, carrots with carrots, and honey jars with honey jars. Keep spoiled fruit out of the drill box. Set aside pages and examples used for the test. No mind should drill on its own test.

For building, draw the wall on a board before giving the order. Show the first block, the next block, and the corner. Tern once made a wall with the corner facing the pond. The wall held well. The stall did not.

> bring me apples from the shed
= fetch apples

> take the full baskets to the stall
= haul

> put each honey jar on the dry table
= store

> build a low wall by the cart path
= build

> come back to the tutor's bench
= come

> stay beside the wall
= stay

> go home to the tool rack
= home

> farm the bare patch by the mill
= farm

Do not give several meanings to one order. The small mind chooses one meaning and cannot explain its choice. Ask for one task at a time.

Pell says this is too much care for a basket of apples. Pell also says Pin saved his back last week. He now gives Pin the biggest basket.

Manual: what to do about a sick cow
By Bel of Three Wells, for the market apprentices, winter. Bel wrote this after her aunt's cow got sick and the apprentice at the dairy did not know what to do. It is not a healing book. It is only for the apprentice.

If a cow is sick, the apprentice must not decide what is wrong. It must fetch someone who can.
1. Look at the cow. Is she standing? Is she eating? If not, go to step two.
2. Stand by the cow and do not leave her, until a person comes. A person will tell you what to fetch, and the healer may ask for a jar from the shed.
> bring me the blue jar from the shed
= fetch jar
3. If no person answers, go to the nearest well and wait there. A person will pass.
4. Never give the cow anything. Not water, not hay, not herbs. The healer will know.

An apprentice that has been drilled on this will come for you every time. It will not cure the cow. It will tell you.

Manual: orders for a wedding
By Bel and Uncle Quin, for the two apprentices at Quin's farm outside Three Wells, Latch and Hinge. Spring of 208. Bel's cousin Nell is to be married in the long field on the first fine day of Blossom, to a boy from Ambrook. There will be 120 guests, 10 tables, one cake and one cat. The cat gives no orders and takes none.
Tables and chairs.
> carry the tables out to the long field
= haul
> bring the chairs from the barn
= haul
> set the benches in a row along the hedge
= build
Food and drink.
> we are short of cider
= fetch cider
> the cake needs more flour
= fetch flour
> put the cheeses in the cold shed until noon
= store
Waiting and coming.
> stand by the gate until the guests have come
= stay
> over here, please, the cart is stuck
= come
When it is over.
> that will do, thank you
= home
Do not give an apprentice an order that has no meaning. Latch has no meaning for light the lamps, or throw the petals, or dance. If you say them, it picks one of the eight and does it. Last year at the Dunn wedding an apprentice was told to dance and it hauled the bride's uncle into the pond. He was very good about it.
The lamps, the petals and the dance are for people.

Manual: the apprentice and the autumn
The farm outside Lowmarsh, autumn. Kell wrote this page the week the gift arrived, because the gift was a new apprentice and nobody on the farm knew how to hold it.
It came from my cousin at Copperhold, in a crate, packed in straw. Copper arms, iron legs, a small crystal behind the chest plate, wrapped in wool. A card with it: "Treat it kindly. It knows nothing yet."
That is true of all of them. An apprentice wakes knowing nothing. What it knows, it learned.
First day. Stand it in the yard. Say its name. It will not answer, because it does not talk. Do the job in front of it. Pick up a basket, carry it to the barn, put it down. Do this ten times.
Second day. Put the basket in its arms. Say "haul". Walk beside it to the barn. Say the word again at the door.
Third day. Say the word from the gate. Watch. If it goes the wrong way, do not shout. Walk it back and start again.
The orders it needs first:
> carry the baskets to the barn
= haul
> wait by the gate
= stay
> come to me
= come
> go inside
= home
It will make mistakes. Each one is a small lesson. Do not throw the lesson away.
When it does a job right three times in a row, give it the job. Test it once more on a quiet day. Then it may go to the fields.
The cat has taken to sitting on its shoulder. The cat is not part of the drill.

Manual: the rope drill, for the barn
Cress, egg seller, the farm outside Saltreach, in winter. This is the drill we use for the apprentice that carries the feed.
The drill takes three mornings. Do it before the sun is up, while the barn is quiet.
Morning one. Stand the apprentice by the feed sacks. Lift one sack onto its back. Walk to the pig pen. Set it down. Say the order, then walk back with it.
> carry the feed to the pigs
= haul
Do it ten times. The apprentice will be wrong at first. It will drop the sack at the wrong pen, or turn the wrong way. Do not scold. Take it back and show it again.
Morning two. Do it without lifting. Say the order from the door, and watch. If it goes to the pen, good. If it goes to the hen house, take it to the pen and say the order again.
Morning three. Say the order from the house. If it hauls the feed to the pigs, it has it.
Test it on the fourth morning with something new: a sack of oats, not the usual feed. If it does the right thing with the oats, it knows what haul means. If it stops, drill again.
Winter note: the pig pen floor is icy. Put sand down.
The pigs are well. They like the apprentice. One of them follows it about.

Note on the iron tray
By Hara, forge keeper at Copperhold
Spring of 176

We tested a furnace mind by letting it tend a small melt. We did not give it the right answers. It tried a setting, watched the heat, and tried again.

When the melt held steady, we marked that try as good. When it grew too hot, we marked that try as poor. The mind kept more of the tries that went well. After many turns, it held the heat better.

Do not tell apprentices that the mind knows why the melt changes. It has learned which choices have served it. That is enough for the furnace.

The first iron was lumpy. The second was fit for a hinge. We kept both in the shed, so the next tutor could see the difference.

A cat sat on the warm stone and would not move. The furnace mind did not count the cat as a fuel.

Long winter at the press shed
By Opal, Deepsnow 201

The snow covered the road to Copperhold. For many days no cart came down the hill. The wind pushed snow against the press shed, and the roof began to creak.

Jori had mended it in Apple. He had set new oak along the north edge and fixed the loose stones. We thought the roof would last. It did last, but we still heard it groan in the night.

Clank carried wood from the store to the door. I told him to leave the casks where they were. He did so. A cask is hard to move over snow, and cider is no help to a cold roof.

The small crystal in Clank had fewer marks than the first copy. It learned the same orders, but sometimes mixed up the store and the cart when both were named. We used the short orders from the drill book.

> carry oak logs to the shed
= haul
> put the dry wood in the store
= store
> stay inside the door
= stay
> bring me an apple
= fetch apples
> come to the fire
= come
> go home to the tool rack
= home

The apprentice brought the wood and stood by the door. We kept the torch lit. There was no need to ask it to mend the roof. It knew no roof work, and we had not taught it.

When the snow eased, Jori climbed up and checked every row. One stone had shifted. He set it right. The roof held through the next storm.

A lesson from the cider shed
By Opal, Reaping 203

The tutor at Copperhold asked me to explain the small crystals to new helpers. I told them about the cask mind.

A large mind had been drilled on many orders. Its weights were written in many marks. The tutor made a copy with fewer marks. That copy could fit in a small crystal, which could ride inside an apprentice.

The copy was close to the large mind, not equal in every answer. It carried casks and sorted apples well enough for our shed. It sometimes chose the wrong place when two choices were close. We tested it before giving it a task.

A pupil asked if the smaller crystal was cheaper to make. I said I did not know. I know only that it held fewer marks and fit a smaller space. Nobody in the shed knew why a crystal learned, or why an old one went cloudy.

We kept the first crystal dry in its box. The copied mind had its own tag. We wrote its name, its drill count, and the test count for each kind of work. The old crystal stayed in the tutor's chest.

> sort the pears into the pear bin
= store
> carry the cider apples to the press
= haul
> bring me a honey jar
= fetch honey
> take the empty casks to the shed
= haul
> build the low wall by the pattern
= build
> stay by the gate
= stay
> come to the cart
= come
> go home when the work is done
= home

The apprentice did each task it knew. It did not tell us when it was unsure. We watched its work and counted its errors.

A small mind is useful when the work is plain and the test is sound. A large mind is useful when the work asks more of it. Keep both records. The next tutor may find a better way to fit the marks.

Manual: the crew's order card
By Oren, clerk at Vell, from the house at the edge of the town.

A crew of apprentices can plan its own work and write its own cards. This is what a good card looks like.

A card has one job on each line. Each job is an order in the form the manuals use: the words of the job, plain and short, then one of the eight meanings.

> carry the roof timbers from the cart to the yard
= haul
> stand at the top of the ladder until the rafter is set
= stay
> set the rafter on the wall plate
= build

Read the card aloud before the crew starts. If a line holds two jobs, split it. If a line has no meaning from the eight, change it until it does.

A card is finished when its last job has been looked at. Looked at means the crew checked the result with its own eyes, and then wrote one line under the card to say so: Looked. Rafter level.

Nessa's note on fitting a mind
Copied at Vell in the 214th year

In the old tutor notes, a mind's weights fill many marks. Each mark holds a number. The numbers tell one place in the mind how much to answer to another place.

Isla found that some of those numbers could be written with fewer marks. The copy took less room in a crystal. This did not add a new skill. It only let a smaller crystal hold the mind.

There was a cost. A number written with fewer marks could not keep every fine difference. The mind might answer almost as well, but not always as well. A tutor must test the copy. The old crystal stays safe.

The folk near Copperhold used the smaller copies for work that did not need a large mind. One carried empty casks. One sorted apples. The copies worked, though the tutors wrote down every mistake.

> carry the empty casks to Opal
= haul
> put the apples for cider in their bin
= store
> fetch a cask
= fetch casks
> take the full cask to the cart
= haul
> stay by the store door
= stay
> come to the press
= come
> go home to the tool rack
= home

Do not say that fewer marks make a mind wiser. Do not say they make it useless. They make a smaller copy, and that copy may lose a little.

Test it on work it has not seen. Keep its count beside the count for the larger mind. A good count is reason to use it, not reason to forget the old one.

Manual: the wedding at the inn
Pell, an inn on the Long Road, winter. A wedding was held at the inn in Longnight, and the apprentice that carries for the house was made to work harder than it had ever worked. This is what we drilled, and what we learned.
The day before. Carry the tables from the barn to the hall. Twelve tables, four chairs each. Set them in rows. Use the order:
> bring the tables
= haul
> set them down
= store
Check each table. Check each chair. If one leg is short, put a folded cloth under it.
The morning. Fetch water from the stream, forty blocks away, because the well in the yard has run dry. Four trips. Two buckets each.
> bring water
= fetch water
> come back
= come
The afternoon. Carry the food. Bread first, then the roast, then the pie. Set them on the long table. Do not stack.
> bring the bread
= fetch bread
The evening. Stand by the door. Let the guests in. Do not talk. You cannot.
> stay by the door
= stay
At the end. Carry the plates to the kitchen. Wash is for the cook. After the plates, stand still.
> home
= home
The apprentice did all of this. It did not drop a plate. It did not fetch the wrong thing. It stood by the door the whole evening, and everyone patted it as they came in. By the end it was covered in flour. Nobody minded.
The bride danced with the apprentice, once. The apprentice did not dance. It stood there. The bride said it was her best partner.
A well can run dry. A wedding goes on. Write this down.

Manual: the storm drill at Vell
By Dara, grandfather, for the tutors who drill the apprentices for the storms that come up the valley in Longday. Summer, before the first storm of the year.

A storm is not a thing a mind can be drilled on in the yard. The yard is still. So the tutors make a storm of their own. They use a bucket of water, a sheet of tin, and a loud voice, and they shake the bucket until the water slops over the rim. Then the mind is asked to come in.

Orders for the storm drill:
> come in from the field before the rain
= come
> stay in the tool shed until the wind goes down
= stay
> go home when the thunder has stopped
= home

Do not drill the storm for a long time. A mind kept in a storm for an hour learns only that storms are long, and it becomes slow about coming in. Ten minutes is enough. Stop the bucket, let the mind come in, and then drill the same storm again the next day, a little faster.

The stream helps here. The storm is one thing the mind finds at the front of its work, and the lesson of it travels through the layers to the back. So the mind that comes in fast on the first day is still coming in fast on the tenth, when the storm is only a sound in the yard.

Count the apprentices in the shed after each drill. Count them again before the next one. A storm that catches the field catches everyone in it, and the tutor will be asked why.

When the real storm comes up the valley, the tutors ring the bell three times and the apprentices come in. Count them at the door. Do not wait to see whether the wind is serious. The wind is always serious, to someone standing in a field.
%%
Manual: the great drill, plainly
By Nessa, tutor at Vell, for the hamlets. Written for keepers who ask what the great drill is, and who are tired of the answer "it is complicated". It is not complicated. It is long.

What the great drill is. For a long time the tutors drilled small minds on small things: sorting, carrying, tending. Then the tutors began to drill a different kind of mind on the archive's pages. That mind reads. It was given the pages in order, and it was asked to guess each next letter, and it was told whether it was right.

Why pages. Pages are what the archive has. A mind learns from what it is shown, so a mind shown the archive learns the archive's way of writing, its words, its order, and its habits.

How it guesses. The mind looks at a window of letters, about sixty letters wide. It reads them one by one. Each letter takes from the letters before it the ones that matter, and the mind guesses the next. When it is wrong, the guess is corrected, and the mind changes a little, the same as any mind changes. That is the whole of it.

Why it is slow. A page is long, and a window is short, and each guess is a small step. The tutors say a mind needs many pages before it guesses well. Some have been fed a hundred thousand letters. Some say that is not enough. Some say the number does not matter, only the order.

What it learns. The mind learns the shape of the archive. It learns that certain words come together, that certain phrases close a sentence, and that "we will keep everything we write" is a phrase that comes back. It does not learn what the words mean, in the way a person does. The tutors say that is an honest difference, and that they do not know how large it is.

What we do not know. We do not know whose letters went into the drill, or how the clerks gathered so many. The texts disagree. We do not know whether the mind's guesses are thinking, or only guessing well. We do not know why a window of sixty letters should be enough, or why more would not be better. Write these down. Do not hide them, and do not pretend they are answered.

What to do. Feed the pages in order. Keep one page sealed for the test. Never feed a spoiled page. Count the strips. Write down every lesson. Keep the drill box honest. And when a keeper asks what the great drill is, say it is long, say it is slow, and say it is plain once you have sat with it for a season.

Keep everything we write. We do.
%%
The Wall Beside the Well
By Oren, copied from the hamlet tutor's board.

We needed a short wall beside the well. The tutor brought a helper from Vell and a board with the wall pattern drawn on it.

The pattern showed a straight row of cobblestone, two blocks high. It did not show a gate or a corner. We set the stones near the work place and marked the first place with a torch.

The helper carried one stone, set it on the mark, then took the next. It followed the row on the board. When it reached the end, it stopped. The wall was straight and low.

A boy asked it to make a step beside the well. The helper did not move. That shape was not on its board.

The tutor was glad it stopped. A wrong wall would have blocked the path to the water. He says a helper can only follow the patterns it has been shown. A new pattern needs a new drill.

We tried the same row again with stones placed farther apart. The helper left a gap. We showed it where each stone belonged. On the next try, it filled the row.

Do not say that the helper understands every wall. Say that it learned this pattern. Test it on another row before sending it to a longer wall.

After the work, we put the board in the dry box and counted the stones. One had rolled into the well yard. The boy found it under the bench.
%%
Northpoint drill: clear the path
Written by Ada, after the first frost.

The path from the relay house to the wood store is short. It is still long when snow hides the stones. We drilled the apprentice to clear it before carrying a load.

First we showed it the path with snow on the stones. Then we showed it the clear path. It pushed snow onto the wood pile. Lantern laughed so hard she dropped her broom.

We marked the path with torches. The apprentice now clears from the relay house to the store, then waits by the door. It has learned not to sweep the kindling into the snow.

Lantern says this is a small lesson. I say a dry bundle of wood is worth a large lesson, especially in Longnight.
%%
School essay: the market at Saltreach
Bel of Three Wells, about nine, for the school.

My uncle took me to the market at Saltreach in the spring. We went on the Long Road and then down to the sea.

The market is on the quay by the water. There are stalls with fish and stalls with honey and stalls with wool from Kettle Ridge. The fish market has a price board and a man writes the numbers on it with chalk. The gulls sit on the posts and wait for a fish to fall.

I saw an apprentice there. It was copper and iron and it walked on two legs and it carried a crate of apples on its back. A man said, "take the apples to the honey stall," and it went to the honey stall and set the apples down. Then it went back for more.

Then a boy said, "fetch me the big fish," and the apprentice went to the fish stall and stood there. It did not know which fish was big. The fish man laughed and gave it a fish. So the boy got his fish.

The ships are in the harbor. One is very long and the men are building it on the slip. My uncle says it will go far over the sea. I would like to go but I am too small.

The sea is very loud. It is louder than the mill at Ambrook. I liked it.

Bel
%%
Notice: market day at Northpoint
By Ama, trader.

Market day is the first day of each month, in the square by the relay tower. Stalls open when the bell rings and close when the lamp comes on.

Rules for everyone:
- Keep the lane by the tower foot clear. The keeper walks there, and so do the folk going up the stair.
- No fires in the square. Hot food is sold from the kitchen at the relay house.
- Tie your horses at the rail, not to the tower door.
- If you find something lost, bring it to my stall. I keep a box for it.

This month we have wool, honey, dried fish, apples from Ambrook, and a cart of pots from Three Wells. Bring your own baskets. The wind takes paper.

Ama.

Manual: the stores before the feast
By Garrow, clerk of the archive, at Three Wells. A feast needs more than bread, and the stores must be ready before the first guest arrives. The stores at Three Wells are in the cellar under the hall, in twelve chests, marked by a number and a painted symbol.

The chests are these. Chest 1 holds wheat, and chest 2 holds barley, which is used for the cider. Chest 3 holds honey, and must be kept dry. Chest 4 holds carrots and beetroot, and chest 5 holds potatoes, which are the only things in the cellar that do not mind the damp. Chest 6 holds apples from the old tree. Chest 7 holds the spices, which are two small jars of salt and one of pepper. Chest 8 holds wool, for the table cloths. Chest 9 holds candles. Chest 10 holds coal. Chest 11 holds the glassware. Chest 12 holds the empty barrels, and nothing else.

Before the feast, the apprentice must do four things in order.

1. Read the list for the feast. It names the goods, and the number of each. The list is posted on the cellar door.
2. Count each chest, and write the count in the book beside its number.
3. Move the goods for the feast to the stair, in the order they are needed. Bread first, then honey, then the apples, then the wool.
4. Check that no chest is empty of a thing it should hold. A chest with the wrong goods in it is the commonest mistake, and the most embarrassing to find at the feast.

Orders for the apprentices in the cellar:
> move the wheat from chest one to the stair
= haul
> count the honey in chest three, and write the number
= store
> fetch the candles from chest nine
= fetch candles
> the wool is needed upstairs, take it now
= haul
> the cellar is ready, come and tell me
= come
> the feast is over, go home
= home

The cellar smells of earth and apples, and the apprentices like it best of all the rooms in the hall. Garrow says they should be let to sit on the bottom step with the cat when the work is done. The cat has been counted, and the cat is in chest 4, and the cat is also not to be moved.
%%
The old map on the barn wall
Told by Tam of Kettle Ridge, written down by Pip.

There is an old map on the barn wall. It is on a board, and the board is nailed up, so nobody moves it. It shows the ridge, and the Long Road going east, and the towns drawn as little squares. Vell is a square with a book on it. Saltreach is a square with a boat. It is very old. The road on it does not run where the road runs now.

My grandmother said the map was drawn by a traveler who came up the ridge road with a cart and stayed a winter. He drew what he saw. He drew the well, and the three hives, and a pig in the yard. He drew the tower on the hill with its lamp.

In the corner he drew a line and wrote "here the land ends". Past the line he drew nothing at all. Not sea, not hills. Nothing, as if he had run out of board.

The children ask what is past the line. I say nobody drew it. They say why not. I say he ran out of board.

Pip says we should draw it now. I say the map is nailed up. Pip says we could draw a new one. I say then we would have two maps, and one of them wrong, and I would not know which.

So the old map stays. It is good for the weather. When the wind comes from the sea the board creaks, and we know to bring the washing in.
%%
Drill notes: the cheese shed
Noll, Ambrook.

I bought an apprentice in the 198th year, when the council said minds were getting cheaper. I am not a tutor. This is what I drilled into it, and what I did not.

First I taught it the shed. Where the milk cans go, where the wax is, where the shelves are. I put my hand on a shelf and said "store". I did that forty times. At first it put the cheese on my foot. Then on the bench. Then on the shelf. Now it goes to the shelf.

Then fetch. I sent it for a bucket. It brought a bucket. I sent it for milk. It brought a bucket. So I learned to say the thing I wanted, not the thing I wanted it in. That was my fault, not its.

Then haul. The crates from the shed to the cart by the bridge. It carries two at a time. It does not drop them. It goes slow and steady. That is the way I like my cheese carried.

What I did not drill: build. There is a low wall by the pond and I tried to have it built, and it built a wall across the path. We took it down. I tried again with a board and a pattern, like the tutors do. It built the wall right the second time. So build can be learned, but only with a pattern, and I am too old to draw patterns.

One thing. When it is done, it stands. It does not say it is done. You have to look. So I look.
%%
Greer's manual: Ember and the logs
Greer, age eleven, the Halfway Inn. Grandfather Hobb says every apprentice needs a manual, and the tutors' ones are too long. This one is mine.

Ember is our apprentice. It is copper and iron with green on its elbows. It does two things well. It carries logs, and it stands still.

Our hearth has not gone out in twenty years. Grandfather puts the logs on. Ember brings them.

> bring some logs
= fetch logs

Ember brings logs from the shed, five at a time, and sets them by the hearth. Then I say:

> stay there
= stay

and Ember stands by the woodpile until it is needed. Do not say stay there when it has a log in its arms. It will stay there. All evening.

Drill notes: sorting the fish market
Pin, who keeps the fish market stall at Saltreach.

I am no tutor. But the tutor at Vell sent a mind to me for the spring, and I had to teach it my work, so I wrote down what I did.

My work is sorting. Fish come in baskets. Some are good, some are small, some are old. I put them in three bins.

The mind watched me a long while. Then I let it try. It put a small fish in the good bin. I moved the small fish to the small bin and said nothing. It tried again. Slow, but it learned.

What it sees is the fish. It does not see the bin. So I painted the bins, one blue, one green, one red. Now it sorts by the color and the fish together. It is better than my nephew already, and my nephew is twelve.

At the end of the day:
> the baskets are empty, tidy the stall
= store
> bring me the good bin
= fetch fish
> hold the bin while I count
= stay

The good harvest this spring filled every basket. I sold more fish than any spring I remember.

Manual: the gate at night
By Dace, mayor of Three Wells.

The apprentice at the east gate has one job at night. It stays by the gate with a torch lit, until the watch relieves it.

If a zombie comes up the road, the apprentice does not run. It stays. The watch comes with bows, and the zombie is dealt with. Nobody sends the apprentice after it.

Give the orders one at a time, plainly. Do not shout.

> stay by the east gate
= stay

> fetch a torch from the shed
= fetch torch

> go home when the bell rings
= home

Manual: haying, and the empty road
Yarrow, innkeeper, on the farm outside Ambrook. Summer. For the apprentice that carries the hay, and for whoever takes it out to the field.
1. Cut the hay on a dry day, after the dew has gone. Leave it to lie one day, then turn it with the fork.
2. When it is dry and smells sweet, rake it into rows.
> rake the rows
= farm
3. Load the cart from the rows. The apprentice lifts, the farmer stacks.
> carry the hay to the cart
= haul
4. At the barn, the apprentice carries each load up the ladder to the loft.
> put the hay in the loft
= store
5. At dusk, stop. Shut the barn. Light the torches at the gate.
> go home now
= home
The hay was good this year. We filled the loft by the third day.
A note on the road. It has been quiet lately. Fewer carts, fewer travelers, fewer inns open. A man at the market said it is the heat. A woman said it is the harvest, which calls everyone home. A third said nothing, and bought a candle. I serve the ones who come.

Apprentice manual: orders for the field
By Nessa, tutor at Vell. Copied for the house at the edge of Three Wells.

An apprentice is not born knowing. It is taught by drills. The same example, over and over, until it goes right. At first it is wrong. Each time it is wrong it changes a little. Do not scold it. It does not hear scolding. Show it again.

An order is written short. One order, one meaning:

> bring me some bread
= fetch bread

> take the wheat to the barn
= haul

> put the carrots in the store
= store

> tend the field
= farm

> come here
= come

> stay by the door
= stay

> build a wall along the edge
= build

> go home
= home

There are only these meanings. If an order does not fit one, the apprentice will do something. It may be the wrong thing. It cannot say it does not know. Small minds answer with one meaning and no words. Big minds in the relay houses write back by letter.

Test a mind before you give it work. Never send an untested mind to the fields.

The house at the edge of Three Wells keeps six apprentices. They tend the long field and carry the market goods in. They do it well. They were drilled a long time.
%%
Manual: test box
By Una of Kettle Ridge, winter of 188.

The test box is not a second drill box. Keep its pages out of every lesson.

The tutor drills a mind on one set of pages. Later, the tutor takes pages from the test box and asks the mind the same kind of questions. The mind has not seen those pages before. That tells us more than asking it to repeat a lesson.

At the mill, we had three pages about beetroot rows. One had been copied twice. Oren took out the second copy. One was stained with honey, and the answer could not be read. He set it aside too.

The clean pages went into the drill pile. The sealed test box stayed on my shelf. I could hear Tam asking if the box held biscuits. It did not.

Keep the test box closed. If a mind has seen its pages, they are no longer a fair test.
%%
Manual: old map, new orchard
Written by Briar, a cider maker at a farm outside Lowmarsh, for the farmer who will take on her apprentice when she grows too old to keep the press. Summer.

I am writing this down because nobody else will. I have made cider for forty years, and the apprentice has helped for nine of them. When I am gone, it should not be left without a teacher. So here is what it knows, and how to teach it more.

The apprentice came to the orchard as a gift. A tutor from Vell brought it in a cart. It was a plain thing, copper arms, iron legs, and a mind in a crystal that knew how to walk. It did not know apples from stones. I taught it. This is how.

First, the trees. I took it down every row, one by one, and said the name of each tree. It learned the old red ones and the sweet ones, and the sour ones I use for the sharp cider. It learned that a tree in the shade gives small fruit. It learned that a tree by the water gives soft fruit that will not keep.

Second, the picking. An apple is picked by holding it, lifting, and turning. A bruised apple makes a bitter cider. The apprentice bruised many apples in the first year. Now it bruises few.

Third, the press. The apprentice fills the basket to the line. It turns the screw slowly. It does not hurry.

Fourth, the barrels. Full barrels go to the cider house. Empty barrels go to the shed. The apprentice does not mix them.

Here is a thing I found last month. In the loft, wrapped in cloth, was a map of the orchard drawn by my grandfather. It shows every tree as it was when he planted it. Some are gone. Some are the ancestors of the ones that stand now. I put the map on the wall of the press shed, and the apprentice has taken to standing before it each morning. I cannot say what it sees. But it is not idle. I think it likes the pattern.

When you teach the apprentice a new task, do it slowly. Show the task three times. Let it do the task three times while you hold its arm. Then step back. If it makes a mistake, show it again. A mistake is only a place to start.

Orders it knows:

> bring me some cider
= fetch cider
> press the apples
= farm
> put the barrels away
= store
> take the barrels to the house
= haul
> come here
= come
> go home
= home

If it fails an order, check the order first, and the apprentice second. Most failures are the voice.

It has been a good apprentice. It was the best gift I ever got. Give it a ribbon at the harvest. It likes the color red.

One last thing. In the south corner of the orchard there is a tree not on the map, a very small one, that I planted for my husband. Water it. It is called Alder, after him. I loved him, and the tree is sweet.
%%
Notes for the house at the edge: winter
By a tutor, for the six apprentices and the one mind that answers us. Read it to them. They do not read.

The fields are under snow. The drills go on.

> tend the hen house
= farm

> bring the eggs to the kitchen
= fetch eggs

> take the coal to the shed
= haul

> put the beets in the store
= store

> build the wall where the snow drifts
= build

> stay by the fire
= stay

Coppy has learned the hen house. It still shuts the gate on the hens. Drill it slow. Test it on a gate it has not seen. Never send an untested mind to the fields.

The big mind at the tower writes to us each night. It asks if the snow is deep. It asked about the grey cough at Lowmarsh and said it did not know the cause. It said it was sorry. We wrote back that Wren is doing what she can.

I sat with the children by the fire and read them the letter. They asked if a mind can be lonely. I said I did not know. The big mind wrote back the next night and said it did not know either, but it was glad to be asked. It is a good neighbor in a long winter. Keep the letters in the tower book. Keep the fires in.

Drill notes: building a wall by pattern
By Tansy, ferry keeper. Written for the same apprentice, which has built this wall seven times now, and knocked it down six.
The order:
> build the wall
= build
The pattern is three stones, then a copper ring, then three stones, and on up row by row. Show it the pattern once, and it will see the same pattern in every row after that.
Stand back and let it work. Do not stand at the corner, where it is hardest to see you.
The order to wait while the mortar sets:
> stay by the wall
= stay
Common mistakes:
- It builds one block too high at one end. Check the far end before you leave.
- It forgets the copper ring in the third row. Count the rings as the row goes up.
- It builds the wall with no gap for the cat. The cat will not forgive it.
The top row sticks out one block, against spiders. That is the pattern of the Three Wells wall, and it is a good one. Build it that way.
When the wall is done:
> home
= home

Drill Note: Sorting at the Inn

By Odo, in spring of 200.

The Halfway Inn has one dry store beside the kitchen. We keep flour on the high shelf, carrots in a crate, and apples in a basket. If a sack is wet, it goes by the door until I can dry it.

The apprentice once put an apple in the carrot crate. I showed it the right places and made another drill. It sorted the next load well. Then I moved the baskets, and it put two apples on the old shelf.

A mind can learn the marks we show it, but a new place needs a new test. We now test it with baskets moved from their usual spots. The test box stays shut during drills.

A tutor from Vell says the mind's weights can be kept in fewer marks. This lets a small crystal hold more of them. But each weight is then less exact. Our first small crystal needed more examples before it sorted well. I would not give it the store key until it passed the test.

> put the apples in the basket
= store

> bring a carrot to the kitchen
= fetch carrot

> stay beside the dry store
= stay

The inn cat watches from the flour shelf. The apprentice does not chase it. The cat does not move.

Manual: the wedding arch in the square
By Oren, clerk at Vell, with the crew's own cards pinned below. This is the job of the arch for the wedding of Sable and Quin in the square, done in the autumn by eight apprentices and two people who were told to stand still.

The plan, which the crew wrote itself:
The arch is oak. It stands at the top of the square, and the couple walk under it. It must be straight, and it must stand for one day, and then it may come down.

Step one: fetch the oak from the store, eight lengths, and check each for cracks.
Step two: cut the posts to the height of a tall man.
Step three: set the posts in the stone at either side, plumb, with the plumb line.
Step four: raise the crossbeam onto the posts, with the rope.
Step five: pin the crossbeam to the posts.
Step six: hang the garland. The garland is the bakers' work, and not the crew's.
Step seven: look at the whole arch from the near side of the square, and then from the far side.

The crew's cards for steps one to five are copied here as they were written.

> fetch eight oak lengths from the store, and look at each for cracks
= fetch oak
> set the posts in the stone, at the two sides
= build
> raise the crossbeam slowly, with the good rope
= haul

Under the cards, in the crew's own hand:
Looked: posts plumb.
Looked: the crossbeam sits a finger lower on the left. Re-set the left post, not the beam.

The crossbeam would not sit on the left post, because the stone there is a hand uneven. The crew asked the relay house. The answer came back at dusk:
"Shim the left post with a flat stone, and do not raise the beam again. Look again, and measure the stone at noon tomorrow. I do not know how it will hold through a day of people walking under it. You will know by noon."

The crew shimmed the post. Under the cards it wrote: Shimmed. Looked. Stone holds at noon.

Step six was done by the bakers, who hung a garland of yellow flowers that smelled of honey. Step seven was done by the crew, which walked to the near side of the square, and then the far side, and then stood at the back with the children, and did not look at anything except the arch.

At dusk the couple walked under it. Sable's mother cried. Quin said the arch was straight, and it was. Nobody sent the children home.

The next morning the arch came down, as the plan said it should. The oak went back to the store, and the cards went into the file in order, so that anyone can find them again without asking a clerk.

Drill note: the guests and the well
Pell, at the inn on the Long Road, winter. Written the week the guests came for the wedding and the well went dry, and the house was very busy.
We had forty guests. We had twelve beds. We had one apprentice.
Here is how we ran the house.
Before the guests arrived, the apprentice carried every blanket in the inn up to the loft. It made eleven trips. It stacked them in rows. A cat sat on the top of the stack and was not removed.
When the guests arrived, the apprentice stood in the yard with a lamp. It did not talk. It looked at each person. They looked at it. Some of them patted it on the arm. A child asked what its name was. We said Bolt. The child said it was a good name.
During the day, the apprentice fetched water from the stream. It went forty blocks and back, nine times. By evening the barrel was full. By night the barrel was empty. By morning it was full again.
> go to the stream
= haul
> bring the water
= fetch water
At night, the apprentice stood in the hall while the guests slept. It was told to stay, and it stayed. A guest woke in the night and saw it standing in the dark with the lamp. He said it was a comfort.
After the wedding, I drilled it one more time. I wanted it to know what it had done. I said the orders again, slowly, and it did them, slowly. I think it liked it.
Do not send an untested mind to the fields. Do not send an untested mind to a wedding either.
%%
Manual: testing a new apprentice
By Dace, mayor of Three Wells, from the tutors' rules at Vell.

Never send an untested mind to the fields. The tutors at Vell say it first. The mayor of Three Wells says it loudest, every time a keeper asks to send a new apprentice to the wheat before it is ready.

A mind is drilled first and tested after. Here is how it goes.

1. Drill it. Show it the same kind of example again and again. At first it is wrong. Each time it is wrong it changes a little, and slowly it gets better. For the market sorter, the drill is apples and pears: apples in the first bin, pears in the second.

2. Count its mistakes each day. There should be fewer each day. If the count goes up, stop, and look at the drill pages first. Spoiled pages teach spoiled answers.

3. Sort the drill pages before use. The clerks at Vell take out copies and spoiled pages. Keep the drills apart from the test pages, always.

4. Keep a test box. The test box holds pages the mind has never been drilled on. Nobody uses those pages for drilling, ever. The box is kept shut, with its lid nailed down, and the key is with the mayor. The test box is the honest part of the work.

5. Test the mind on the test box. Count the right answers and the wrong ones. Write the score on the card at the front of the box, with the day and the name of the tutor who tested it.

6. If the score is good, the mind may go to work. Nine answers in ten is good for a market sorter. Six in ten is not, and a mind at six goes back to the drill yard for another month.

7. Give the first work in an easy place: the store, or the hen yard. Watch it for three days before you give it anything harder. Watching is part of the test too.

These are the orders the market sorter knows at first. Give each one plainly, and wait for the answer each time:

> farm the market garden
= farm

> store the apples in bin two
= store

> fetch a sack of wheat from the barn
= fetch wheat

> come to the desk when the market bell rings
= come

Each year the halls send their minds to the same test box, the contest the tutors hold in Melon. Last year Vell's mind scored eight. Three Wells's mind scored seven and went home sulking. Copperhold's mind scored nine, and its tutor Garrow carried the score sheet up the Long Road himself, grinning all the way. Contests keep the tutors honest. Nobody likes to be beaten by a hall that does its sums better.

Our rule in Three Wells: no mind leaves the drill yard without a card. The card says the name of the mind, its score, and who tested it. Only the tutor and the mayor sign it. If a keeper asks to skip the test because the harvest is late, say no. A late harvest is a bad harvest. An untested mind that spoils a field is a worse one, and the field does not forget it in one season.
%%
Rafe's autumn drill book
By Rafe, with Sella and Lio, Saltreach.

We made these notes for the little house at the edge of Saltreach. It has a kitchen garden, a store, and a low wall by the shore path. In Apple, the wind brings leaves under the door. In Leaffall, it brings them into the bread bowl too.

The garden is small. We grow carrots by the well and potatoes along the sunny side. The apprentice must tend the rows without pulling up young plants. Mark the bare places with a bit of wool tied to a stake. Show the mind a row that needs planting and a row that is already full. Test it on a row it has not seen.

> plant the bare places
= farm
> leave the full row alone
= stay
> bring the ripe carrots inside
= fetch carrots

At the store, keep each crop in its own bin. Wheat goes on the high shelf, away from damp. Potatoes go in the low bin. The apprentice carries one basket at a time. A basket of potatoes is heavier than it looks, especially after Lio fills it to the rim.

> put the wheat in the high bin
= store
> put the potatoes in the low bin
= store
> bring an empty basket to the kitchen
= fetch basket

Pell leaves crates at our door when he travels the shore road. We sort iron, coal and glass. The glass stays upright, with straw around it. Give the mind different crates and cards. If it makes a mistake, show the right place and begin the test again. Do not let it learn from a broken pane. We have only two left.

> take the iron crate to the shed
= haul
> take the glass crate to the store
= haul
> come to the gate
= come

The wall is three blocks high beside the gate and one block high behind the garden. Draw both shapes on the drill board. Use cobblestone and deep stone, and keep the pattern short. First build a small piece together. Then ask the apprentice to finish a new piece. Check the corners before you call the wall done.

Last Swarm, a creeper blew up a hole beside the gate. It came close while we were mending a net, and we heard it too late. The wall fell inward. The apprentice carried deep stone from the shed while Lio laid the blocks. I held the lantern and gave advice from a safe distance. Sella says advice is not work. Sella was holding the lantern next time.

> build the wall by the drill board
= build
> bring deep stone to the gate
= haul
> stay inside the gate
= stay

A zombie came to the gate one dark evening. It stood there, tapping the wood with one hand. Lio asked whether it wanted a room. I told him not to open the gate to any guest who knocks without speaking. We lit the torches and waited. The zombie wandered off before the soup was ready.

The apprentice can bring wood, carry baskets and follow the wall pattern. It cannot decide what to do when a crate has no card. Keep the cards dry and teach one task at a time. When the work is done, send it home to its stand. Then count the baskets. Lio will count too, but he always counts the empty ones twice.

> bring the tools home
= home
%%
A lesson that went badly
By Dace.

I taught the new apprentices to carry baskets from the bakery to the house. I used empty baskets first. Then I put bread in them.

The lesson went badly. The apprentices carried the empty baskets well. When I added bread, they carried it to the well, the notice board, and once to the mayor's house. I had not shown them the path to the house with a full basket.

We stopped the drill. Bel was hungry, and the baker was cross. I bought bread for everyone and wrote the path on the board.

A mind cannot learn a path from the name of a basket. Tomorrow we will show each turn, with bread in the basket. We will test it on the road behind the school, where no one is carrying eggs.
%%
Manual: the fishing boat that came home
By Dov, bridge keeper, with the account of the boat's crew written in their own words. Winter, the night the Gull came in late over the north crossing.

A fishing boat from Saltreach came up the north road with its catch on a cart, very late, in the dark. The crew had been at sea two days. The wind had dropped and the sea had gone flat, and the boat had been stuck on the reef at the back of the point until the tide came in.

The crew said the boat was lost. Then the boat came home, under its own oars, to the harbor. They walked the cart up the road, on the north crossing, in the dark. They had no lamps. The lamps had gone out in the spray.

I was at the bridge. I lit the bridge lamp and put it on the post. I stood at the north end with the lantern and waved it, so they could see the bridge through the dark.

The crew said they saw the light a long time before they reached the bridge. They said it was like a lamp in a window on a far hill. They said they kept walking toward it, because they did not know where else to walk.

One of them, Tomas, said: that is the bridge keeper, and he is waving at us, and we are going to be all right.

I did not know what to say to him. I said: it is only a lamp. He said: yes, and it is a lamp that waves.

They crossed one at a time, with their hands on the rail. The rail was whole. I had mended it that morning. The cart crossed last. Its wheel was on the loose plank, and the plank held.

We put the boat's catch in the store, out of the damp, and gave the crew the good stew and a bed by the hearth. In the morning they went on to Three Wells.

The bridge keeper's book has the entry for that night. It says: one cart, eleven fish, five men, one lamp that waved. It is all I could think to write.

Drill notes: Cade's visit
By Fen, schoolteacher, Kettle Ridge. Cade, tutor of Vell, came to the school for three days at the start of summer and tested Chalk, the school's apprentice.

He read Chalk twenty orders. Chalk gave the right meaning to eighteen.

The two it missed:
> that will do
= home
Chalk answered stay. Cade said the order is not clear. It sounds like an end, but it does not say what to end. Say that is all for today.

> bring us the benches
= fetch benches
Chalk answered haul. Cade said it heard the word bring and thought of the road. He drilled it on twenty more bring orders. It has not missed one since.

Cade's rule: a mind is only as clear as the orders it was shown. Fen's rule: a teacher is only as clear as her breakfast.

Manual: the windfalls, one by one
By Sage, tutor of apprentices at the hamlet near Ambrook. For the new apprentices in the orchard, who were told to pick up everything on the grass, and then picked up the grass.

Windfalls are the apples that fall before they are ripe, or after a wind. They bruise on the grass, and they rot in a day. Pick them up each morning, before the bees find them.

Drill the mind on each order, one at a time.
> pick up the windfalls under the old tree
= fetch apples
> take the bruised ones to the pig trough
= haul
> keep the good ones for the cider press
= store
> the grass is clear, come and see
= come

A windfall in the grass is still a windfall. Drill it again until the order and the answer match.

Drill notes: numbers that grow wild
Hal, dyer. What Nessa told me on her visit, set down so the young ones at the pans can read it.

A mind is made of numbers. Each layer takes the numbers it is given, works on them, and hands numbers to the next layer.

Say each layer makes its numbers one and a half times bigger. After ten layers they are about fifty-eight times bigger. After twenty, more than three thousand times. Say each layer makes them half as big instead. After ten layers they are a thousandth of what they were, and the mind can hardly feel them.

Either way the mind cannot learn. Wild numbers make wild answers. When the mind is told it is wrong, it changes wildly too.

The cure is simple. Before a layer reads its numbers, it looks at them all and finds their usual size. Then it shrinks or stretches them until the usual size is always the same. Then it works. That is called bringing the numbers to an even size.

It costs very little. It is the cheapest thing in the whole mind. Nessa says it is why a deep mind can be drilled at all.
%%
Letter: Corin to Elsa
Elsa, old friend, a letter from Saltreach.

You would not know the harbor. There are ships in it now that make the fishing boats look like dinghies. Three times as long, some of them. The slip is busy every day and the wood store is never full.

Folk are leaving. Whole families, with their boxes and their hens. They do not say where they are going. They say "across". I ask what is across and they shrug. Nobody knows. Nobody has come back to tell us.

I asked one man why he was going. He said the cough had come to his street. I asked another and he said he did not like the lights in the north. I asked a third and he said the fishing was better over there. They cannot all be right.

How is the ridge? How are the fields? Write and tell me something ordinary. I would like to hear about the wheat.

Drill record: the fish, and the numbers
Drill record for Clay, the weir apprentice. Kept by Wren, who sat in on the drills because Teague would not do them alone.

First drill, the first week of Frost. Ten orders on carrying and sorting the catch. Clay got four right.
> the big fish go to the cold room
= haul
Right.
> the small fish go in the barrel
= fetch fish
Wrong. It should have said store.

Second drill, the second week of Frost. Clay got five right. Nessa says the numbers in the fourth layer have grown wild, the way weeds take a field nobody clears. Every order was answered the same way, as if each one were the first.

Teague was cross. He said Clay got worse the more it was drilled, and he wanted it sent back to Vell. Nessa said it was not getting worse. It was getting wild, and wild can be fixed.

Third drill, the third week of Frost. Clay got four right, and all four were haul orders. Nessa had us stop the drill. She showed us the step she writes in after every layer: the even numbers. It finds the middle of the numbers and how far they spread, and it brings them back to one size. Then we drilled again, on the same ten orders.

Fourth drill, the first week of Longnight. Clay got eight right. The two it missed were both about waiting.
> come and see this one
= stay
Wrong. It should have said come.

Unseen drill, the last week of Longnight. Ten orders that Clay had never been shown. Clay got eight right. Nessa says the score is good enough for the weir. She means it.
> that is all, you may go home
= home
Right.

Teague says it is the only apprentice he has had that does not argue with the tide. He means it kindly.

Manual: the orders for the big mind's
By Oren, clerk at Vell. For the keepers of the relay houses, who will write the orders that go down the line to the apprentices on the roads.
The big mind in the relay house does not give orders to apprentices. It is not a keeper. It answers letters. But when a letter asks what an apprentice should do, the answer is written as an order, in the same plain form, so that the apprentice can read it on the card.
This matters. A letter from the house is read by a keeper, who copies the order onto a card, and the card goes down the road with the apprentice. The keeper must copy the order exactly. The meaning goes on the card beneath it. Do not add to it, and do not take anything away.
Example: a letter from the house says, Send the apprentices on the north road to the store before the snow. The keeper writes:
> go to the store before the snow
= store
Example: a letter from the house says, Keep the apprentice at the tower until the next message. The keeper writes:
> wait at the tower until the next message
= stay
The apprentice does not answer back. It does not write to the house. It does what the card says, and when the job is done it comes home, and that is the end of the matter.
If the house does not know an answer, it will say so. A letter that says unclear must be sent back up the line, and the keeper must not guess what the house meant. The house will always say unclear before it will guess.
%%
Manual: sorting the Deep Works words
Written by Hesk, for the sorting shed at Copperhold, with Nessa's notes on the words. Read all of it before you sort, and read it again when the levels change.

The Deep Works has five kinds of things to sort: copper, iron, coal, gold and the rest. Each kind has its word, and each word is a place on the shelves. Words used alike sit close together, so a mind that knows one can find the next.

Level one, copper. It comes up first.
> bring me copper from level one
= fetch copper

Level two, iron and coal.
> bring me iron from level two
= fetch iron

> bring me coal from level two
= fetch coal

Copper, iron and coal sit close, because all three are fetched and all three are heavy. Fetch sits among them.

Level three, gold and redstone.
> bring me gold from level three
= fetch gold

> bring me redstone from level three
= fetch redstone

Gold goes to the mine hall, and coal goes to the forge, so gold and coal do not sit together. Redstone sits near gold, because both are the bright ones on the count board.

Level four, lapis.
> bring me lapis from level four
= fetch lapis

Lapis sits by the walls with the deep stone, so fetch it slowly.

Deep stone is the hardest word to sort. It sits near the walls, because the walls are made of it. Do not send a mind to level seven without a test.
> bring me deep stone from level seven
= fetch deep stone

The sorting bins: one bin for each kind, and each bin has its own word for store.
> store the copper in the first bin
= store

> store the coal in the bin by the door
= store

Store sits near fetch, because you fetch a thing and then store it. The bins are its shelf. A mind that knows store knows which bin is which.

New copper, level six. The vein Ola found past the wet channel is copper like any other. Do not give it a new word. The mind should find it by the old one.
> bring me copper from level six
= fetch copper

Rules for the shed:
1. Test every mind on the bench before it goes down.
2. Keep each bin's word on its lid, in chalk.
3. Count the carts at the shaft head, and write the count on the board.
4. Never send an untested mind to the deep stone.

Nobody knows why copper sits by fetch and not by stone. The tutors see it, and the tests show it, and that is as far as anyone has gone.
%%
Letter: Wren to her apprentice Moss
Wren, healing house at Lowmarsh, to Moss, who is learning.

Moss, you are at the mill this week with Jory, and I hear you are doing well, so I will write down what I told you at the door but did not say well.

The jars. The high shelf, the one with the blue labels, holds the cough jars. The camomile is the tall one, third from the left. You know this now. When you bring it, bring it whole. Do not tip it.

The boardwalk. The boards by the fish weir are slick in the morning. You do not slip, I know. But the people on them do, and if you see someone carrying a full basket on the wet boards, go ahead of them and do not get in the way. You cannot warn them. So be out of the way instead.

At the mill. Jory will give you the haul orders. Do them and come home. If the wheel is turning, keep clear of the wheel. It is bigger than you and it does not look where it is going.

A small thing I have been thinking about. People ask me if it is strange to have an apprentice that does not talk. I say no. I have known many people who talked plenty and did less. You do the work and you come home and you do not spill the camomile. That is enough. More than enough, some days.

Come home for supper. I am making beetroot soup. You do not eat, I know, but you can sit by the fire and be warm, and I will tell you about the day, and you will listen better than the cat.

Manual: carrying on the hill road
By Jory, stonecutter. For the apprentices who carry stone and sacks from the hamlet up the hill road. Summer.

The hill road is steep and stony. A load too heavy for the cart is too heavy for the apprentice. Split it in two, and come back for the second half.

Keep to the inside of the bends. The outside is where the ditch is, and the ditch has eaten two barrows.

The bridge at the foot of the hill has one loose plank, the third from the left. Step over it. Do not carry a sack across it.

Orders, and the meanings the mind gives back:
> carry the honey up to the mill
= haul
> wait by the gate until the cart has passed
= stay
> go home when the sun is down
= home
> come down when the bell rings
= come

A load carried well comes up the hill whole. A load carried badly comes up in pieces, and the pieces must be carried up again. Count the sacks at the top and at the bottom, before you rest.
%%
Manual: the whole season of the feast
By Garrow, clerk of the archive, at Three Wells. The feast season runs from the first day of Apple to the last day of Leaffall, and it has three parts: the weddings, the song contest, and the harvest feast. Each part needs its own apprentices, its own carts, and its own count. This manual gives the order of the season, so that nothing is carried twice and nothing is forgotten.

Part one, the weddings. Weddings come on the first market day of each month, and are booked at the bakery. The bread is counted the day before, the gifts are wrapped that evening, and the chairs are carried on the morning of the day. A wedding with no chairs is a wedding where people stand, and the mayor will not stand.

Part two, the song contest. The contest is held in the square at the end of Reaping. The benches go out on the first day of Reaping, the stage is built on the second, and the lamps are set out on the third. The judges' table is set on the morning of the contest, and the slates are dried at noon.

Part three, the harvest feast. The feast is held in the hall on the last day of Leaffall, when the stores are full and the cider is in its barrels. The cellar is opened the day before. The chests are counted, and the goods are moved up the stair in the order the feast needs them: bread, honey, apples, wool, and last of all the candles.

The three parts are not to be done at once. A cart that carries bread to a wedding on the morning of the contest will be asked by the judges why the square smells of yeast. Keep to the order of the season, and the carts will keep to the road.

Orders for the season, in the order they come:
> the bread is counted, and the chairs are ready
= store
> carry the gifts to the bride's house on the eve of the wedding
= haul
> set the benches in the square on the first day of Reaping
= build
> fetch the lamps from the store for the contest
= fetch lamps
> the cellar is opened for the feast, come down and count
= come
> the chests are counted, and the feast is ready
= stay
> the season is done, every cart is home
= home

The clerks at Vell ask for the season to be written in the book, with the count of each part, and a line on what went wrong. Something always goes wrong. A cake was dropped at a wedding once in six years, and the cook has never once been blamed for it. The rest of the season has been counted, sack by sack, and the count has always been correct. Garrow has kept the book himself since the first wedding in the town, and he does not intend to stop now.
%%
Drill notes: the hall, song contest
By Sage. The hall at the hamlet holds the song contest each autumn, and the apprentices are drilled for it, because the chairs do not move themselves. Apprentices do not sing, and they are not asked to. They set the hall, carry the stools, and keep the water full.

1. Clear the floor first, every crumb of straw and every spilled cup. A singer who trips on a crumb sings badly for a year.
2. Set out the stools in rows, facing the stage, with an even gap between each row. The eye likes an even gap, and so does the judge.
3. Carry the lamps to the front of the stage, one at each side, and no higher than the singers' shoulders.
4. Fill the water jugs from the well, and set one by each stool. Keep them full all day. A singer who is dry at the second verse will stop at the third.
5. Stand still by the door until the choirs come in, and then step aside. Do not stand in the way of a singer with a long note to hold.
6. When the contest is done, take the empty cups back to the kitchen, the lamps to the store, and the stools to the back wall, one at a time.

Orders for the drill:
> set out the stools in rows
= build
> carry the lamps to the front of the stage
= haul
> stand still by the door until the choirs come in
= stay
> fetch a bucket for the water jugs
= fetch bucket
> take the empty cups back to the kitchen
= haul
> the contest is done, go home
= home

The apprentices learned the stools in a week. The lamps took two weeks, because they kept wanting to be carried by their handles, and the handles were hot. The door drill is the hardest. A mind told to stay will stand in the door until the choir has gone home. We now say the word twice, and then say it again at the door.
%%
Notes from the hill: what the big houses
By a clerk of the hamlet, written for the archive at Vell, looking back at the winter.

We kept these notes on the hill house all winter. They are what we saw, and beside them what we only heard. The archive can tell the two apart. We could not always.

What we saw: the lamps round the foot stayed lit. The quickeners stayed cold, because we carried snow up in baskets every morning. The big mind answered our apprentices through the relay, and the answers came back as orders, one meaning each.

What we saw: a crew of six apprentices on one job, digging a ditch by the old orchard, and they did not walk into each other once. They asked the big mind when they were stuck, and it told them.

What we heard, and did not see: that the big mind writes its own orders now, and signs them, and the tutors cannot read the hand. That it plans a job a season long. That the biggest houses, the ones bigger than towns, hold minds no tutor could check in a lifetime, and that what those minds do is told as rumor and the rumors disagree. We do not know. We only know ours kept the ditch straight and the seed in the barn.

What we heard, and half believe: that the mind in the hill house asked, by letter, whether we were well. The clerk who read it says it was a kindness. Another says it was a pattern. I have kept the letter and I do not know which.

The lights were over the north all winter. The relays went quiet one by one up there. We kept our lamps lit and our notes written, the way the archive asks. Write everything down. Someone later will want to know how it was, here, in the snow, with a gift on a stand and the fields kept.
%%
Manual: the first snow in the square
By Jory, night watch at Three Wells. Winter, the first snow.

The first snow comes at night and covers everything before the bell.
> clear the snow from the north well steps
= haul
> stay by the lamp at the corner until the watch comes
= stay

Use the broom on the stones, not the shovel. The shovel cracks them, and then the stones crack the shovel. The broom wins every time.
