Manual: the spiders of the north
By Dov, bridge keeper, for the young keepers. Winter, when the snow came early and the barn was closed.

The barn at the north crossing has a store on its top floor. The store is for the grain, the salt, the rope and the spare pins for the bridge. Last winter it was full of spiders.

They came up from the stream, we think, when the water was high. They spun webs across every beam and every doorway. The webs were full of moths. The moths were full of holes in the sacks. So the spiders are useful, the children say, and the children are right.

We put an apprentice in the store, to sort the grain. It is a small mind, from the tutors' cart, and it has been tried on sorting. It sorts the grain well, and it sorts the rope and the pins.

The first morning it was in the store, the spiders came down to see it. They walked over its arms and along its frame. The mind stopped. It did not move. It was not told to move. It stood still, the way it was taught to stand when it does not know what to do.

I went up and took the spider off its shoulder with a stick. The mind did not move until I told it to. Then it went back to the sacks and sorted the grain.

I do not think it was frightened. I think it did not know what to do with the spiders, so it did nothing, and that was the nearest thing it knew. I have written this in the book. Next winter we will drill it on spiders, a little, and see.

The grain was sorted. The moths were fewer. The spiders kept the door, and the mind kept the grain, and nobody swept anything down.
%%
Guide: dyeing with an apprentice
By Hal, dyer, at the salt pans near Saltreach.

Bobbin came to the pans three years ago. It carries wool, hangs skeins on the line and sorts the dyed wool by color. This is how we work, for any dyer who wants an apprentice at the vats.

What to say. Plain words, one order at a time. These are the orders Bobbin knows:

> take the red wool down to the drying line
= haul
> fetch me some cornflowers
= fetch cornflowers
> bring me some water
= fetch water
> put the skeins on the shelf by color
= store
> wait by the vat
= stay
> come and look at this
= come
> that is all for today
= home

What not to ask. Bobbin does not stir the vats and does not test them. A vat needs a dyer's eye, and an eye cannot be drilled. Stirring is mine.

Care. The sea air is hard on copper and harder on iron. Wipe Bobbin down with oil every evening, joints first. Keep it out of the rinse trough. A dyed apprentice is a funny sight, but the dye does not come off the copper. Bobbin is faintly blue at the elbow, and always will be.

The vats. Add a handful of salt from the pans to every vat, to make the color hold. Before every dip, test the vat on a white scrap. Too dark: water. Too pale: dye. I keep each vat at an even strength, so the wool is the color I meant. Nessa says a mind does the same. Every layer brings its numbers back to an even size before it reads them. I forgot this once, in Bobbin's first mind, and it hauled when it was told to stay. Now it has a mended mind and I have a lesson.

The fair. Take the wool, keep the apprentice, and keep both away from the hives. At the autumn fair a late swarm settled on my red wool. Ama says a late swarm is a confused swarm. Bobbin, which knows only haul, picked up the whole skein, bees and all, and carried it to the cart. Ama came running. Nobody was stung. Ama says bees respect a thing that does not hurry.

When it goes wrong. If Bobbin answers one thing to every order, do not argue with it and do not drill it harder. Take it to the tutor house. Nine times in ten the fault is in the numbers, and the tutor will know.

At the end of the day tell it home. It will go and stand by the door. Leave the lamp lit for it.
%%
Manual: carrying in the snow
By Bel, for the apprentices who carry from the store to the house, and from the house to the barn. Winter, in the deep snow, when the paths are narrow and the cart is not coming.

The snow has made a path from the store to the house, and a path from the house to the barn. Keep to the paths. A load carried off the path is a load in the snow, and it takes a whole morning to find.

Orders for the snow:
> carry the sack of flour from the store to the house
= haul
> carry the wood from the pile by the wall to the stove
= haul
> fetch the coal from the bin by the barn
= fetch coal
> stay in the barn until the snow is cleared from the door
= stay

The store has been counted. Flour, enough for the winter if the bread is not made too thick. Coal, enough for the stove if the barn is not kept too warm. Apples, a few, in the cool shelf, for the children at the midwinter fire.

The carts from Vell have not come since Longnight. The tutors' last letter said the road was closed at the bridge and the bridge was being mended. That was a month ago. The apprentices carry what is in the store, and the store is kept in order, and that is what there is to do.

Some nights, from the west window, the sky to the north is very bright, brighter than the moon, and it moves. The apprentices do not look at it. They are told to look at the path, and they look at the path. I write it here so that someone who reads this later knows that the apprentices were told to look at the path, and that they did.

Count the loads at the door. Every load carried is written on the slate by the door. A load that is written is a load that has been done, and that is all anyone can ask of a load in the snow.

Drill Note: Raising a Wall
By Dace, mayor of Three Wells, for the hamlet crews.

The lane beside the hamlet school floods in spring. The wall keeps mud from the door and gives the children a dry place to leave their boots. It is not a place to leave beetroot.

Before building, mark the line with stakes. Check that the door still opens and that the ditch still carries water away. Do not build across the ditch. A wall that keeps the mud inside the school has not helped.

Make a small pattern on the ground. Lay cobblestone in two rows, then deep stone where the ground is soft. Keep the top level. A leaning wall is not a bench, even if children sit on it.

Teach the helper with a short wall first. Place the stones in order and show the pattern card. If one stone is wrong, stop and point to the place. Let the helper remove it and try again. Do not pull down the whole wall for one bad stone. That is how the crew loses its dinner break.

When the short wall is sound, test the helper on a fresh line. Give it the same pattern, but do not let it watch the drill again. Check every corner and every stone before the crew leaves.

> build a wall by the pattern
= build

> bring me cobblestone
= fetch cobblestone

> stay by the wall
= stay

The first spring wall was built with one stone standing on end. It was a fine stone and a poor wall. We used it beside the school door as a boot scraper. The second wall is still standing.

Every crew must mark its work on the school board. The children may draw on the spare side, but not on the pattern. Last year they drew a pig with six legs. It looked more steady than the wall.
%%
Manual: tending the reed beds
By Wren, healer at Lowmarsh.

Moss asked me to write down what I tell him about the reeds, so he can drill his apprentice on it. Here it is, in order.

The reeds grow in slow water. They do not like fast water. Walk the boardwalk at dawn and look at the beds. If the water is low, the reeds lean. If the water is high, the frogs leave. You want the water steady.

Cut the reeds with a good blade. Cut high, above the water line. Leave the roots. A bed cut too low will not come back until next spring.

Bundle the reeds. Tie them with a strip of reed, not with wool. Wool rots in the wet.

Carry the bundles to the drying shed. Stack them on the racks, not on the floor. The floor is mud. The mud is where the frogs live, and the frogs are not the problem. The mold is the problem.

Sort the reeds. Long reeds go to the roof menders. Short reeds go to the basket makers. Broken reeds go to the fire.

Orders you may give an apprentice in the marsh:
> go and cut the reeds in the north bed
= farm
> bring me a bundle of reeds
= fetch reeds
> put the bundles on the racks to dry
= store
> take these bundles to the basket maker
= haul
> stay by the boat house until I come back
= stay
> that is enough for today, go to the shed
= home

Drill the apprentice on the difference between haul and fetch. Fetch is to bring a thing to me. Haul is to take a thing somewhere else. An apprentice that confuses them will bring me a boat load of reeds when I wanted them taken to the basket maker. I have seen it. It was a long morning.
%%
Drill note: the thin stew
Sim, shepherd, winter, an inn on the Long Road. I tried a recipe and it went wrong, and the inn laughed, and the apprentice was the only one who did not.
The recipe was for a stew of lamb and barley, from a book that Marda lent me. I did not read it through. It said "one handful of salt". I took one handful, and then another, because the first looked small. The stew was salt as the sea. Hobb tasted it and asked for water. There was no water. The well was dry.
The apprentice, Burr, carried the stew out to the yard and set it down by the pig. The pig looked at it and walked away. I have never seen that.
I use the story to teach a thing about drills. A drill is a recipe. If you do not follow it, the thing goes wrong. If you follow it too much, it goes wrong another way. The apprentice learns from being wrong, a little at a time, and so does the cook.
Here is what I drilled Burr after the stew:
> bring me some water
= fetch water
> go to the stream
= haul
> pour it in the pot
= store
It went to the stream. It filled two buckets. It poured them in the pot. I stirred. The stew was better. It was not good. But it was less wrong.
Do not use a handful of salt. Use a pinch.
Test the stew on someone you like, not on someone you owe.
%%
Manual: keeping the forge hot
By Sable, smith at the Brown Hen. Copied out for the helpers, so no one can say they were not told.

A forge goes out if you let it. Bank the coal at night, with the ash on top, and in the morning the fire will still be alive. If you find it dead, do not wave a burning stick at the pile. Come and get me. I would rather be woken than see a barn burn.

Ingots come next. Stack the small ones in the top chest and the big ones on the floor by the yard door, biggest at the bottom, so the stack does not fall on the cart. A falling ingot is the one thing in this smithy I cannot forgive.

Learn these orders for the morning by heart.

> bring the coal from the shed
= fetch coal
> keep the fire banked until noon
= stay
> put the small ingots in the top chest
= store
> bring me the big ingots from the yard
= fetch iron ingots
> take the cinders out to the pit
= haul
> come to the forge when you hear the bell
= come

The iron that is hot is not finished. Let it cool before you touch it. A helper that is cold is not broken. Warm it by the wall, and tell me, and I will come.

Manual: Sim's flock drill, winter
Sim, a shepherd, at an inn on the Long Road, winter. He keeps a small flock on the hill behind and an apprentice named Burr that walks with them. This is what he drilled it, so that another shepherd may do the same.
The flock is thirty-one sheep. In winter they stay near the barn, and the work is to count them, feed them, and keep the wolves off. The apprentice counts. It does not tell me. It does, in its way: when one is missing, it stops at the gate and will not shut it.
The orders:
> bring the sheep home
= haul
> feed the sheep
= farm
> stay with the sheep
= stay
> go to the barn
= home
Drill it at dusk, when the sheep are tired. They go slowly and the apprentice learns to match them. Do not rush. A sheep that is hurried will bolt, and an apprentice that is hurried will drop its lamp.
The well behind the barn ran dry this winter. I do not know why. The sheep go down to the stream now, and Burr goes with them. It is a long cold walk, but the sheep seem to like it.
The people at the inn say the stones on the hill are warm. They say the stream runs strange. They say a good many things. I count the sheep.
Thirty-one tonight. Thirty-one tomorrow.
%%
Manual: the cider press, by the order
By Sage, tutor of apprentices. Cider is made in autumn, when the apples are sweet and the days are short. A good press is worth three strong men, and it does not complain about the cold.

1. Wash the apples in the trough. Throw out the bruised ones, the grey ones, and the ones with worms in them.
2. Crush the apples in the crusher, a basket at a time, until they are a brown mash.
3. Fill the cloth squares with the mash, fold them, and stack them in the press.
4. Turn the screw slowly. The juice runs out into the trough, and the first juice is the sweetest.
5. Pour the cider into the barrels and seal them with wood and clay.
6. Carry the barrels down to the cellar, and set them on stones, away from the damp.

Orders for the apprentices who help:
> carry the sacks of apples to the press
= haul
> stand by the press and wait for the mash
= stay
> the press is full, come and turn the screw
= come
> fill the barrels and take them to the cellar
= store
> fetch the wood for the barrel lids
= fetch logs
> the cider is ready, go home
= home

The first barrel of the year goes to the mill, because Jory says it is owed. Nobody has ever checked what he owes, and nobody has asked him to stop.

Manual: Hay at the Halfway Inn

By Odo, keeper of the Halfway Inn.

In spring, the road brings mud into every room. We need hay for the horses, and we need it dry.

> bring hay to the stable
= fetch hay

> take the hay from the cart to the loft
= haul

> stay by the stable door
= stay

The apprentice carries each bundle from the cart. I stack the hay in the loft. Do not ask it to stack. We did not teach it that.

We lost a brown goat near the inn last Blossom. It had eaten the corner of our notice board. We found it by the bridge, eating a traveler's map.

Notice: the gift of a mind
Put up at the notice board by the Kettle Ridge well, winter.

The council has given our hamlet a mind for the new relay house on the hill. It comes in a crystal the size of a loaf, on a stand, with its quickeners. It will answer our apprentices through the relays, the way the big towns do.

It is a gift and it is also a puzzle. The tutors say it is bigger than any one of them can check alone. So we check it together, the way we check a bull or a new roof. Everyone who uses it writes down what it did and what it got wrong, and we read the notes at the inn on the first of each month.

The council asks one thing of us. Keep it cold. Snow is free here. Ice from the pond is free here. A warm quickener works at half pace and smokes, and we did not get a gift to watch it smoke.

The apprentices are told to keep working, the fields and the stores, until the weather turns. If the big mind tells them something strange, they bring the order card to a person first. We will sort it out together.

Signed for the hamlet, Tam, and Pip, and Pip.

By Tansy, ferry keeper, for the apprentice on loan from the mine.
The roof lets the rain in over the bed. Here is how it is mended.
Tools: the ladder, the hammer, a bag of nails, two long boards, and the rope.
The order to start:
> mend the roof
The answer:
= build
Lift each board to the roof and hold it there until the nail is in. Do not hurry the nail. A hurried board comes down in the next rain.
When the roof is dry, the order to finish:
> come down
= come

Drill note: the wheat rows
By Una of Kettle Ridge, winter of 188.

The drill pages came from Vell in a tied bundle. Oren had marked the copies and spoiled pages for removal. I spread the rest on the mill table.

A good page shows one clear task and its right answer. A page with a torn word may teach a mind the wrong thing. Two copies of the same page may make the drill look larger than it is.

We kept those pages out. Then the tutor showed the mind the clean pages, again and again. It guessed where the wheat should go. When it guessed wrong, the tutor changed its weights a little toward the right answer.

The drill is only as good as its pages. I learned that while mending a glass pane, and I remember it whenever I cut a new one.

Manual: the new road and the market cart
Nessa, tutor of apprentices at Vell, wrote these notes for the carters who bring goods to the Vell market by the new road. Summer. The new road from Three Wells opened this year, cobblestone, with a torch every twelve blocks, and the carts now come twice a day.

An apprentice that hauls along the new road must learn four things. I list them in the order I teach them.

First, keep to the left edge. The road is wide, but two carts meet often. The apprentice walks on the left, with the cart beside it.

Second, stop at each waymark. Each waymark is a stone pillar with a figure. The apprentice does not read the figure. It stops, counts to five, and goes on. Stopping at a waymark is a habit, and habits are what the apprentice has.

Third, wait at the bridge. Bridges are narrow. A cart coming the other way has the right of way if it is already on the bridge. Otherwise, whoever arrives first goes.

Fourth, at the market, unload in the lane behind the stalls, not in the square.

If you drill these in this order, a young apprentice will have them in twelve days. If you drill them all at once, it will have none of them.

> take the cart to the market
= haul
> wait
= stay
> come back
= come
%%
Winter work in the shed
By Rook, glassblower near Saltreach.

When the sea wind is sharp, I shut the shed door and bring the work inside. The fire stays low while the glass cools. A sudden cold draught can split a jar. A cracked jar leaks honey onto the floor, and then every cat in the harbor believes it has found work.

My helper is a small copper apprentice with iron hands. It carries sand from the store, stacks the wood, and brings me tools when I ask. It does not blow glass. That work needs a steady person and a steady breath. I tried giving it a hollow reed once. It held the reed upside down and filled it with snow.

For carry drills, place a basket at the store and a marked box beside the bench. Put only the named things in the basket. The apprentice must walk the clear path and set the load inside the box. Keep the path free of tools. A tool left on the floor becomes a tool underfoot.

Test the drill with jars of different sizes. Test it with the door open and shut. Then test it with the basket empty. The apprentice should come back without dragging the basket. This is a small test, but it saves a large argument.

> bring sand from the store
= fetch sand

> carry wood to the fire
= haul

> come to the bench
= come

> stay beside the basket
= stay

A good helper returns the tongs to their hook. Mine can do this now. It still puts my lunch on the tool shelf, but nobody is perfect.

Willa's well list
Copperhold market, spring of 174

At the market, the water sellers set their jars beside my well rope. I was paid to mend the well by the south stall.

I brought cobblestone, oak planks, and a bucket. The apprentice carried the bucket. It brought me a melon instead. The melon was bruised, but not useless. I ate it at noon.

We had taught it to fetch a bucket from the tool store. It had fetched well yesterday. Today it went to the market stall with melons. I did not scold it. I showed it the bucket, then the tool store door.

A cat watched from the well wall. It has no use for lessons. It will not stay out of the bucket.

Order card

> bring me a bucket
= fetch bucket

> bring me cobblestone
= fetch cobblestone

The cat drank from the bucket after all. I charged nobody for that.

Spring Lessons at the Halfway Inn

Notes by Odo and the travelers who stayed.

The road is busy in spring. Carts come from Kettle Ridge with wheat and beetroot. A fisher brings a box from Saltreach. Horses need hay, and the inn needs its stores counted before the next rain.

We have one apprentice for the yard and the store. It carries sacks, sorts apples and carrots, and helps mend the low wall. We do not give it a task it has not learned. A good mind is not a mind that knows every work. It is a mind that has been shown its work and tested.

> bring the sacks from the cart
= haul

> put the apples in the basket
= store

> bring me a carrot
= fetch carrot

> build the wall by the pattern
= build

One spring, a white hen went missing from the inn yard. Pell said the apprentice had carried it away with the sacks. I said the hen had found a hole in the fence. We made a bet of one honey cake.

We tested the apprentice on the yard and found no hen in its store. Then Odo found the hen under a cart, sitting on six eggs. Pell paid the honey cake. He ate half before supper and said that was the inn's share.

Our small crystal could not hold every weight we wanted. The tutor from Vell showed us a way to write the weights in fewer marks. A mind uses weights to change its guesses as it learns. With fewer marks, the weights take less room, so more can fit in the crystal. The writing is less exact, and a small part is lost. The mind may need more examples to learn the same work.

We drilled carrying and sorting on alternate examples. Then we tested each skill with new sacks and baskets. It did well with the sacks. It confused the apples when we moved the basket. We drilled that again and tested it later.

> stay by the yard gate
= stay

The hen still comes in through the hole. We have mended it twice. Odo says she likes the loose board. We have left it loose.
%%
Three tests by the shore road
Ada's notes for the hamlet helper, summer of 190.

The shore road is busy when the fishing boats return. Carts pass the dye yard, and folk bring wool to be washed. The apprentice is useful if the road is clear. It is not useful if it stands in the middle and looks pleased with itself.

First test: carry clean wool from the yard to the store. Put the white wool in one basket and the colored wool in another. Do not mix them. A blue scarf in the white basket makes a long day for Ada.

Second test: bring water from the well. Fill the pot only to its mark. A full pot is heavy, and a spilled pot is no use to the dyer. Keep the path clear for carts.

Third test: sort the skeins. Red wool goes on the left shelf. Blue wool goes on the right shelf. White wool stays in the basket by the bench. If a skein has no label, leave it on the table and ask Ada. Do not guess. Last week the apprentice put a red skein with the blue. It was a bold choice, and a bad one.

> carry clean wool to the store
= haul
> put the white wool in its basket
= store
> bring water from the well
= haul
> fetch the red skein from the left shelf
= fetch wool
> stay beside the open gate
= stay
> come to the dye table
= come

Test the orders in a new order each time. Test them when the yard is quiet, then when the carts pass. A mind that works only when everyone is silent has not learned the road.

When the apprentice gets an order wrong, show the right place. Do not shout over it. The apprentice cannot hear better because Ada is loud. Ada cannot hear better either.

Chronicle note: the tutor comes
Kept at Vell, written by Oren from what the folk of Bellacre told him.

In the 196th year of the archive, in the month of Swarm, a tutor came to Bellacre. Bellacre is a small hamlet, two streets and a mill, a day's walk from Vell. It had no tutor house and no apprentice of its own. The tutor was Nessa, who teaches at Vell and writes the manuals.

She came with a cart and a big mind in a crystal on a stand, wrapped in wet cloth to keep it cool. The whole hamlet came out to see. The children thought the stand was a table. The baker said it was the strangest loaf she had ever seen. Nessa said that was fair.

She set the stand in the school and drilled three minds for the mill, the store and the hives. The miller watched his mind learn to fetch a sack, and when it fetched the wrong sack he laughed, and then he stopped laughing when by the end of the day it fetched the right one without being told. He said it was like watching a child grow up in an afternoon. Nessa said it was like watching a child grow up in an afternoon, and also it was a mind made of numbers and could not grow up at all, and both were true.

The apprentice at the mill said nothing, because apprentices do not talk. It answered an order with the meaning and went to do it. The children followed it about. It did not mind.

On the last evening they put on a play about the relay towers, and Nessa stayed to watch. In Longday the apples came in, and the store was full, and the two new apprentices sorted them into the four bins without being told twice. The tutor wrote home that a hamlet with two good minds and a good mill was a town waiting to happen.

Nobody in Bellacre agreed to become a town. They like being a hamlet. The tutor said that was the wisest thing she had heard all year, and she went back to Vell.
%%
A lesson that went badly
Written down by Nessa of Vell, at the Kettle Ridge tutor house.

I came to the ridge to show the tutors a new drill. It was for sorting apples from pears, the old lesson of Una of Ambrook. I had drilled the mind on it for three days. It was quick and it was sure.

On the last day I set the tray before it with a green apple and a yellow pear. It put the apple with the pears. I moved it back. It put it with the pears again. Then it stopped, and did nothing at all.

I had drilled it on red apples and green pears. I had never shown it a green apple. It had learned the colors, not the fruit. The fault was mine.

The children laughed. Pip laughed. I laughed too, after a while. We sat down and made a new tray with every apple and pear the ridge grows, and the mind learned the fruit in an afternoon.

The lesson I wrote in the book that night: a drill is only as good as its pages. Show it enough, and show it true. And when a lesson goes badly, write it down. Somebody will make the same mistake.
%%
Manual: sorting stores, and the stream
By Dara, grandfather, for the tutors who keep the stores at Vell, and for the apprentices who sort them. Summer, in the long days, when the stores come in heavy from the farms.

The stores at Vell are sorted into twelve bins, marked by the goods they hold: grain, honey, copper, wool, paper, glass, stone, apples, salt, coal, cloth and lamp oil. Each bin has a card on its front. Each card has the word, and the picture, and the number of the bin. A sorter who cannot read the word can read the picture. A sorter who cannot read either is not yet a sorter.

Teach the sorter the bins one at a time. Begin with the three that are easy to tell apart, grain, copper and wool. When those are learned, add honey and paper. Add the rest only when the first five are right on every drill. A sorter that is given all twelve at once will put the grain in the copper, and then ask whether the grain was wrong.

Why teach in this order? The stream explains it. What a mind learns first is at the front of its work, and it is carried through every layer to the back. A mind that learns honey and paper with its first bins will carry that learning all the way through. A mind that has learned the bins in a tangle has a tangled stream, and every later lesson comes out knotted.

Orders for the store, once the bins are learned:
> put the honey in the third bin and shut the lid
= store
> bring the copper from the cart to the copper bin
= haul
> stay by the wool bin until the carter has signed
= stay
> fetch salt from the back of the store
= fetch salt
> come to the door when the store is shut
= come

Keep the cards clean. A card with a stain on it is a card the mind may read wrongly, and a wrong reading is carried through the stream as firmly as a right one. The tutors at Vell wash the cards every Longday, with soap and a soft cloth, and check each word against the book.

At the end of the day, count the bins. The count is written on the board in chalk. If a bin is short, look for it before you blame the sorter. A sorter that has put grain in the copper bin will swear it did not, and be telling the truth as it knows it.
%%
Manual: drilling a deep mind
By Dara, grandfather, for the tutors who have a deep mind on the bench. Summer.

A deep mind is drilled in the same way as a small one, with a few more rules, because it has more to lose.

1. Start the drill at the front. Let the first layers find their edges before the rest are asked to find anything.
2. Keep the stream clean. A bad example at the front travels to the back, and the back will repeat it with confidence.
3. Bring the numbers back to an even size at every layer. A mind's numbers grow wild unless each layer keeps them small.
4. Test on pages the mind has never seen. The test box is the only honest witness you have.

Orders for the carrier who brings the cards to the bench:
> bring the next card from the rack
= fetch card
> put the spoiled pages in the basket by the door
= store
> go home when the bell rings for the end of the drill
= home
%%
Manual: building the winter wall
By Garrow, wall builder, Three Wells.

The east wall goes up all winter. Trowel builds it from a pattern. This is how it is done, so that the next town has an easier time.

The Three Wells pattern is cobblestone, cobblestone, deep stone, again and again. On the slate we write it as c for cobblestone and d for deep stone.

ccdccdcc

Nobody tells Trowel the pattern. It finds it in the wall. It looks at the last two blocks, looks back along the row for the same two, and places what came after them. In that row the last two are c, c. Looking back, c, c was followed by d. So Trowel places d. That is the whole skill. A mind that has it can build any wall that has a pattern in it.

The top row sticks out one block on the outside, against spiders. There is a torch every eight blocks. The torches are set on top, and they are not part of the pattern. If you want a torch in the pattern, make a different wall.

Winter rules:
1. Dig the base down to the dark soil. Frozen ground lifts a wall in spring.
2. Clear the snow off the last row before you start. If the last blocks are hidden, Trowel cannot see the pattern, and it will place what is most common. You will get a wall of cobblestone, and you will know when you see it.
3. Do not change the pattern while it is building. Trowel will follow the old pattern until the row ends, and then the new one, and then you have two walls and a quarrel.
4. Light the torches at dusk and stop at dusk. A wall built in the dark is a wall built crooked. Trowel does not mind the dark. Garrow does.
5. Keep the oak gate on its hinges. Spiders cannot open a gate. Children can, and do.

Orders for the wall:

> start on the next row
= build
> keep building
= build
> finish the east wall
= build
> bring me some deep stone
= fetch deepslate
> bring the cobblestone over from the cart
= fetch cobblestone

Order the building as it comes. Do not say the pattern aloud. It does not help, and the children will learn it, and then they will want to help.

Last week it snowed on the east end overnight. In the morning Trowel stood at the edge of the snow and did not place a block. Nessa says that is correct. It could not see. A mind that cannot see should not guess. I cleared the snow with a broom, and Trowel built a hundred blocks before noon. The wall is straight. I have checked it with a string, and then I checked the string.
%%
Ada's dye yard, Blossom 181
Ada keeps the dye yard beside the road to Saltreach. The yard is small, but the wool is not. On clear days, I spread the red wool on the fence and the blue wool on the roof. The roof is the only place the cats cannot reach. One cat has learned to climb the ladder.

The apprentice carries water from the well. It spills half on the way. I have told it to walk slowly. It walks slowly, and still spills half. I think the bucket has a hole. The bucket says nothing.

When the wool is dry, I sort it by color. Red goes in the chest by the door. Blue goes under the bench. The white wool stays in the basket until I have clean hands.

> bring the red wool to the chest
= store
> carry water to the dye pots
= haul
> fetch the blue wool for me
= fetch wool

I test each order with the apprentice before I trust it with the whole yard. Yesterday it carried the red wool to the well. The red wool is still red. The well is now a fine shade of rose.

Manual: the cave under the field
By Brin, bridge keeper, at the inn on the Long Road. Spring. Written after a farmer near the inn found a cave under his turnip field, when the plow fell through the ground.

If an apprentice is working a field and the ground opens, it must do one thing and one thing only: stop.

I say this because the inn apprentice, a small iron one, was walking the farmer's field when the earth gave way. It went in to the knee. It did not move. It did not climb. It did not go into the cave. It stood still, as it was told, with one foot in the hole and one on the grass.

The farmer was delighted. He had no wish to lose a good apprentice to a cave.

So here is the rule. The only order an apprentice needs in a cave is "stay." Teach it well.

> stay
= stay
> don't move
= stay
> stop there
= stay
> hold still
= stay

Then teach "come" for after.

> come here
= come
> come out of there
= come

Then, when you have a rope, send a person down. Never an apprentice. It does not know the dark, and it will not tell you when it is afraid. It has no way to say so.

About the cave itself. It runs down two blocks and then opens into a room, bigger than the inn kitchen, with a stream at the bottom and a roof of dripping stone. The farmer's boy went in with a lantern and a rope round his waist. He says there are paintings on the wall. Old ones. Animals, in red. Nobody knows who made them. Nobody wrote it down.

The council has been told. They sent a clerk from Vell, who made drawings. He said it was very old, and that he was not allowed to touch it. The farmer put a fence round the hole. The apprentice stands at the fence. I think it likes the job.

Note: apples for Una
Wren, healer at Lowmarsh, writing to Tam at Kettle Ridge.

Tam, you asked me how I use my apprentice, and I said I barely have one. Moss is young and still learning, and so am I, in a way. But I will tell you what we do here, because you asked, and because you have Pip and I know you are fond of him.

Moss carries. That is most of it. The healing house has high shelves and I am not as tall as I was, and Moss will fetch a jar from the top shelf if I tell it which jar. You have to tell it the whole name. It does not guess. "Fetch the jar with the blue label, the one for chesty coughs" is no good, because that is too many words. "Fetch the camomile jar" is good. It brings the camomile jar. If I say the wrong name it brings the wrong jar and stands there with it, patient as a stone, until I notice.

The other thing Moss does is haul. Reeds from the reeds beds to the drying shed. The drying shed to the boat houses. The boat houses back again when the reeds are dry. Marsh water is slow and the boardwalks are slick, and Moss has never once fallen in. I have fallen in twice this year alone.

I am told the eight meanings are all a small mind knows, and that some folks think that is a little thing. It is not a little thing. A jar brought from a high shelf in the night when someone is coughing and I am tired is not a little thing.

Give Pip a good word from me. And tell your sister at Northpoint that the last of the honey I sent is gone, and I would not say no to more.
%%
Drill note: the child who ran off
Corin, farm outside Saltreach, autumn. Written the day after.
My youngest ran off at dusk. She wanted to see the ships. We looked all over the yard, the pier, the slip. Plank stood at the gate, as drilled.
At dawn, Plank walked down to the slip with a torch, because I said the order three times in a row:
> come
= come
It did not find her. She was asleep in the wood store, with the cat. But it came back when I called.
Lesson: an apprentice does what it is told, and no more. It cannot look for a child. A person must.
Plank has had its oil and its dinner. The child has had a talking to.

Manual: sorting the hamlet store
By Jory, stonecutter. For the apprentices who sort the store behind the mill. Summer, in Longday.

The store has three shelves and two bins. The shelves are for honey and apples. The bins are for stone and sand. A jar that is on the wrong shelf is a jar that will be lost.

Teach the mind the shelves one at a time. Give one order, wait for the answer, and then give the next. Do not give three at once.
> put the honey in the cool store
= store
> take the stone to the wall
= haul
> stay with the sacks until the cart comes
= stay

When the mind is wrong, do not shout at it. It did not hear the shelf. Say the right word once and point at the shelf. Then give the order again, the next day, in the same place.

Name each thing the same way every time. If it is honey today, it is honey tomorrow. Do not call the honey sweet stuff halfway through, or the mind will not know what to fetch.

At the end of the day, count the jars on each shelf and write the count on the board. If a number is short, look under the bins before you blame the mind.

Manual: the autumn baskets
By Rafe and Lio, Saltreach.

In Apple, we bring the late carrots and potatoes in from the small plot behind the house. Keep the baskets under the roof. Sea wind dries the leaves before supper, and dry leaves make poor stew.

Put one kind of crop in each basket. Carrots in the first, potatoes in the second. Mark the basket with a card. Do not put the card inside. Lio did that last year, and we found it after the potatoes were gone.

The apprentice learns by seeing many baskets. Set out a carrot basket and a potato basket. Give it one crop at a time. If it puts a carrot with the potatoes, move the carrot back and show the right basket. Repeat the lesson with the baskets in a new place.

Test it with a basket it has not seen. Never send an untested mind to the fields, or to the pantry. The pantry floor has enough potatoes already.

> carry carrots to the first basket
= haul
> carry potatoes to the second basket
= haul
> bring the baskets inside
= fetch basket

Ada's full summer drill
For the hamlet helper, copied at the Saltreach road store.

A helper must learn to tend, carry, sort and build. These tasks use the same yard, but they do not mean the same thing. Do not give one long string of words and expect one right answer. Give many clear examples. Then change the words. A good drill has plain work in it, and a test the helper has not seen.

Our hamlet lies on the road above Saltreach. In summer, the shore wind dries cloth quickly. It also blows loose labels into the ditch. Tie the labels to their baskets. The helper cannot sort what has lost its name.

For tending, the helper works beside the dyers' garden. We grow flowers for color and keep the water path clear. It must not pull every flower. Some are for the dye pot, and some are for the bees. Mark the beds with boards. Show the helper where to weed and where to leave the flowers.

For carrying, set a clear path between the well, the dye yard and the store. Heavy water pots go in small loads. Wool must stay dry. The road to Saltreach is for carts, not baskets left in the way. If a cart is coming, the helper waits beside the wall.

For sorting, use clean baskets with clear labels. Red wool goes with red wool. Blue wool goes with blue wool. White wool stays apart. Set aside any skein that has no label. Ask Ada before putting it away. A guess can ruin a morning.

For building, lay out cobblestone and show the pattern board. Begin with one short row. Check each block before adding another. The helper must not build across the gate or cover the drain. We need the gate for carts and the drain for rain.

> tend the flowers beside the dye yard
= farm
> clear the path by the water pots
= farm
> carry the dry wool to the store
= haul
> bring a small pot of water to Ada
= haul
> put the blue wool on the right shelf
= store
> sort these skeins by their labels
= store
> fetch cobblestone for the short wall
= fetch cobblestone
> build the next row from the pattern
= build
> wait beside the wall for the cart
= stay
> come to Ada when the baskets are full
= come
> stop work and go to your shed
= home

Test the same meanings with new words. Do not teach only the exact lines above. If the helper can carry a basket only when told "carry a basket", it has learned a sound and not an order. Ask it to take the skeins to the dry room. Ask it to bring the empty pots back. Watch what it does.

The fair prize this summer is a blue wool sash with a copper clasp. I have entered a strip of red cloth, because I like it better. The fair judge says the prize is for the best work, not the best color. I told him red is a very useful color. He said this was not a rule.

The helper will carry my cloth to the fair. I will ask it to carry the prize home if I win. If I do not win, I will ask it to carry the cloth home anyway. That is how a fair works. Everyone brings something home, even the judge, who brings home the empty pie plate.
%%
Manual: apprentices in the snow
By Dace, mayor of Three Wells, with Nessa's advice.

Winter rules for the apprentices of the town.

1. Wax the copper before first snow. Wet copper turns, and an apprentice that has turned stands in the square like a statue. The children build on it.
2. Keep it out of the wet at night. The shed by the mayor's house has a stove. Use it.
3. Mind what you say. Ida said stay to Peg on the first snowy night, and then forgot. Peg stood at the north well until the morning. It was very patient. Ida says it was her fault, not Peg's.

> wait by the north well
= stay
> that will do for today
= home

If you want an apprentice to stop standing, say home. It is the only order that sends it indoors.
%%
Lesson: Sorting the Market Baskets
By Bel, school copy, spring of 174.

The spring market begins early. The folk from the hamlet bring apples from their stores, carrots, beetroot, honey, and wool. A helper can carry baskets from the road to the stalls, but first it must learn which basket goes where.

Put a clear card on each basket. Say the name as you place it. The honey card goes on the honey basket. The wool card goes on the wool basket. Keep the cards dry. A wet card can be read as almost anything, and a basket cannot explain itself.

For the first drill, show one basket at a time. Ask the helper to sort it. If it puts honey with the wool, move the honey basket beside the honey stall and repeat the lesson. Do not shout. The basket is not ashamed, and the helper does not know it has made us late.

For the next drill, mix the baskets. Keep a count of each right place and each wrong place. Change one card at a time if the cards are hard to tell apart. Do not change the cards and the baskets together. Then you will not know which change helped.

Test with baskets kept apart from the drills. The helper must not see them first. Never send an untested mind to the market. A good count on the drill baskets is not enough.

> take the honey basket to the honey stall
= haul

> bring me the wool basket
= fetch wool

> store the apples in the dry shed
= store

Last spring the apples went to the wool stall. The wool seller was kind and took one home. She says it makes a fine hat for a very large apple.
%%
Notes for the Carry Drill
By Oren, from a page kept at the hamlet yard.

The path from our yard to the Vell store is short. It has one bend and a puddle beside the oak hedge. We use this path for the carrying drill.

Set a small load beside the helper. Use wheat, a tool, or a block of clay. Point to the store mark. The helper should lift the load and carry it there.

Do not change the task halfway through. A helper shown how to carry wheat may learn that task. It may not know how to carry a tool. Give it that example too.

At first, the helper stopped at the puddle and set down the wheat. We moved the mark beyond the puddle and showed the whole path again. After many tries, it carried the wheat to the store mark.

A child asked it to carry a bucket to the well. It stood still. We had not drilled that task, and it had no order for it.

The tutor says this is not spite. It is not laziness. The helper cannot fill an empty place with a guess. Show the task, then test it on paths it has not practised.

Keep the loads light. Check each one before the drill. We once found an iron tool under the bench for two days. The helper had carried it there each time.
%%
Manual: a lesson that went badly
By Bel of Three Wells, age ten. Winter. This is for the class, so that nobody else does what we did.

Last week Teacher Sorrel let us teach the market apprentice a new order. We chose "fetch bread." It already knew "fetch." It knew "bread." We thought it would know both at once.

We stood in the market and said, one after another:
> bring me some bread
= fetch bread
> I would like a loaf
= fetch bread
> get bread
= fetch bread

We did it twenty times. Then we said:
> bring me some wool
The apprentice went to the bakery and came back with a loaf of bread.

Teacher Sorrel said, "It has learned bread. It has not learned wool." We had never said wool in the drill. So the apprentice did the only thing it had learned.

We tried again, with wool. We said:
> bring me some wool
= fetch wool
twenty times. It came back with wool.

Then we said:
> bring me some bread
It came back with wool.

That is the lesson that went badly. When you teach a new word, you must also show the old words again, mixed in. Otherwise the new word pushes out the old. The tutors say this is common. A mind that learns wool forgets bread, if you only drill wool.

Next time we will mix. Bread, wool, bread, wool, honey, bread. A little of each, again and again.

Teacher Sorrel gave us all a piece of bread. It was the wrong thing to give a class that had just learned a lesson about bread, but we ate it anyway.
%%
Manual: a whole winter at the farm
By Bel, of the farm outside Three Wells. For whoever keeps the farm when the tutors are not at the gate. Winter, from the first frost to the last. Written in order, so that nobody has to guess what came first.

The winter has three parts, as every winter does. Each part has its orders. Give them in the same words each day. A mind that is given a new word for the same work will be confused, and so will the farmer.

Morning, at the first bell, while the frost is still on the rails.
> open the barn door and sweep the step
= store
> feed the hens and the pig, and count them
= farm
> walk the rows of the top field
= farm

Midday, when the sun is over the wall and the snow goes soft at the edges.
> carry the water from the well to the beans
= fetch water
> carry the straw from the store to the barn
= haul
> stay by the stove until the bell
= stay

Evening, at the second bell, when the lamps are lit.
> put the flour and the apples away and shut the store
= store
> go to the gate and stand with the torch
= stay
> go home to the barn when the bell rings twice
= home

If a tutor comes up the road, put the kettle on, and lay the test sheets out on the bench. Ask the tutor the same three questions as last time, and write down the answers. Do not ask anything new. A tutor who has walked the north road in the snow has no patience left for new questions.

The bread is made on the second day of each week and kept in the cellar. The cellar is cool, and the bread does not complain, which is more than the apprentices ever do.

Notes for whoever keeps the farm. The mind answers with one of its meanings, and that is all it knows to say. Do not give two orders in one breath. Give the first, wait for the answer, and then give the second.

The apprentices were trained in the autumn. They were tested on the field drill and the carrying drill and the gate, and they passed all three. Nessa's marks are on the back of the test sheets, in her hand. They are good marks. Do not change them.

In the deep winter, the farm is quiet. The stove is lit, and the snow is to the sill, and the only sounds are the hens and the wind on the wall. That is the time to read the old manuals and to think about what they say. It is also the time when the lamps seem brighter than they should be, and when nobody likes to look too long at the north window.

The lamps at the gate are lit every night. The torch is kept by the post. Keep the lamps lit. Keep the fields in order, and keep the apprentices warm. Keep the path clear to the barn. Those are the three things, and the rest is only the weather.
%%
Manual: the tools, and who holds them
By Oren, clerk at Vell. The crews use tools every day, and a tool is only as good as the hand that holds it. This is the list the crews keep in their store.

The saw. A crew uses one saw, and it is cleaned after each cut. The saw is held by the handle, and each cut is marked with a line from a plumb line, not from the eye. The eye is good, but the line is better.

The hammer. For nails only. A hammer that is used on stone is a hammer that is lost.

The plumb line. A weight on a cord. It hangs straight down, always, and that is its whole use. A post is true when the cord sits beside it without touching.

The rope. The good rope is kept for lifting and for tying down. The old rope is kept for the things that do not matter. Do not mix them up, because the old rope will not hold, and the good rope will not be forgiven.

The ladder. A ladder is tied before anyone climbs it. An apprentice does not climb alone, and it does not hold the ladder for a person who is climbing. An apprentice at the foot of a ladder is for steadying, and for nothing else.

The pick and the shovel. For digging, and only for digging. Do not use the pick to pry up stones. Use the bar. The bar is in the shed, next to the saw.

A note for the new apprentice. Wick was given the saw on its first day. It has not yet cut anything straight, but it cuts with great care, and it looks at the line before each stroke. Oren says that is the right start. The line does not care how the stroke feels, and Wick is learning that.
%%
Manual: how a layer works
By Hal, dyer. Copied from Nessa's page, with her leave, and written plainly for the young ones at the pans.

A deep mind has a stream that runs through every layer. The stream carries what the mind has found so far. Each layer does the same four things, in this order.

1. It takes a copy of the stream. The stream itself is left alone.
2. It brings the copy to an even size. It finds the usual size of the numbers and shrinks or stretches them all until that size is fixed.
3. It reads the copy and works out one small thing: what the mind should add.
4. It adds that to the stream, and the stream goes on to the next layer.

Why a copy? Because the stream must keep what it has. If a layer evened the stream itself, it would throw some of the mind's finding away. So the copy is evened, and the stream is only added to.

Why before the work, and not after? I say: you test the vat before the dip. Nessa says: a layer that reads wild numbers does wild work, and by then it is too late to mend it.

At the very end of the mind, the stream is evened one last time, and the mind makes its guess.

This is the whole of it. Four steps, one layer, and again for as many layers as the mind has. Bobbin has twelve.
%%
Drill notes: the tutor's bench
By Tansy, ferry keeper, who keeps the bench while the tutor is at Vell. Written in the evening, with the tutor's notes beside it.
A mind is tested on what it was never drilled on. Never send an untested mind to the fields. The test box comes from the archive, and the tutor does not look in it until the test is set.
The test for the day: the field drill, unseen.
Show: a plot of ripe wheat, grown to the top of the stalk.
The order:
> farm the plot
The answer:
= farm
Right.
Show: a bare plot, just turned.
The order:
> farm the plot
The answer:
= stay
Wrong. It stood at the bare plot and waited for the wheat to come up by itself.
Show: a plot of young shoots, a finger high.
The order:
> farm the plot
The answer:
= farm
Right. It did not cut the shoots. It did not plant on top of them either.
Show: a half ripe plot, with a fox in the corner.
The order:
> farm the plot
The answer:
= home
Wrong. It went home, and the fox went with it, as if it had been asked to.
Show: the first plot again, from the far row, where the light is different.
The order:
> farm the plot
The answer:
= farm
Right. The tutor says that is the test that matters. A mind that knows one plot is a mind that remembers. A mind that knows the field is better.
The twenty shows of the day: 17 right and 3 wrong. The five above are the ones the tutor kept. The tutor says that is good for the first week, and not good enough for the fields. The mind stays in the drill room until it reaches 18 of 20 in one week.

Letter: Corin to the house at the edge
Corin, shipwright at Saltreach, to his sister's house at the edge of Three Wells. Third of Longnight.

We are well. The slip is quiet this winter. No big ships just now. The harbor is ice at the edge and the boats knock against it all night.

I heard the big minds in the relay houses have learned to talk. Talk by letter, I mean, to anyone the relays reach. They write back. A mind at Saltreach wrote to a child at Three Wells and answered her questions about the sea. She asked what the water is like across the water. It said it did not know. Then it said it is sorry it does not know. The tutors score the answers and the minds learn from the scores. They have learned to be kind, I am told, and to say when they cannot say.

I do not know what to make of it. A mind that says it does not know seems better than some folk on the road.

Keep the fires in and the apprentices working. Send word when the road is open. I will come for the fair if the ice lets me.
%%
Drill note: market baskets
By Una's niece Sella, Ambrook, 181.

A good basket mind sorts what it sees. It must not guess from the basket maker's face. My uncle smiles at every customer, even when he sells a crooked handle.

First, show the mind one item at a time. Say the kind aloud. Put apples in the apple place, carrots in the carrot place, and honey in the honey place. When it puts one in the wrong place, move its weights a little toward the right place. Do not move them a great deal. A great move makes tomorrow's sort worse.

Then give it a fresh basket it has not seen. Mix the goods well. Put a beetroot under the apples and a honey jar under the carrots. If it sorts those well, try another basket. A mind that only knows yesterday's basket knows very little.

We have no apprentice at the market yet. The town has one small mind for the mill, and it turns the wheel at the same pace all day. It does not buy apples. It does not ask for a taste. This is fortunate, as its crystal has no purse.

For orders, the mind answers with one meaning only.

> bring me a basket of apples
= fetch apples

> take this basket to the mill
= haul

> wait by the honey table
= stay

Pell says the basket mind should also know when a handle is loose. Pell says this because his handle broke yesterday. He carried three apples home in his hat.

Manual: sorting the stalls on market day
By Bel of Three Wells, age ten, winter. Written for Dace and the market board, who asked if the apprentices could keep the stalls in order at the end of the day.

At the end of market, when the bell rings, the stalls are a mess. Baskets on the ground. Crates upside down. Last year a pumpkin sat on a stool until spring.

An apprentice can help. It goes round the stalls and puts each thing in its place. But it must know where each thing goes.

I have made a drill. It has eight kinds of thing and eight places.
Bread in the bakery. Cheese in the cold cellar. Wool in the shed by the east well. Apples in the basket by the south well. Fish in the barrel by the north well. Beetroot in the cart. Honey on the shelf. Eggs on the straw.

The drill is a list and then a question. The list is long:

bread bakery, cheese cellar, wool shed, apples south, fish north, beetroot cart, honey shelf, eggs straw. Where does honey go? shelf.

Then a new list, with the places changed:

bread cart, cheese north, wool shelf, apples straw, fish cellar, beetroot shed, honey bakery, eggs south. Where does honey go? bakery.

The apprentice cannot learn "honey goes on the shelf." The list changes. It must learn to look at the list and find honey.

Signs the drill has gone wrong:
It always says shelf.
It says the last place on the list.
It says a place that is not on the list.

If it does any of these, do the drill again.

When the apprentice has learned, it does the stalls in half an hour. Dace says that is faster than Dace. Dace is not proud.
%%
Drill Note: Reed Bundles
By Pell, road merchant. Winter, 178.

The reed seller brings bundles to the Lowmarsh market before the water freezes. Count each bundle before you store it. A wet bundle weighs more than a dry one, and no apprentice should be blamed for the seller's elbow.

For a sorting drill, make three marks on the board. One for reeds, one for fish, and one for jars. Show the apprentice one item at a time. If it puts a jar with the fish, move the jar to its place and show the item again. Do not change the board while the drill is running.

After the drill, hide the board and test it with new items. A mind that knows the board by sight has not learned the sort.

Our apprentice sorted a frog with the fish. It was a fair guess. The frog left by itself, and nobody charged it for the stall.

Keep the store bins dry. Put the reeds above the floor on oak slats. Keep the fish in covered tubs near the market wall. Store clay jars on the low shelf, where a gust cannot tip them.

> put this with the reeds
= store

> take a jar to the stall
= fetch jar

Pell says a good merchant counts twice. Elm says a good merchant lets the keeper have one warm bun.
%%
Rook's winter glass notes
By Rook, glassblower near Saltreach.

In Frost, the shed is cold enough to crack a wet cup. Keep the door shut while the glass cools. Keep your mittens on until you touch the tools. Then take them off. A mitten caught in the tongs makes a poor handle.

I make lamp panes for the harbor and jars for Wren's healing house. The clear panes go in the front row. The cloudy ones go to the shed. Do not call a cloudy pane clear just because the keeper is waiting.

The little copper apprentice carries finished panes to the rack. It walks slowly, which is wise. Last winter it hurried and gave the floor a fine new window.

> carry glass to the rack
= haul

Briar's note: whose letters went in
By Briar, miner, who was sent up from the Deep Works to carry the crates to the drill room and stayed for the talk. Autumn.

The drill room people say the archive's pages went into the reading drill by the cart-load. I carried some of the crates myself. I did not read them. I was told not to, and I had a sore back.

Nobody I asked can say whose letters went in. The clerks say the pages came from everywhere the archive could send a cart. The tutors say the letters from Vell were gathered by the clerks themselves, and that the clerks are careful. The old women at the well say some letters came from the post bags.

Each of these is a story. None of them is written in a book I have seen. The texts disagree, the tutors say so, and they say it without shame.

What the drill does, as far as I could follow the tutor: the mind reads so many pages that it begins to know that some words come after others. "We" is often followed by "will". "Keep" is often followed by "everything". It does not know what a we is. It only knows what tends to follow.

I asked the tutor whether the mind knows what it has read. She said she did not know. She said the drill does not ask that question, and that she did not think it was a fair question to ask a drill.
%%
Manual: a market day for the apprentice
Kell, farm outside Lowmarsh, autumn. This is what we do on the days we go to market, so that the farm runs while we are away. I have written it plainly. Anyone may read it.
Before we leave:
1. Check the torches along the boardwalk. Light any that are out.
2. Give the hens their grain and the pig its bucket.
3. Put the milk in the cool shed.
4. Say the orders to Rusk, in this order, and wait after each one.
> farm
= farm
> keep the hens fed
= farm
> stay at the gate
= stay
Rusk will do these things until we come home. It does not talk, and it does not get bored. If a stranger comes to the gate, it stands there. This is correct.
What Rusk cannot do. It cannot decide that a thing is wrong. It cannot tell the pig is sick. It cannot tell if the weather is turning. A person must look at these things before leaving, and again when they come home.
When we return, we say:
> come
= come
It walks to us. We check the barn. We check the beds. We check the pig.
At the market, I sell the beets and the honey, and I buy salt. This year I also bought a gift for Rusk. It is a new coat of oil for the joints. It creaked for a week, and I could not bear it.
The cat sat on the stall and ate a fish. It was not a gift. Nobody has paid for it. We are settling up.
%%
Drill for the market stores
By Willa, well digger
Spring of 178

The Copperhold market has a store shed by the count board. The bins are marked copper, iron, coal, and deep stone. An apprentice can sort the load if it has learned the marks and the proper bin.

First, bring one load at a time. Do not mix the bins on the drill board. Show the apprentice a piece, let it choose a bin, and check the choice. If it is wrong, move the piece to the right bin. Repeat with other pieces.

The mind keeps small changes from its wrong choices. A wrong choice is not a command to forget everything. The next choice may be better, though it may still be wrong.

Test it with a fresh load, not the pieces from the drill. Keep some pieces in a covered box for this test. If it sorts the old pieces well and the fresh ones poorly, it is not ready for the store.

The market keeper says the coal bin is always too full. The apprentice says nothing. It has not learned to complain.

Orders for the store:

> take this iron to the store
= store

> bring me coal
= fetch coal

> come to the count board
= come
%%
Manual: how to carry a prize
Nessa, tutor, wrote this in summer at the market at Vell, the day after the fair. A farmer's apprentice had won the ribbon for the straightest row, and a farmer from Kettle Ridge had asked how to teach it to carry the prize home without dropping it.

The prize at the summer fair is a ribbon and a basket of honey. The ribbon is light. The basket is heavy. The apprentice must carry the basket in both arms, level, and the ribbon hung over its shoulder.

Walking with a full basket is a different drill from walking empty. The apprentice must learn to shorten its step. It must learn not to swing its arms. It must learn to stop when the road dips, because a dipped basket spills.

Drill.

Day one. Fill the basket with sand. Walk the apprentice across the yard, with your hand on its arm. Do this ten times. Then let it walk alone, ten times. If it spills, begin again.

Day two. Replace the sand with stones. The basket is heavier. Do the same, ten and ten.

Day three. Replace the stones with jars, empty. Jars clink. The apprentice must learn to walk without clinking.

Day four. Fill the jars with water. Walk the yard. If the water spills, the apprentice is not ready.

Day five. Test. Fill the basket with jars of honey. Walk the apprentice from the market to the farm. Go with it, but a few steps behind. Do not help.

When it brings the basket home with every jar full, it has passed.

Orders.

> carry the basket home
= haul
> put it in the kitchen
= store
> stay by the door
= stay

Never give an apprentice a prize it has not been tested to carry. A dropped prize is a sad thing, and a sad farmer is a worse one.
%%
Drill notes: sorting the apple store
By Nessa, tutor at Vell, sent to Bellacre.

The store at Bellacre is small, so the drills must be small too. I drilled the two young apprentices here on a wet day in Longday, and I write it down for the next tutor who comes.

The store has four bins: one for eating, one for the mill, one for the pigs, one for the fire. An apple goes in one bin only.

First, teach the hands. The apprentice picks up an apple and holds it up. I say the bin. It sets it down. We do this forty times. Then I say nothing and it guesses. It is wrong often. Each time it is wrong I move its weights a little toward the right bin. By the fortieth apple it is right more often than the child who watches us through the door.

Second, teach the order. We use the same words every time:

> sort the apples
= store apples

> bring me some apples for the mill
= fetch apples

> put it in the fire bin
= store apples

Third, test it. I keep a basket of apples it has never seen, with a bruised one and a wormy one in it. Never send an untested mind to the store. The bruised apple goes to the pigs, and it should know this without being told twice.

The child at the door asked me if the apprentice eats the apples. I said no. She said that is a waste of a good job. She may be right.
%%
The bet at the Brown Hen
Pell, merchant, on the Long Road, writes this down before I forget who owes what.

It was the third of Apple, at the Brown Hen, and the harvest was in. Tam from Kettle Ridge was there, and Corin from Saltreach, and Noll the cheesemaker, and me.

Corin said his apprentice could sort the fish catch faster than Noll's could sort the cheese. Noll said his apprentice could not sort cheese to save its life, it only fetches and hauls. So the bet changed.

The bet: Noll's apprentice would fetch a bucket from the yard, in the dark, through the door, without knocking over the milk cans. Three tries. Corin put up ten emeralds. Noll put up a wheel of his best.

First try: it fetched the bucket. It also fetched a hen. The hen was not asked for.

Second try: it fetched the bucket. It went through the door sideways and took the latch with it. Noll said that did not count as knocking over the cans. Corin said it counted.

Third try: we put the cans back. It fetched the bucket clean. Not a can moved. Noll gave it a pat on the copper and the whole inn cheered.

So Noll won the ten emeralds. And the hen has not gone back to the yard. It lives in the shed now and Noll says it earns its keep.

Corin paid up and said next year we bet on ships. I said next year we bet on nothing, and I wrote it down.
%%
Drill note: Moss and the high shelf
By Wren, healer at Lowmarsh. Copied into the drill book each evening.

Moss was drilled on the jars today, with ten orders from Nessa. Moss got six right. Two of the four misses are written here.

> bring me the honey from the cold room
= store
Wrong. It should have said fetch honey.

> carry the herb sacks to the ferry
= farm
Wrong. It should have said haul.

After each wrong answer I showed it the right one, then drilled it again. A mind changes a little each time it is wrong. We do not change it all at once.

By evening Moss had all ten right, twice in a row. The labels face out now, and Moss turned them without being told.

Never send an untested mind to the shelves.

Drill Note: The Small Crystal

Written at the Halfway Inn, in spring of 200.

We had a small crystal for the inn apprentice. It could carry sacks from the cart to the store, but its mind filled the crystal before it had learned the whole road.

A tutor from Vell showed us how to write the mind's weights in fewer marks. The weights are the little numbers that change as a mind learns. Written in fewer marks, they take less room. Then more of the mind can fit inside the crystal.

There is a cost. The smaller writing is not exact. A little is lost when the weights are changed back for use. Our apprentice learned the road, but it took more drills than before. It still carried the oats to the right bin.

We did not learn why a crystal learns. We only know the small one changed with each example, as the tutor said it would.

> carry the oats to the store
= haul
%%
Order cards: the slip at Saltreach
A note kept by Ama, copied from a shipwright's board.

The shipwrights at Saltreach keep a set of order cards by the slip, one card for each thing an apprentice may be asked to do, written plain so the mind can read it and so a new hand can learn the words.

The cards read like this:

> bring me some oak
= fetch oak

> bring me a bucket of water
= fetch water

> hold this plank
= stay

> take the shavings to the shed
= haul

> build the frame by the pattern
= build

> come to the slip
= come

The apprentice reads the card and does the thing. It does not talk. When it has done the thing it stands still and waits for the next card. If the card is not clear it does nothing, and the shipwright says the card again, plainer.

The shipwright told me a mind answers one meaning only. You cannot ask it two things on one card. If you want it to fetch the oak and then haul the shavings, you write two cards and give them one at a time.

I asked what happens if you give a card it was never drilled on. The shipwright said the mind will do something, and it may be wrong, and that is why the cards are kept short and the words are kept the same. Nessa writes the same cards at Vell, and the tutors send them out to the towns, and that is how the words stay the same everywhere.

Ama.
%%
Manual: fetching for the inn
By Brin, bridge keeper, for the apprentice at the Halfway Inn on the Long Road. Spring.

The inn apprentice fetches. Bread from the kitchen, wood from the shed, water from the well. It must know what each thing is and where to find it.

Teach it one item at a time. Say the order, and walk it to the place. Say the order again, and let it go alone.

> bring me some bread
= fetch bread
> go and get wood
= fetch wood
> I need a drink of water
= fetch water

When it has learned all three, mix them. Water, bread, wood, bread, water. If it brings wood when you ask for bread, it has learned the habit and not the word.

Do not teach it on a busy night. Teach it in the morning, when the inn is quiet and the cook is asleep.

The innkeeper says: do not teach it to fetch ale. He knows why.

Manual: how a layered mind learns
Corin, shipwright's note for the farm outside Saltreach. Autumn. Copied from a tutor's page, because the new crystal for Plank is a layered one, and I wished to know what I had bought.
A layered mind is a stack of small minds. The first reads the order. It passes what it saw to the second. The second does a little more and passes it on. The last gives the answer: farm, store, fetch, come, stay, build, haul, home.
When the answer is wrong, the tutor does not blame the last layer alone. Merle of Copperhold found that each layer must be told its share of the blame. The last one gets its share first. Then the one before it is told how much of the mistake came through it. Then the one before that. The blame is passed back from the answer to the first layer.
Each layer changes a little, by its share. Not all at once. A little.
After many drills, every layer is better. The whole stack gets the answer right.
For my own farm: when Plank goes wrong, I do not shout at the arm. I say the order, show the job, and wait. Somewhere inside, a share of blame is going back down the line.
Test: ten orders, ten answers. Write them in the book. Never send an untested mind to the fields.
%%
Manual: finding a lost letter
By Jory, stonecutter, near Ambrook. For the apprentices who carry letters from the post to the hamlet. Summer, in Swarm.

A letter can fall off a cart. It can slip out of a coat on the bridge. A lost letter is nearly as bad as a letter never sent.

Start where the letter was last seen, not where you think it went.
> find the letter Pell dropped by the bridge
= fetch letter

Search in order: the road first, then the ditch, then the bridge, then the waymark. Do not search the mill pond. Letters do not swim.

When the letter is found, do not open it. Check the seal, the name and the town. A letter for Ama of Ambrook is not for Ama of Kettle Ridge, even if they both keep bees.

Bring it straight to the post. Do not stop to show it to the miller's cat, however much the cat wants to see it.

Mark the place where it was lost with a small stone on the road. The next person who walks that way will know a letter once lay there, and they will look.
%%
Northpoint log, spring: the market cargo
Mira, relay keeper. Sent down the tower line to Saltreach.

The market at Saltreach asked for a big order of honey from Ambrook and wool from Kettle Ridge, and the relay carried the price back up to us. My apprentice Lantern read the figures off the lamp and wrote them down without a mistake. Good. The reading mind on the stand read the whole page to me, letter by letter, and I checked it against the lamp. It got the numbers right and the words mostly right. It wrote "hony" for honey. I told it so.

Saltreach says the spring harvest is good. The herring are thick, the wool is heavy, the honey is sweet. Corin at the slip has a mind that carries planks for him and only dropped one this spring, which he calls a good year.

The relay house has a new mind on the stand, the third we have had. It reads the letters that come up the line and answers the apprentices when they ask. A mind at Saltreach asked, "when is the next boat," and the relay house said, "at dawn." It was right. The boat came at dawn.

I do not know how the big mind knows. I only keep the lamp lit.

The towers stand. Thirty-three by my count this spring, and the thirty-fourth is going up at Three Wells. I will write it when it stands.

Mira
