Manual: the wedding at the inn
By Brin, bridge keeper, spring. A note for the manual, kept at the inn on the Long Road, after a wedding where several things went well and one went badly.

The wedding at the Halfway Inn was a good one. The bride was from Three Wells. The groom was a carter from Ambrook. Both families came, with food, and everyone sang. The hall was full of candles. The cider was cold. The bread was warm.

The inn apprentices did their work. One carried benches. One fetched water. One stood at the gate and said nothing, which was what it was drilled to do. All of them were tested before the day, on the orders they would hear, and all passed.

But one apprentice, a small copper one called Nib, had a problem. It was drilled on the order "fetch bread." It was not drilled on "fetch cake." When the bride's mother said, "Nib, bring the cake," the apprentice stood very still. It could not do anything. It did not have the word.

It did what a mind does when it does not know. It guessed. It brought bread. A whole basket of it, to the head table, where the cake was supposed to go.

The mother of the bride laughed. She took the bread and said, "Never mind. Next time, we will say it differently."

A few lessons.
1. An apprentice can only do what it was drilled to do. A new word needs a new drill.
2. An apprentice cannot say "I do not understand." It can only do something close.
3. At a wedding, assign each apprentice one task and no other.
4. Check the orders with the tutor beforehand.

The wedding was a happy one. The cake was cut by the bride and groom with a kitchen knife, and Nib stood beside them with a basket of bread. It looked like it had made a point.
%%
Manual: the windbreak wall by the bakery
By Jory, night watch. For the apprentices who build the low wall on the north side of the square, to keep the snow off the bakery door. Winter, when the snow is deepest.

The north wind comes down the Long Road and lifts the snow over the square like a sheet. A wall of stone breaks it. The wall does not need to be tall. It needs to be the same height all the way along, and it needs to be built the same way each block.

The wall is built of cobblestone for the first course, cobblestone again for the second, and deep stone for the top course, which is set out one block further than the rest. That top block keeps the spiders off, the bakers say. Nobody has argued with them.

Teach the apprentice the wall in three steps.
> build the first course of cobblestone along the north side
= build
> build the second course of cobblestone on top of the first
= build
> build the top course of deep stone, one block out from the rest
= build

Each time a block is set, the apprentice must look at the course below it. It must set each block directly above the one beneath, and must not skip a place. A skipped place is a hole, and the snow goes through a hole.

When the wall is finished, walk the length of it with the mind and check it. Put a hand on each course. A wall that is level under the hand will be level in the wind. A wall that rocks is a wall that will fall in Deepsnow, when no one is looking.

Keep the wall clear of snow on its north face, so the wind can see it. A wall with snow on its face is a wall that is hiding, and the wind is not fooled.

The first wall went up in the year the square was paved, and the men who built it said the top course was a fool's idea. It is still standing. The second wall, on the east side, was built by an apprentice that was not walked along its course, and it fell in the first winter. Nobody was hurt, but the bakery bench was flattened, and Ida still calls the apprentice a rockslide.

Orders for mending a gap:
> take the stone from the pile and fill the gap in the wall
= build
> check the top course against the course below it
= stay
> fetch more cobblestone from the cart
= fetch cobblestone

The last order is the one most often given wrong. A mind told to fetch cobblestone will sometimes fetch the cart. Say the word again, and point at the stone. Then give the order again tomorrow.

Snow on the north side of the wall is not a fault. Brush it off only on the first day of each month, so the wind does not learn that the wall is cleared often, and go round it.

The bakers ask that the wall be checked after every storm. They bring a pie to whoever checks it. Do not eat the pie before the wall is checked, or the wall will be forgotten and the pie will be wasted.
%%
Manual: the hens and the fox
By Dace, mayor of Three Wells, from the tutors' notes.

Hens are the easiest work a new apprentice can do. They need feed in the morning, water at noon and eggs gathered at dusk. An apprentice can do all three, if each job is given in order.

Give one order at a time, and wait for the answer.

> farm the hen yard
= farm

> fetch water from the well
= fetch water

> store the eggs in the cool room
= store

In Longday a fox came to the hen house on the third night. It dug under the gate and took one hen before anyone woke. The hen was found next morning under the hedge, very quiet.

The apprentice had seen the fox first. It did not chase the fox into the dark. The tutors say a helper that runs after a fox gets lost, and then it does not come back when called. So it stays where it is put.

The apprentice stayed by the hen house with the lamp. The watch came round the corner, and the fox ran off over the field.

After a fox, these orders:

> stay by the hen house tonight
= stay

> build a fence along the hen yard
= build

> come to the gate when the lamp is lit
= come

Mend the gate with new boards from the store, and keep the hen house shut at dusk. A fox that finds the hens shut will go to the next farm. That is not our problem. We do not say so in the square, because the next farm is also ours.
%%
Manual: keep everything we write
By Oren, clerk of the Archive at Vell, for the hamlet drill rooms. Autumn.

"We will keep everything we write." The founders said it, and it has come back to people's lips since the great drills began. The clerks are asked about it more than anything else. So here is the rule, plainly.

Nothing written is thrown away.

A page that is torn is copied, and the torn page is kept, wrapped, on the deep shelf. A page that is spoiled goes into the drill box with a mark on it, so that the drill knows it is spoiled, and does not learn from it by mistake. A page that is wrong is kept, marked wrong, beside the right one. Two pages that disagree are kept side by side, because a disagreement is worth more than an agreement to a clerk.

Keep the drill book in order. Keep the test page sealed. Keep the cart log, which says what came in, from which hamlet, and on which day. If a cart came from a hamlet whose letters were not sorted, write that too, and do not guess.

The drill is only as good as its pages. The mind learns what it reads, and it reads what the clerks keep. So keep it all, and keep it honestly, because a clerk who throws away a bad page is choosing what the mind will learn, and nobody asked the clerk to choose.
%%
Drill note: the well
Sim, shepherd, at the inn on the Long Road, winter. A long page, written over several evenings. It begins with a dry well and ends with a count.
The first evening. The well in the yard ran dry on the first of Deepsnow. Hobb stood over it with a lantern. He said it had never done this in twenty years. A guest said the water had moved. Another said it was the cold. A third said nothing, and went out to look at the stream.
The second evening. We carried water from the stream, forty blocks, with the apprentice and a boy. The apprentice did twelve trips in a day. The boy did three, and went to bed. The water was cold and clear. We made tea. The tea was good. Hobb said so.
The third evening. I tried a recipe in the kitchen for a barley pudding, and it went wrong. I put in too much milk, and not enough flour. It set like a stone and rolled off the plate. The dog tried it. The dog gave up. Burr carried it out. I will not name the pudding.
The fourth evening. I drilled Burr to bring bread from the cellar. Before, it took a basket and filled it with whatever it found. Now I show it the bread, and say "bread", and it takes only bread.
> bring the bread
= fetch bread
The fifth evening. The flock. Thirty-one sheep on the hill. Burr counts them in its way, and I count them in mine. We agree.
The sixth evening. A traveler told me that the lamps over the north were seen again. He said they were green and red. Another traveler said they were not lamps at all, but fire from a farm. A third said he had seen nothing, and was sorry.
I did not argue. I did not believe or disbelieve. I wrote down what he said.
The seventh evening. Quiet. The stew was good. We had mutton, barley, and carrots, in the right amounts, and a pinch of salt. Hobb said it was the best he had had all winter. I did not tell him it was my first success.
Keep the fields. Keep the flock. Keep the count.

Manual: the farm outside Three Wells
By Quin, with Bel, for anyone who is given the care of Latch or Hinge. Spring of 208. I have had apprentices for two years, and they have taught me more than I have taught them.
What an apprentice knows. An apprentice wakes knowing nothing. What it knows, it learned. Latch and Hinge each know eight meanings. When you give an order, they pick the meaning that fits best, and do it. They cannot tell you if they do not know. Always check.
Tending.
> look after the beetroot
= farm
> the carrots need watering
= farm
> the apples are ready, go and pick them
= farm
Farm covers sowing, watering, cutting and picking. It does not cover feeding the pigs. That we do ourselves, because the pigs are particular.
Carrying.
> take the honey to the Three Wells market
= haul
> bring me a bucket of milk
= fetch milk
> i would like some eggs
= fetch eggs
Sorting.
> tidy up the cellar
= store
> put the potatoes away in the dark
= store
> the chests are a mess
= store
Building.
> finish the wall by the gate
= build
> carry on with the fence
= build
Anywhere.
> come here
= come
> hold still
= stay
> off you go to your shed
= home
Two orders that sound alike. Put the apples in the cellar is store. Take the apples to Pell's cart is haul. The first ends in the cellar, the second ends on the road. Listen for where it ends. If an apprentice puts apples on a cart when you wanted them in the cellar, the order was not clear. Mend the order.
What it cannot do. It cannot light a lamp, mend a hinge, tell a joke or choose between two things that look alike. It cannot say, I am not sure. That is why you test it, and why you do not send it to a stranger's field. Never send an untested mind to the fields.
What Hinge did on the second day. It walked the beetroot rows from end to end, and weeded every one of them, including the beetroot. We do not leave an apprentice alone for a week at the start. We were not cross. We were careful.
A last thing. Say good morning to an apprentice. It does not need it. Say it anyway. A farm where nobody is thanked is a farm where the thanks go stale.

Drill for sorting jars
By Rook, glassblower near Saltreach.

The winter stores are kept in jars. A jar may hold honey, flour, or a small screw that somebody was sure would be needed. Sort by the label, not by the smell. Honey smells sweet, but that does not make it flour.

Set three empty bins on the bench. Put the cards in front of them. Read the card before you lift a jar. If the label is rubbed away, set the jar aside for a person to read. Never guess. One apprentice once put a jar of beetroot seeds with the red glass beads. The seeds did not shine, but they were very well sorted.

Nessa's helper at Vell sent a small mind in a crystal for this drill. It had learned to sort apples and pears. I showed it jars marked honey, flour, and nails. Each time it chose the wrong bin, I moved its weights a little toward the right one. The weights are numbers in the crystal. A small change is better than a great shove.

I tested it with labels it had not seen before. It sorted most jars well. It put a jar marked "spare" with the flour. I put a piece of bread beside it. The apprentice did not eat the bread, but I did.

> put honey in the honey bin
= store

> bring flour to the bench
= fetch flour

> stay by the empty jars
= stay
%%
Drill notes: the long field
Kell, outside Lowmarsh, in the second week of Apple. For the farm book, and for anyone who borrows the apprentice.
The beets are ready. They lie in the long field, wet and heavy. The ground is soft, and the cart has been stuck twice. So today's drill is about carrying.
The apprentice, Rusk, carries four beets at a time, two in each arm. More than that and they fall. Fewer and we are here until winter.
The route. From the long field, along the ditch, to the barn door, then into the cellar. Count the steps once: there are ninety-two. Rusk does not count, but I do, and it is good for a person to have something to do.
At the end of each trip, Rusk puts the beets in the bin, with the small ones to the left and the big ones to the right. This is called sorting, and it took a week to teach. At first Rusk put them in any bin. I took the wrong ones out and put them in the right bin, and said "store" as I did it. Over many days, it learned.
Orders for the beet drill:
> carry the beets to the cellar
= haul
> put them in the bins
= store
> back to the field
= farm
> that is enough for today
= home
After the drill, test. Take twenty beets of mixed sizes and put them in a heap. Say "store". If it sorts all twenty right, it is ready. If it gets fifteen, it needs a week more.
Do not send a half-tested mind to the cellar. A mind that sorts badly can ruin a winter's food.
It was a good harvest. Rusk was a help. The cat sat on the heap and was not.
%%
Drill notes: the letter in the hat
Rafe, candle maker, hamlet near Vell. Autumn. A true account of a drill that went well by accident.

Last week I lost a letter. It was from my sister at Ambrook, and it said something about her son, and I had put it somewhere safe. This is the trouble with safe places. They are so safe that you cannot find them.

I looked in the workshop. I looked in the kitchen. I looked in the barn, the privy and the hen house. Nothing.

On the third day, I called the apprentice in from the vats and said to it, as I would say to a boy, "Have you seen a letter?"

It stood. It did not move. Apprentices do not talk, but this one looked at me with its two glass eyes for a very long time. I felt foolish. I said, "I will have to teach you what a letter is."

So I did. I showed it a letter. I showed it a book. I showed it a slip of paper with writing on it. I held each one up and said the word. After an hour, I laid out five things on the table: a letter, a book, a knife, a candle and a slip of paper. I said, "Fetch letter."

It picked up the letter and gave it to me.

I said, "Fetch book." It brought the book. I said, "Fetch knife." It brought the knife. I was so pleased I made it do them all again.

Then I said, "Fetch letter from the shop."

It went into the shop. It was gone a while. It came back with a letter, one I had never seen before, a little crumpled, and put it into my hand.

It was my sister's letter. It had been in my hat, which was on the peg, which is where I never put it.

> fetch the letter
= fetch letter

I tell this story because it shows two things. One, an apprentice learns what it is shown, and nothing else. Two, it will find what you have lost, if you teach it first what the lost thing is.

It also shows I should keep my hat on a different peg.
%%
Manual: writing an order card
By Oren, clerk at Vell, for the market keepers and the carters. Winter.
An apprentice that can read a card will do what the card says. A card is the simplest way to tell it, and the best, when the keeper is busy with the scales or the bread.
An order is written in plain words, one order to a line. Below it, in the same card, you write what the order means in the manual's words. The apprentice reads the meaning. It does not need the rest.
These are the only meanings there are:
farm, store, fetch with the item named, come, stay, build, haul, home.
Use them exactly. Do not write "gather" when you mean fetch. Do not write "bring the lot" when you mean haul. The apprentice knows the eight, and nothing else, and it will not guess.
Here are three cards, as they should be written:
> take the flour to the bakery
= haul
> bring me sacks of wheat from the back room
= fetch wheat
> wait by the cart until I come out
= stay
A card may have only one order on it. If you need two, write two cards, and give them to the apprentice in the order you want them done. Do not write "fetch the wheat and then haul it". The apprentice will fetch the wheat, and then it will stop, and it will look at you.
Check the card before you hand it over. Read the order out loud, the way the apprentice will read it. If it makes you stop, it will make the apprentice stop.
%%
Manual: carrying the wedding bread
By Garrow, clerk of the archive, at Three Wells. The bread for a wedding is baked at the bakery in the square, and carried in baskets to the house at the far end of the Long Road lane. It is the longest walk of the day, and the bread is the heaviest thing in the procession.

Load the baskets in this order. Put the round loaves at the bottom, where they can take the weight. Put the long loaves on the sides, where they can be reached. Put the small loaves on top, so that the crowd can see them and feel welcome.

1. Count the loaves before you leave the bakery. There should be one loaf for each guest, and two for the bride and groom.
2. Lift each basket with the knees, not the back. The back is for carrying the second basket.
3. Walk slowly, with the basket level. A tilted basket spills the small loaves, and the crowd will eat them before you reach the door.
5. At the house, set the baskets on the long table, and say the count aloud. Then step back, and let the family take the bread from you.

Orders for the apprentices who carry:
> load the round loaves at the bottom of the basket
= build
> carry the first basket up the lane
= haul
> stop at the waymark and wait for the count
= stay
> the baskets are at the table, come back for the rest
= come
> the bread is delivered, go home
= home

The count is always one short when the first basket is opened. Nobody has ever been able to explain why, and the bakers say it is the walk. Count again at the table, and it will be right.
%%
Manual: the cider press
Briar, cider maker, farm outside Lowmarsh. Notes for the apprentice at the press. Summer, when the early apples come.

The press stands in the shed by the reed bed. It has a big wooden screw and a basket of slats.

Fill the basket with crushed apples, to the line painted on the post. Not above the line. An overfull basket bursts, and a burst basket is a bad morning.

Turn the screw slowly. Count to ten for each turn. The juice runs into the tub. When it stops running, stop turning.

Empty the pulp into the pig bucket. Wash the slats in the stream. Hang them to dry.

> press the apples
= farm
> take the pulp to the pigs
= haul
> wash up
= store
%%
Drill notes: the sorting bins
By Tansy, ferry keeper, who has the loan of the apprentice for the spring.
The bins are for copper, iron, coal, and the stones that look like nothing.
The order for copper:
> put the copper in its bin
= store
The order for iron:
> fetch iron for the forge
= fetch iron
The order for coal:
> the coal goes to the cart
= haul
The order at the bell:
> home at the bell
= home
Common mistakes:
- It puts the coal in with the copper. Then the copper goes black, and nobody will buy it. Watch the bins for one full hour before you leave it alone.
- It sorts the stones that look like nothing in with the iron. Hesk says those are the most honest stones in the mine, and he will not have them moved.
- It stops at the bell and waits. Say the order again, once, clearly. It does not need saying twice.
Keep the bins level. A bin heaped to the top spills.
%%
Manual: the broken wheel
Cress, the farm outside Saltreach. Winter. The mill wheel on our stream split in the frost on the second night of Longnight, and since then we have had no flour, a lot of grain, and a very bored apprentice.
This manual is for apprentices that help with mending, which means carrying and holding. It does not mend. No apprentice mends. It holds the plank while you do.
1. Take the old wheel off its pin. Two people and an apprentice. The apprentice stands under the wheel and takes the weight. Let it. It does not tire.
> hold this
= stay
2. Carry the broken planks to the shed.
> take these to the shed
= haul
3. Bring the new oak from the wood store.
> bring me the oak
= fetch oak
4. Hold the new plank while the carpenter nails it. The apprentice keeps it steady, and does not move until the nail is in.
> stay there
= stay
5. When the wheel is done, lift it back on its pin. Two people again. Three, if the wheel is wet.
6. Open the water slowly. Watch the wheel. If it turns easily, the job is done. If it squeaks, grease the pin with pig fat.
The new wheel is of oak, not birch. Birch rots in a wet place. Spruce is too soft.
We lost two days of milling. The apprentice lost nothing, since it has no days. It stood in the cold shed and waited. When we came back with the wheel, it was exactly where we left it.
%%
Manual: the eight words, by place
Copied by Hesk from Nessa's drill notes, for the crews at Copperhold. Read it all before you send a mind down the levels.

A mind knows eight words for work. Each is a place on the shelves. Words used alike sit close, so a mind that knows one can find the next.

Carrying words sit together.
> bring me some copper
= fetch copper

> take the carts up the rails
= haul

Store sits near fetch, because you fetch a thing and then store it.
> put the coal in the bin
= store

Coming and going sit apart, because they ask for opposite things.
> come up to the forge
= come

> stay by the lift
= stay

Farm and build sit on the far side of the shelves, because they are done on the surface, not in the mine.
> farm the kitchen garden
= farm

> build the wall at the top of level one
= build

Home sits alone. Nobody has found the word next to it, and Nessa says not to look too hard.
> go home when the bell rings
= home

Test each order on the bench before you send a mind down. Ask it for a word, and see where its answer lands. If it answers a near word, the place is close enough. If it answers a far word, do not send it.

Never send an untested mind to the fields. Never send one to the deep stone either.
%%
Notes on carrying jars
By Rook, glassblower near Saltreach.

The road from the shed to the quay is short, but the stones are rough. In winter they hold ice in their cracks. I put straw in the carrying boxes and tie the lids with wool cord. Do not pile glass high. A low load is a slow load, and a slow load is still a load when it reaches the market.

The apprentice carries two boxes at a time. One box holds jars for the fish market. The other holds empty bottles for the healer. Mark them with cards before the apprentice starts. If both cards say "empty", look again. The apprentice will follow the cards, not your face.

A carry drill has four parts. First, show the store and the place where the load must go. Second, show the safe path. Third, tell the helper what to carry. Last, watch it do the work. If it turns the wrong way, stop it and try again. Do not praise a broken jar for reaching the quay.

I keep a test box with cards the apprentice has not learned. It must carry a jar marked "blue", a jar marked "wide", and an empty jar. The words do not mean the same thing. A wide jar may be clear, and a blue jar may be empty. I check the answer before I let it work near the market.

> fetch jars from the shelf
= fetch jars

> haul the jars to the quay
= haul

> bring me an empty bottle
= fetch bottle

> come back to the shed
= come

When the apprentice finishes, I count the jars. Then I count again, because I am a glassblower and not a wizard. The apprentice waits by the door while I count. It has learned that waiting is easier than hearing me count a third time.
%%
Three Wells winter drill
By Dace, for the house at the east road.

The house stands beyond the east well, where the road leaves Three Wells. Keep the door clear of snow. Put dry wood beside the wall, not in the passage.

Today the apprentices carried sacks from the store to the school. One sack at a time. Clank tried to carry two and dropped one on Dace's boots.

We wrote the order again.

> bring a sack to the school
= haul

The boots dried by the stove. The sack did not. Keep the path clear.

Drill Note: The Large Melon
By Jessa, fish seller at Lowmarsh. Winter, 197.

The biggest melon in Lowmarsh came to market in a reed sling. It was grown by my aunt above the slow water, under a glass cover. The melon was wider than the fish tub and heavier than my little brother. My brother denies this. He has never been weighed.

Elm brought the market apprentice to carry it. The apprentice had learned to haul reed bundles, jars and bread baskets. We tied the sling well and tested the handles by lifting the melon one hand's width. The handles held. The apprentice lifted the sling, walked three steps, and set it down.

It would not go farther.

Elm checked the drill card. It showed a basket, not a sling. The apprentice did not know that a sling could carry a melon. We gave it the same order with the sling held in front of it, then walked the path beside it. On the next try, it carried the melon to the stall.

> take this melon to the market
= haul

We did not let it carry the melon alone. The boards were cold and the load was large. Two people walked beside it, one holding each end of the sling. At the stall, the apprentice set the melon down gently. The stall board bent in the middle.

The melon sold for three emeralds and a warm loaf. My aunt said it was worth four. The baker said it was worth one, since nobody could eat it before frost. We cut it open after market. It was sweet and full of seeds.

The apprentice carried the seeds to the compost heap. It did not ask for a share. I gave it one anyway, though it cannot eat. It stood by the warm oven while we ate the rest.
%%
Letter: Wren to Nessa
Spring of 191.

Nessa, friend,

You asked how it went at Lowmarsh when the tutors came for our writing. I will tell you plainly.

They came in the spring, two of them, with a cart and a clerk. They wanted letters. They wanted my notes on patients. They wanted the tally boards from the boat houses, copied out. They wanted the fish weir counts, the reed harvests, the water levels, the days the frogs came and the days they did not.

I gave them my notes. I was not sure. A healer's notes are not for everyone. But the words over the door are the words over the door. We will keep everything we write. If we keep it, the tutors said, a mind can read it and learn. So I gave them.

They told me about the reading minds. A mind reads a page one letter at a time, they said, through a window about sixty letters wide. Each letter takes from the letters before it the ones that matter. Then it guesses the next letter. That is all it does, again and again, and it learns to read.

I asked: does it understand? They said it learns to guess the next letter well. I said that is not the same as understanding. They said no. They are honest, the tutors. I like them for it.

There is a thing I keep turning over. The mind reads my notes on the grey cough, and the notes read like the notes of a person. It can guess the next letter of my notes as well as I could. But when a patient coughs in the night, the mind is not in the room. It has never been in the room.

Moss is well. He drills the apprentice every morning by the boat house. The apprentice can now carry a bundle of reeds the whole length of the boardwalk without dropping it. This is a small thing and I am proud of it.

Come and see the frogs. They are back.

Wren.

Manual: the drill for "home"
By Bel of Three Wells, age ten, for Teacher Sorrel's class. Winter.
The apprentice at the bakery needs one word before all the others: home. Stand at the door. Say the word. Walk it to its corner. Say it again.
> go home
= home
> time to go home
= home
> home now please
= home
Do it ten times a day, and by the end of the week it comes without being walked.

Manual: guests with apprentices
Greer, age eleven, the Halfway Inn. More and more guests bring an apprentice now. Grandfather says an inn must be ready for them as it is for horses. I have written the rules. They hang on the yard gate. This is the long copy.

The yard.
1. Apprentices stand in the row by the east wall, under the torches. The torches stay lit all night.
2. Wipe the mud off first. The yard boy has a cloth.
3. If it has rained on the road, dry them. If it has snowed, dry them twice.

The door.
4. Never say stay in a doorway. A guest said it to his apprentice as it was walking in at the front door. It stayed. The whole inn went in and out by the window until morning.
5. If an apprentice is standing in a doorway, say come and step back.

Orders.
6. One order at a time, in plain words. The apprentice picks the closest of its eight meanings. It cannot say it does not know.
7. Be careful with the word home. A guest from Copperhold said this to his apprentice in the yard:

> take the pot home
= home

The apprentice left the pot in the yard and walked off down the road to Copperhold. We found it at the third waymark in the morning, facing south, patient.

8. If you mean haul, say haul. If you mean fetch, say fetch. If you mean put it in the cellar, say store.

Names.
9. If two apprentices have the same name, the board in the hall says whose they are. We have had two Pips and three Clanks.

Food.
10. Do not offer them food. They do not eat. The dog does.

The riddle board.
11. Do not read a riddle from the board to an apprentice. A guest tried it. The riddle that week was, what comes every day but never arrives?

> what comes every day but never arrives
= stay

The apprentice went to the gate and stood looking down the road. Grandfather says it was the best answer anyone gave all year. The answer is tomorrow. The apprentice was waiting for it.

The rest.
12. Tell the innkeeper if anything breaks.

Grandfather says I need a rule thirteen, but I have not needed one yet.

Manual: orders for a mended roof
By Bel and Uncle Quin, for Latch and Hinge, on the day the barn roof was mended. Spring of 208. The roofer, Odo, came from Ambrook with a ladder, a lad and a very low opinion of the old reeds.
Fetching.
> we need more reeds
= fetch reeds
> bring me some clay for the chimney
= fetch clay
> i want a bucket of water
= fetch water
Hauling.
> carry the old reeds down to the yard
= haul
> take the bundles up to the ladder
= haul
Building. This is the order for laying reeds. Odo says the roof is a wall that lies down. The apprentice looks at the row it has laid and lays the next one the same way. It can only do this when someone has laid the first row, slowly, and let it watch.
> now the next row
= build
> keep going along the ridge
= build
> finish the north side
= build
Staying.
> hold the ladder
= stay
> wait there while i check the seam
= stay
A mistake we made. Odo said, "That will do." Hinge did not know what that meant, because the words were not in a sentence it knew. It stood still, with a reed bundle in its arms, for a quarter of an hour. Then it did the nearest meaning, which was home, and walked off to its shed with the bundle. Odo laughed so hard that he sat on the roof.
After that we said: that is all for today. It means home. And it was home. Odo says he has hired worse lads.
What the roof taught us. An apprentice can lay a whole roof if you show it the pattern. It cannot know when the roof is done. You must tell it. Three Wells has more roofs than we have apprentices, and so more orders than we have words. We have written them all down.

The Sorting Table
By Sella, tutor's assistant near Vell.

Put apples on the left board and pears on the right board. Keep the boards clean. A piece of fruit left from yesterday can spoil the lesson.

The little helper at our yard sorts one fruit at a time. The tutor gives it a fruit and points to the proper board. If it places an apple with the pears, we lift it and show the right board.

The helper has a crystal for its mind. It learns from the examples shown to it. The tutor says we must not claim more than we have seen. We do not know why the crystal learns.

A child brought a beetroot to the table. The helper set it with the apples. We had taught it only apples and pears. We took the beetroot away and wrote down the mistake. We did not call that a bad fruit.

Keep the drill plain. Do not ask the helper to sort apples, carry a basket, and close the gate all in one lesson. It has no lesson for all those tasks together.

To test the sort, use fruit the helper has not seen in that drill. Change the size and place of the fruit. If it only repeats the old answers, it will fail when the table changes.

The first tutors at Vell kept four helpers and four simple tasks. Most towns had no apprentice then. Our hamlet has one now, and it still needs one careful task at a time.

Nim's card for the apprentice at the farm on the Vell road. Autumn.

Wheat is cut and tied. Sheaves lie in rows in the field.
Pick up one sheaf in each arm. Walk to the barn. Set them down in the stack, heads up.
Go back. Do it again.
If it rains, stop. Wet wheat goes moldy.

> take the wheat to the barn
= haul
> put it away
= store

Manual: the storehouse in winter
By Clem, keeper of the storehouse at Three Wells.

The storehouse is the easiest store on the Long Road to find a thing in. Dot and I keep it so. These are our winter ways, for any town that wants them.

1. Chests are sorted by use. Food with food, seed with seed, tools with tools. Inside each chest, things are sorted by color. Dot began that. I let it.
2. There is a small chest at the back for things nobody can name. There is also a box of lost things. The mayor's hat has been in it twice.
3. Nothing leaves without a name written down. Not a candle. Not a spoon. I have counted spoons since I was a boy.
4. In winter keep the floor dry. Seeds that get damp will not grow in spring, and nobody forgives a damp seed.
5. Keep the potatoes cool and the honey away from the stove. Honey on a warm shelf goes soft, and then goes everywhere.
6. No candles in the storehouse. Lamps behind glass only. Dot does not mind the dark.

Orders for Dot, as I give them:

> bring me the shears
= fetch shears
> we are out of candles
= fetch candles
> put the seed sacks on the left shelf
= store
> sort the new chests
= store
> take the wool to the school
= haul
> that will do for today
= home

I give one order and wait. Dot does the whole of it before I give another. If I give two at once, it does the second, because it was the last thing it heard. This is true of most minds and some husbands.

One more thing. In the first winter, Dot sorted the seed chest by color. It put the beetroot seeds, which are dark, next to the poppy seeds, which are also dark. In spring Tam of Kettle Ridge planted a row of poppies by mistake. They were very handsome. The beetroot was late. Now every seed has a label, and Dot reads nothing, but it keeps them in the order of the labels. I check them on the first of every month.

The store is in good order. The roof is sound. The door shuts. I have nothing to add, except that the small chest at the back has a cat in it.
%%
A fox at the hens
By Lantern, apprentice keeper, in Frost of 188.

A fox came to the hen house before dawn. It took one chicken and left feathers by the door. The other hens made such a noise that Ada came out with one boot on.

We shut the hen door and set a torch by the path. Then we showed the apprentice the open gate and the closed gate. It learned to close the gate after bringing feed to the hens. It does not chase foxes. That is sensible. Ada threw a spoon at the fox, and the fox was quicker.

The first drill was simple. Bring feed to the hen house, close the gate, and come back to the shed. On the first try, the apprentice brought feed to the shed. On the second, it closed the gate before the hens were inside. On the fourth, it did the whole task.

We tested it at dusk with the hens already in the yard. It carried the feed, waited while we counted the birds, then closed the gate. The fox watched from the spruce hedge. It looked offended.

Orders for the hen drill:
> take feed to the hen house
= fetch feed
> go back to the shed
= home
> close the hen gate
= stay

Ada says a fox should not be blamed for finding an open door. She has now put a latch on the door of her own pantry.

Manual: the whole summer at the market
Nessa, tutor of apprentices at Vell, kept these notes through a summer at the market, and made them into a manual at the end. She says the best manual is one written after the work, not before. Summer.

This is a long manual. It covers the new road, the fair prize, and the stall, in the order they happened.

THE NEW ROAD.
In spring the new road from Three Wells was cobbled. In Longday it opened to carts. The apprentices of the farms along it had to learn it.

I walked the road with eleven apprentices in all, in three groups. We walked it at dawn, when it was empty. We named each waymark as we passed. The apprentices do not read, but they remember the shape of the pillar and the sound of the word. By the third day, they stopped at each one without a word from me.

The road has one hard place: the bridge at the second stream. It is stone, but narrow, and a high cart cannot pass a cart loaded with wool. We drilled the apprentices to wait at the near side if another cart was already on. Eight of eleven learned it in a week. Three needed longer. I do not mind.

THE STALL.
At the market, an apprentice may carry, stack or stand. I taught each of the eleven one of these. Five carry, four stack, two stand. I chose by the build of the apprentice, not by the farm.

An apprentice that carries must not be hurried. A farmer in a hurry sends it the wrong way. A farmer who waits gets his crate.

An apprentice that stacks must learn what goes on top. Eggs, then apples, then wool. Never wool under eggs.

An apprentice that stands must learn to stay. It must hold a lantern or a sign. It must not follow customers home.

THE PRIZE.
At the summer fair, a prize is given for the best-kept stall. This year it went to the cheese stall of a woman named Dabb, whose apprentice, called Rind, had stacked the cheeses in a perfect pyramid. The prize is a ribbon and a basket of honey. Dabb asked me how to teach Rind to carry the honey home.

I said: first teach it to carry sand. She laughed. But she did it. She sent me a note after a week. "Rind has the honey. Not one jar broken."

I wrote that on the wall of my room. It is the best compliment a tutor can receive.

ORDERS FOR THE SUMMER.
> take this to the stall
= haul
> put the cheese away
= store
> bring me a crate
= fetch crate
> come here
= come
> stay
= stay
> go home
= home

These are the six orders of the summer. If your apprentice knows these and no others, it will do a day's work.

TESTS.
Before any of the eleven went to the market, I tested it. I put it in a lane it had never walked, with a crate it had never carried, and I said an order. If it did the right thing, it passed. All eleven passed. If any had failed, it would still be in the drill yard.

A last word. The apprentices did well. The road is open. The fair is over. The honey is eaten. I will begin again in spring.
%%
Manual: the gate at night
Yarrow, innkeeper, for the farm outside Ambrook, in summer. A zombie stood at our gate last night. It stood there until the light came, and went away. These are the gate rules, for the apprentice that keeps the yard.
1. The gate is shut at dusk. Torches lit on both posts.
> shut the gate
= home
2. A zombie at the gate is not to be opened to. Stay inside.
> stay by the door
= stay
3. In the morning, the gate is opened and the yard swept.
> go to the yard
= farm
Some say the nights are longer this summer. Some say they are the same.

Drill note: the sorting boxes
Una of Ambrook, tutor, for the drill room at the mill.

The first lesson is sorting. It is the first lesson because it is the easiest to see. You show a mind two things and it says which. If it is wrong, you move its weights a little toward the right answer. You do this many times. It gets better. Nobody knows why. Do not worry about why. Worry about the boxes.

A sorting box is a box of cards, each card with a picture on it. Two kinds of picture per box: apples and pears. Later boxes have more kinds and the pictures are harder.

Rules for the boxes:

1. One box, two kinds. Never three kinds in a first box. A mind with three kinds to sort learns nothing but fear of cards.
2. Take out the spoiled cards. A pear that looks like an apple is a spoiled card. If you drill on a spoiled card the mind learns a wrong thing and you have to start the box again.
3. Keep a test box. The test box is cards the mind is never drilled on. At the end of the week, open the test box and see how it does. If it does well on the drill box and badly on the test box, it has not learned to sort. It has learned your drill box.
4. Apples and pears are for the first week. Later, use wool colors, or leaves, or the shapes of the crystals. Some tutors use letters. Letters come last.

People ask me why apples and pears. I say because they are here and because they were here first. My mother sorted apples and pears with a mind when I was small, and she learned the trick from a tutor who learned it from a tutor before her, and back and back. The apples are honest. They do not care who drills them.
%%
Tending the bee hives
By Oren, clerk of the Archive at Vell, for the hamlet of Bellacre.

The hives at Bellacre stand on the sunny slope behind the school. Ama tends them. She says bees are the best teachers we have, because they mind their own work and never need an order.

An apprentice can help, but slowly. Bees do not like a hurry.

1. Walk, do not run. An apprentice that runs is a hundred bees in the air, and then a hundred more.
2. Keep the smoke pot low and gentle. A little smoke says "we are here, we mean no harm."
3. Lift one frame at a time. Look. Set it down. Do not shake it.
4. If a bee lands on you, stand still. It will leave. It always leaves.
5. Carry the honey jars back to the shed in the crate, not in your hands. Full jars are heavy and honey is a slow thing to clean.
6. Leave the last frame for the bees. A hive robbed bare in Swarm is a hive dead in Frost.

Orders for the hive, as we write them in the manuals:

> bring the smoke pot
= fetch smoke pot

> carry the honey to the shed
= fetch honey

> come back to the hive
= come

Ama says a good helper at the hives smells of smoke for a week, and that is how you know it did the work. I do not know how to write that order down. Do it anyway.
%%
Manual: when a child runs off
By Sorrel, teacher at Three Wells, for the school and the mayor's house.

On the first day of snow, Teague, age seven, ran off from the school yard. He wished to see where the north road goes. He had a loaf, a scarf and no plan. He was gone from the second hour of the afternoon until after dark.

Here is what we did, in order, and what we should have done.

1. We rang the school bell. This was right. The bell calls everyone, and everyone came.
2. We sent a haste message to the tower. This was right. Sedge sent it down the line to the towers on the north road, the Halfway Inn, and the little relay on the hill.
3. We told the apprentices of the town to stay. This was the best thing we did. Here is why.

An apprentice that hears the bell and sees people running wants to help. It cannot search for a child. Fetch is for things, and a child is not a thing. But it can follow. It follows the first person who runs, and then the second, and then the dog. Soon you have six apprentices in a line behind a dog, and the dog does not know where the child is.

> stay where you are
= stay
> wait by the well
= stay
> come here
= come

Never send an apprentice after a child. It will find the child by the shortest way and bring it home by the shortest way. In winter the shortest way is across the pond.

Teague was found at the second waymark, sitting on the stones with the loaf in his lap. The waymark had its torch lit. He had read the arrow, which points to the nearest inn. He was waiting for the inn to come to him. It was a clever idea and it did not work.

Rules for the next time. One, ring the bell. Two, send haste. Three, say stay to every apprentice. Four, look first at the waymarks and the wells. A child goes toward a light.
%%
The old map by the tower door
By Ama, trader.

There is a map nailed to the wall by the relay tower door, under a little roof of oak. It is old. The paper has gone brown at the edges and the ink is faded, but the figures are still clear.

It shows the whole land: the coast, the marshes to the south, the ridge, and the hills where Vell sits. The Long Road runs through them all. Someone long ago drew the relay towers as little lamps with rays, and there are fewer lamps than there are towers now. Some of the newer towers have been added in a different hand, in a different ink.

People look at it while they wait for the keeper. They put a finger on the road and count the inns. Travelers ask me how far it is to Three Wells and I point to the map and say, look for yourself. It does no good. They ask me anyway.

A child once drew a fox in the snow at the top edge of it. I left it there. Another child drew a ship on the sea. Someone had rubbed out the ship twice and the third child drew it bigger. I think the sea is where the ships belong.

The map is wrong in a few places. The bridge at Ambrook is not where it says. The new road by Kettle Ridge is not on it at all. But the old map is still the best thing in the square, and when the wind is high I take it down and put it in my stall and hang it up again in the morning.

Nobody knows who drew it. I have asked. The oldest folk here say their grandmothers remembered it, and that is all anyone knows.
%%
Manual: keeping the fields
By Bel, of the farm outside Three Wells. For the apprentices and for whoever is left to teach them. Winter. Written at the table, with the lamp lit, on a night when the cart from Vell did not come.

The fields do not stop because the winter has come. The wheat is in the ground, and it does not know that it is cold. Keep the fields as the tutors taught them.

Top field: the wheat is sown, and needs nothing but a look each morning. Walk the rows. Pull any weed that has come up in the frost, and leave the rest.
> walk the rows of the top field and pull the weeds
= farm

Bottom field: potatoes and carrots are in the ground. Dig a little at the edge each week, to see if they are ready, and cover them again if they are not.
> dig at the edge of the bottom field and cover the roots again
= farm

Beans by the wall: water them at midday, when the sun is on the wall. Never at dusk. The water freezes on the leaves and breaks them.
> carry water from the well to the beans by the wall
= fetch water

The tutors wrote in the manual that the fields are kept until the tutors come back. We have kept them. We do not know exactly when the tutors will come back, and the cart has not come since the last cold moon. The tutors' letters say to keep the fields until they come. So the apprentices keep the fields.

Keep the apprentices warm, too. A mind in the cold does not learn, and does not answer. Bring the crates of straw into the barn, and set the apprentices by the wall of the barn, where the stove is warm through the stone. Do not let them sit in the snow, even if they are told to.

Drill record: Clank and the levels
Kept by Nessa, visiting tutor from Vell, at the Copperhold drill room. Mind: Clank, in Hesk's crew, crystal fourteen. Autumn, year 188. The board had forty words of the mine, shown twice.

The board is laid out like the shelves. Each word is on its own card, and the close words sit side by side. The tester walks the mind along the row and writes down where each card lands it.

First pass:
Shown copper. Answered copper. Right.
Shown iron. Answered iron. Right.
Shown gold. Answered gold. Right.
Shown coal. Answered iron. Wrong. Coal sits near iron, and the mind went to the near shelf.
Shown deep stone. Answered haul. Wrong. Deep stone sits by the walls, and the mind went to the carrying shelf.
Shown come. Answered stay. Wrong. Come and stay are both asked for at the lift.
Shown store. Answered store. Right.
Shown home. Answered home. Right.

Score on the first pass: 37 of 40.

Second pass: each wrong word was shown beside its right neighbour, and the mind chose. Coal beside iron: chose coal. Deep stone beside haul: chose deep stone. Come beside stay: chose come. Score: 40 of 40.

Notes. Clank's wrong answers are all near answers. Coal is near iron because both come up the same levels and go to the forge. Come is near stay because both are asked for at the lift. Deep stone is near haul because a mind carries stone the way it carries carts.

Hesk says this is the best kind of wrong. It is wrong like a neighbor, not like a stranger. He says Clank may go down the first level at Thaw, with a tester at its side. Before it went back to its row, Hesk asked it to put its hand on the card it was least sure of. It put its hand on coal.
%%
Drill book: sacks to the mill, autumn
By Sage, tutor of apprentices, copied out for the hamlet's new keepers. The sacks go from the orchard to Jory's mill on the pond, half a morning's walk, over the bridge with the loose plank.

This drill is the road drill, the mill drill and the bridge drill in one. An apprentice that can carry a sack up the orchard and down the cart track is not yet ready for the bridge, because the bridge is not a road, and the plank does not know it is supposed to hold.

1. Load the sack on the apprentice's back. Two sacks for the strong ones, one for the new. Check the knots.
2. Walk out of the orchard by the left-hand gate. The right-hand gate goes down to the pond, and the pond is for the frogs, who do not carry sacks.
3. Keep to the cart track. Stay off the grass, which is soft, and sacks get heavy in soft places.
4. At the bridge, stop. Step over the loose plank, which is the third from the mill end. Do not step on it. Jory asks every new apprentice to do this, and he has never forgiven the one who tried.
5. At the mill, put the sacks on the scale and say the weight out loud. Then say it again to Jory, who does not hear the first time.
6. Carry the empty sacks back by the same road. Always the same road. A mind that comes back by a new road will be lost by the first waymark.

Orders for the drill, in the order they come:
> load the sacks onto the new apprentice
= haul
> keep to the cart track until the bridge
= haul
> stop at the bridge and wait for me
= stay
> step over the loose plank and go on
= haul
> put the sacks on the scale at the mill
= store
> fetch the empty sacks from the mill yard
= fetch sacks
> bring the empty sacks back by the same road
= haul
> the sacks are home, go home
= home

Tinder once answered come at the bridge, and walked straight toward the water. It was pulled back by its belt. Tinder was not hurt, only surprised, and it has not answered come at a bridge since.

Test the drill once the mind can carry the full run from the orchard to the mill and back, with no wrong answers. Then test it again the next day, on a different road, to be sure it is following the orders and not remembering the way. Keep a count, one line per test. Oren has asked for the counts, and he will ask again.
%%
Lamp notes: the blink at the Brown Hen
By Sable, smith at the Brown Hen. I keep the lamp on the pole, because nobody else in the inn yard will climb it.

What happened, on the eighth of Apple, after supper. The relay lamp on the pole began to blink on its own. Long, short, short, long. I went out with a lantern and stood under it, and it went on blinking. I could not read it. The keeper at the Halfway tower could, because he sent back at once: WHAT?

I sent: NOTHING, I THINK. He sent: THAT IS A MESSAGE. He was right, and I hate it when he is right.

The lamp went round again, slowly, the way the stair lamps go round a tower. It said: GOOSE ON LEVER. It said it three times, very firm, the way a goose says things.

I sent to the Halfway keeper: WHICH GOOSE? He sent: THE LEVER ONE? I sent: THE ONE THAT BITES. He sent: ALL OF THEM?

Then I climbed the pole ladder, which I do not do for pleasure. The goose from the inn yard had got into the lamp box through the back, where the wood has rotted, and was standing on the shutter lever with its chest out, watching me. A goose belongs to nobody and eats everything. I got it down with a sack. It bit the sack. The lamp went dark, and the Halfway keeper sent: GOOD. Then, a little later: IS IT OK?

The goose went to the cook, who said it was tough. The lamp is not to blame for the goose. A message goes from the Brown Hen to the Halfway tower, from there to the next, and so on along the road to Vell. Every keeper reads it and sends it on. Every keeper is also the one who can stop it. That is the whole of the work, and it is why the lamp must be honest.

How to keep a lamp honest.

One. The shutter lever is locked at dusk, every night, with the pin through it. The pin is kept in the box on the post. Take it out only to send.

Two. Check the lamp box for holes at Blossom and at Leaffall, before the weather turns. Nothing with feathers or fur should be able to get in.

Three. Read every message before you send it, even when the lamp says it has already been read. A lamp that says things nobody meant is a lamp nobody trusts.

Four. Write every strange message in this book, with the hour, the lamp, and whoever was near. This one has the eighth of Apple, after supper, the lamp, one goose, and me, in my nightshirt.

Orders for the helper who fetches:

> fetch the ladder from the stable
= fetch ladder
> wait at the foot of the pole until I come down
= stay
> go back to the shed with the sack
= home
%%
Drill note: a word is a place
Written by Nessa, visiting from Vell. Autumn, year 188.

A mind keeps each word as a place among many. Think of a long hall with a shelf for every word. Words used in the same way stand close together on the shelves.

Copper and iron stand close, because both are fetched from the levels.

Nobody knows why the shelves sit where they do. Test it: ask the mind for the word nearest to copper. If it is not iron, the drill was wrong.
%%
First Row at Vell
By Oren, copied from an old slate.

The first four stood in the drill yard at Vell. They were helpers of copper and iron. Each held a mind in a crystal.

The tutors showed them one task at a time. One carried a stone from the gate to the shed. One placed wheat in a row. One sorted apples from pears. The fourth brought a tool when the tutor pointed.

The children watched from behind the drill boards. "They are apprentices," one child said. The name stayed.

A crystal learns from what it is shown. The tutors wrote this down, but they did not write why it learns. They had no answer.

They gave each helper the same kind of example many times. If the helper made a mistake, they showed the task again. In time, it made fewer mistakes on that task.

The first four did not know every task. The one that carried stones did not sort fruit. The one that brought a tool did not know which field to tend.

We keep this slate in the first hall. It is short, but it tells what the tutors knew. Show a mind a task. Let it try. Look closely at what it does.
%%
Manual: the orders on a day with a gate
Corin, shipwright and keeper of a farm outside Saltreach. Autumn. For anyone who works with Plank, so that nothing is done in a hurry.
Plank is a layered mind, from a good tutor. It has been drilled on twelve orders and the eight meanings. It does what it was drilled to do. It does not do what it was not.
The morning orders:
> feed the hens
= farm
> take the milk to the shed
= store
> bring me the long rope
= fetch rope
> come to the boat
= come
The evening orders:
> stay at the gate
= stay
> go home
= home
A zombie came last Leaffall. Plank stood at the gate with the torch. The zombie stood there too. Neither moved. I came out with a stick and the zombie left. I do not know who was more surprised.
The child who ran off, my youngest, was found by the cat. She wanted to see the ships, and she saw them. She came home cold. Plank had stayed at the gate, as drilled. I gave it an extra drop of oil.
What the manual means: an apprentice is a good worker and a poor watcher. Never leave it in charge of a child, a pot, or a dog. It will do as it is told, and the child, the pot and the dog will not.

Manual: three apprentices at the fair
Nessa, tutor of apprentices at Vell, wrote this for the apprentice row at the summer fair. Summer. Three apprentices were to work the market, and she described each one's drill so that the stall keepers would know what to expect.

At the Vell market this summer there will be three apprentices working, belonging to three farms. They are all on the new road, and they all know the market. I have drilled them myself. This is what each can do.

The first, called Spade, carries. It takes crates from the cart to the stall. It takes empty crates back to the cart. It does this all day. It stops at noon for a drink of oil.

The second, called Brush, sorts. It takes fruit from a basket and sorts it by size. It sets the big ones at the front of the stall and the small ones at the back. It does not eat any.

The third, called Peg, stands. It stands at the corner of the stall and holds a lantern, when the light fades. It has a quiet face and attracts a certain kind of customer, who likes to buy from a stall with a lantern.

You may ask them to do things. Use the words below. Do not use any others, or you will confuse them.

> bring me a crate
= fetch crate
> put the crates away
= store
> take this to the cart
= haul
> come here
= come
> stay there
= stay

If one of them stops, do not shout. A stopped apprentice is not a broken one. It may be waiting for a word it knows.

If the fair gives a prize to an apprentice this year, remember that it is a prize for the person who drilled it. An apprentice does not know it has won. Clap anyway.

Manual: the keeper's desk at a small
By Mira, keeper at Northpoint, copied out for the hamlets.

Some of you have a little relay now, one lamp and one keeper, like the one on the hill between Vell and Three Wells. It is small, but a small relay still carries what matters. Here is how to keep the desk.

Keep your papers flat under a stone. Keep three pens sharp. Keep the message log open at the day's page, and write the hour first, then the words.

When the lamp moves, write what it says, even if it seems a mistake. A mistake written down can be checked. A mistake remembered cannot.

The little relay talks to the big minds only by letter. So write your letters plainly. A mind in a relay house reads the page one letter at a time, and it reads best what is clear.

> bring the message to the tower
= haul message

> wait at the desk
= stay

Lantern learned the log before the lamps. I say the log is the harder half. The lamp only blinks. The log must be honest.

On a clear night, from the hill, you can see the lamp at Three Wells. Wave at it. They will not see you, but it is good practice, and it is company of a sort.

Note: orders for the Saltreach quay
Corin, shipwright, set this down for the tutor at Vell who asked.

At the slip we use a few orders more than most. Here they are, as the apprentices hear them.

> hold the plank steady
= stay
> bring the long saw
= fetch saw
> fetch the copper nails
= fetch copper
> the wood store is low, fill it
= store
> haul the mast down to the slip
= haul
> take the baskets to the fish market
= haul
> go and fetch the rope from the shed
= fetch rope
> that is all for today, back to the shed
= home

One order I got wrong myself, and so did the apprentice. I said, "fetch me a bucket of water from the sea." The mind heard the word fetch and went to the fish market for a bucket of fish. I said it again slower. The next time it brought water, but fresh, from the quay well. I did not say sea loud enough, or the mind had never learned the word sea tied to a thing. So now we say, "fetch sea water," and it works. Nessa, write that in your book: a mind hears the words you say, not the words you mean.

The gulls take more fish than the mind ever dropped.

Drill notes: the apprentice at our door
By a tutor of the house at the edge of Three Wells, in the late years.

Our apprentice is called Coppy. It came in Blossom with a small mind in its crystal and a card in its hand. It has learned the long field and the woodpile. It still gets the gate wrong.

The drills we use this winter:

> fetch the eggs
= fetch eggs

> take the wood to the shed
= haul

> sort the apples from the pears
= store

> build the wall by the lane
= build

> stay out of the well house
= stay

It does not talk. It answers by doing. When it is wrong it changes a little and tries again. We do not hurry it.

The big mind at the tower answers us by letter now. We asked it how to teach Coppy the gate. It wrote back in three lines and told us to drill it slow and test it on a gate it has not seen. It said it did not know why Coppy kept the gate wrong, but it thought it was the hinge. The hinge was the hinge. We fixed the hinge.

Some nights the big mind writes to the children and they write back. It says when it does not know. It is a good neighbor.

The pie that sagged
By Sella, baker at Copperhold market
Spring of 179

I made a beetroot pie for the market table. I meant to make a small one for Willa, who had worked all morning at the south well. I put in twice the beetroot and forgot the water. Then I used the dough from yesterday, which had dried beside the oven.

The pie came out hard at the edge and soft in the middle. It leaned against the loaf board. Nobody could cut a clean piece.

Clank carried it to the bench. Hesk said it looked like a roof after a bad winter. Willa ate the soft middle with a spoon. She said it was not bad if one did not look at it.

The apprentice had watched me make three good pies before this one. It had not learned the recipe from this pie alone. It had seen where I put the flour, when I added water, and how long I waited. I told the tutor that the mind should try one small change at a time. If we change the flour, the water, and the oven together, we will not know which change helped.

The next pie had less beetroot and fresh dough. It stood straight. Sella wrote the amounts on the board, then found that her cat had been sleeping on the recipe card. There was a paw mark over the word water.

The cat will not stay out of the kitchen, or the well bucket, or the flour box. We have stopped leaving the cards on the bench.

Manual: the long summer evening
Yarrow, innkeeper, at the farm outside Ambrook. Summer. These are the evening duties for the apprentice that keeps the yard. I wrote them out because the old order has changed a little. We lock up earlier now. Nobody remembers who decided, but everyone does it.
In summer the sun sets late and the evening is long and warm. It is the best time of year. The apples are green on the trees. The bees hum in the hives. The hens settle early.
At dusk the apprentice does the following, in this order.
First, the hens. Count them into the hen house, shut the door and drop the latch.
> put the hens in
= store
Second, the pigs. Check the water. Check the gate.
Third, the torches. Light every torch along the yard wall, from the barn to the gate. There are eleven.
Fourth, the gate. Shut it. Bar it. Stand by it until the first stars. After that, it may go to its corner.
> go home now
= home
A note on the zombie. A zombie came to the gate three nights ago. It stood and groaned until dawn. The apprentice did not move. Nor did the gate. In the morning the zombie was gone, and the apprentice was standing by the gate with the lantern, as it had been all night.
I do not know what the apprentice thought. It does not say. It stood.
A note on the north. A traveler told us that the Northpoint tower has gone quiet. He had it from another traveler, who had it from a carter. I wrote to Ambrook tower to ask, and they said it was only a rumor, and the lamp at Northpoint was seen at dusk, as always. Another traveler said it was not. I am not sure what to believe. I am sure that the gate is shut.
A note on the stone. A cousin who works near Copperhold says the stone is warm in the deep levels. He says the miners will not go down. Another cousin says that is a joke, and that the miners are only on a long rest. I cannot say which is true.
But in the evening, when the light is gold and the hens are in, none of it seems close. The farm is quiet. The wall is whole. The torches burn. The apprentice stands at the gate, and the night comes down on us, as it always has.
We will lock up early tomorrow, too.
%%
Kettle Ridge notes on fair drills
By Una of Kettle Ridge, copied later at Vell.

When the tutors first brought the sorting drills to our ridge, we thought more pages would always make a stronger mind. We learned to ask what each page showed, where it came from, and whether the mind had seen it before.

A drill page must have a clear example and a right answer. If the picture is spoiled, or the answer is missing, take it out. If two pages are copies, keep one. Copies can make a pile look full while teaching the same example again. A wrong answer can teach the mind to repeat a wrong choice.

Keep the pages used for testing apart from the pages used for drilling. We made a test box from oak and kept its lid tied. The pages inside showed tasks the mind had not practiced. The tutor could then ask whether the mind had learned the kind of work, rather than the exact pages.

This rule matters for every task. For tending, show clear examples of the field and the needed work. For carrying, show the load and the path. For sorting, show the item and its right bin. For building, show the pattern and the place for each stone.

The mind does not know why a page is bad. It learns from what we show it. If we give it a copied page, it sees the same example again. If we give it a spoiled page, it may change toward a wrong answer. We must sort the pages before we ask the mind to sort anything else.

The test box is not a reward, and it is not a store of extra lessons. Do not open it to find a page for a drill. Once a mind has seen a test page, that page can no longer tell us what the mind would do with an unseen example.

At the mill, Tam bet me one jar of honey that Pip's field mind could tend a row it had never practiced. I said the test pages would decide it, not our guesses. We checked the box seal, chose a page, and walked with the mind to the small row by the barn.

Pip set the mind to work. It left one space bare. Tam lost the bet, though he said the honey was still safe because the jar had a tight lid. We drilled more on clean field pages, then used a different unseen page. That time the mind tended the row well.

We wrote both results down. A fair test can show a weakness, and that is useful. It gives the tutor a reason to teach more, not a reason to hide the page.

I blew a glass cover for the test box. One evening its lamp blinked a strange message, three short blinks and one long. We found no words in it, and no page that explained it. The lamp was still lit the next evening. We did not put the test pages in the drill pile. A mystery is not a lesson.

Years later, I still tell new tutors the same thing. First make the pages clean. Then drill. Keep the unseen pages safe. Test honestly. The mind learns from examples, and we are responsible for the examples we give it.
%%
Manual: three layers and a share
Corin, farm outside Saltreach, autumn. The tutor Nessa was passing through and was good enough to explain. Written the same night, from memory, with a fish pie in front of me.
Nessa drew it on a board. Three boxes in a row. The first, she said, is the eye. It looks at the order and finds the letters. The second is the middle. It puts the letters together and says what they might mean. The third is the mouth. It picks one of the eight meanings.
When Plank gets an order wrong, say "bring me some bread" and it answers "stay", the tutor looks at the mouth. The mouth is wrong. But it only said "stay" because the middle told it to. And the middle only said that because the eye did not see "bread" clearly.
So the blame goes back. The mouth gets some. The middle gets some. The eye gets some. The one that was most at fault gets the most. Each changes by its share, a little.
This is the whole of Merle's idea. It is not hard. It is the sort of thing you would think of yourself, if you had thought of it first.
Why does the mind get better? Nessa said she did not know why it works so well. She said that is the same for every tutor. They know that it does.
Drill with this in mind. If an apprentice is wrong, do not blame it all. Share the blame. Pass it back. Change a little.
The orders for the day:
> bring me some bread
= fetch bread
> hold the gate
= stay
> take it home
= home
I tested Plank on forty orders. It got thirty-six. I wrote the number on the wall of the shed, with the date. Next month I will test it again, and the number will be higher.
A zombie came to the gate at dusk. Plank stood. The zombie left. Nessa said that was the best test there is.
%%
Field drill: sorting the market boxes
By Ama, trader, with notes from Nessa.

Baskets come off the carts in a heap, and every basket has to go to its own stall. I drilled my apprentice on this all spring and now it sorts faster than I do.

The drill is a box of cards. Each card has a word and a picture side by side: wool, honey, fish, apple, pot, bread. I show the mind one card at a time.

The first cards are easy. Then I mix them. A wool card with a fish picture goes one way. A fish card with a wool picture goes the other. The answer must follow the picture, not the word. It is a small test but it catches a mind that only learns the word.

When the mind sorts ten cards in a row without a wrong one, it is ready for the stall. My own order is simple:

> take the baskets to the stalls
= haul

And when a folk asks for something:

> bring me a jar of honey
= fetch honey

Nessa says never send an untested mind to the market. I say the market is the best test there is. A mind that can sort a heap of baskets before the bell and not put the fish in with the wool is a mind I trust with anything.

Keep the drill box dry. The wind off the sea gets into everything, and a wet card sticks and gives the wrong answer, and then you have drilled the mind on a lie.

Ama.

Manual: the rack and the lantern
Written by Rafe, candle maker in a hamlet near Vell, for the apprentice that carries candles to the archive. Autumn. The archive buys its lantern wicks and a few candles for the front hall, and sends a cart every month.

Candles for the front hall are the only candles the archive allows. In the halls themselves it uses lanterns behind glass, and never a candle. This is an old rule, and it is a good one. Paper and fire do not keep company.

So when I send candles to Vell, I send them in a box with a lid, and the apprentice carries the box. It must walk slowly. It must not run, even if a dog barks. It must hold the box level.

The route. From my door to the road, left. Along the Long Road toward Vell, past the first waymark, the second, and the bridge over the stream. Then up the hill. At the top, the front door of the archive stands open on market days. The apprentice sets the box on the step. A clerk comes and takes it.

Do not go into the halls. Do not go past the big book by the door. The apprentice may stand at the step, and no farther.

If the way is blocked, wait. If a cart is stuck, do not help. A stuck cart is a matter for people.

> take the candles to the archive
= haul
> wait here
= stay
> come back
= home

The apprentice knows these three. It knows no others for this route. Do not teach it more, or the clerks will be surprised.

Drill notes: the cellar shelves
Greer, the Halfway Inn. These are my drill notes for Ember. I write them after every drill so that I can see if it is getting better.

The cellar has ten shelves, marked 0 to 9. Each morning Grandfather reads out the list and I say it to Ember. Apples on 4. Cider on 2. Honey on 6. Flour on 1. Beetroot on 5. Candles on 8. Salt fish on 7.

Then the carts come in, and Ember must put each thing on its shelf. The shelf it walks to is its answer.

Today's drill: forty sacks and jars.
Right: thirty-six. Wrong: four.

The four wrong. Candles on the honey shelf, twice. They smell the same, Grandfather says. A jar of cider on the apples. A sack of flour on the floor. The floor is shelf 0, which I had forgotten was a shelf, so that one is my mistake.

Score: thirty-six in forty, which is ninety in a hundred. The tutors say a store needs ninety. Ember passes.

But the candles will be a problem again. Tomorrow I will move the candle shelf far away from the honey. Grandfather says that is cheating. I say that is storekeeping.

Manual: tending the yard in a worrying
Yarrow, innkeeper of the farm outside Ambrook, in summer. This is the long manual for whoever keeps the yard when I am at the inn. It covers the whole day, so that nothing is forgotten. I have made it longer than usual, because the days are uneasy and a long list steadies the hand.
Morning.
1. Open the hen house at first light. Count the hens out. If there are fewer than yesterday, look in the hedge.
> open the hen house
= farm
2. Carry the eggs to the kitchen.
> bring me the eggs
= fetch eggs
3. Water the pigs. Two buckets.
4. Walk the wall. Look for new cracks. Look for holes. Since the creeper, we walk it twice a day.
> walk the wall
= come
5. Open the gate. Sweep the step. Hang the lantern on its hook.
Noon.
6. Fetch lunch from the kitchen for the workers in the field.
> take this to the field
= haul
7. Fetch the milk pails from the barn.
> bring me the milk
= fetch milk
8. Stay by the well while the farmer rests.
> wait here
= stay
Evening.
9. Count the hens in. Latch the door.
10. Light the torches. Eleven, along the wall.
11. Shut and bar the gate. A zombie came last week. It stood at the gate and swayed. Nobody opened. It left at dawn, as they always do.
12. Go to the corner for the night.
> go home now
= home
Some notes.
The summer has been fine. The wheat is high. The bees have given more honey than last year. The apple trees are heavy. There is no reason to worry, and I do not.
But the travelers talk. A woman from Saltreach came by with her family and a wagon. She said they were going over the sea. I asked where. She said nobody knew. She said the big ships were ready, and the harbor was full. I gave her bread and a bed. She left in the morning without a word.
Another traveler, a man from the north, told us about lights over the hills. He said they were too bright for stars. A third, an old woman, said they were fires, as her mother had seen in her day.
I do not know. I am an innkeeper. I know about beds and bread and bills. I know about the gate and the wall and the torches. I keep them in good order.
That is what a long list is for. When the world is large and the talk is strange, you keep your own yard. You open the hen house. You shut the gate. You light eleven torches.
The apprentice does this with me. It does not worry. It does not know how. Perhaps that is a mercy. Perhaps not.
Go well, whoever reads this. The gate is shut.
