Drill note: what to do when the order
Kell, farm outside Lowmarsh. Autumn. Written the day after the gift of a young apprentice from the cousins at Saltreach, and the first drill that went badly.
This is the most important page in the farm book. Read it twice.
Sometimes you say an order and the apprentice does something else. It is not stubborn. It is learning. A mind that is wrong has been drilled on too little, or on the wrong examples. A mind that is wrong is never to blame.
When it happens:
1. Stop. Do not say the order again, louder. Louder does not help. I have tried.
2. Look at what it did. Was it close? Did it fetch bread when you said fetch bran? That is a near miss, and it is a good sign.
3. Say the order again. Show it the right action, with your own hands. Say the meaning as you do it.
4. Try again. Wait. Watch. If it gets it right, say nothing. Silence is a reward.
5. Write it in the farm book: the order, the day, what it did. After a week, look back. You will see it is better.
Each time it is wrong, it changes a little. That is how it works. We do not know why. We know it does.
The orders to test with every day:
> bring me some water
= fetch water
> come here
= come
> build the wall
= build
> stay
= stay
> store the apples
= store
Count how many it gets right out of twenty. Write the number in the book. In a month, you will have a line of numbers going up. That is what a good mind looks like.
Never send an untested mind to the fields. If you are not sure, ask the cousin. She knows more than me, and says so.
%%
Manual: when an apprentice is wrong
By Quin, tutor of apprentices at the hamlet near Copperhold, who has been wrong himself more often than he would like to admit.
Every apprentice is wrong. This is not a fault. It is how the mind learns. The first answers will be wrong, and the next will be wrong, and then one day the answer will be right by accident, and then the day after by design.
When an apprentice is wrong, do the following, in this order.
First, look at the order. Was it clear? If you gave two orders at once, or if you used a word the apprentice has not learned, the fault is yours. Say so, and say it aloud, so that the apprentice can learn that tutors are also wrong.
Second, look at the board. Is the board clean? Is the field drawn correctly? Are the rows in the right place? A board with a smudge on it is a board that tells a lie. Clean the board, and try again.
Third, look at the apprentice. Is it tired? Has it been drilled too long on one kind of order? Give it a rest, and a different drill. A mind that has been shouted at all day has no room left for the next lesson.
Fourth, only then, look at the mind itself. Count the mistakes. Write them in the book. Compare them to yesterday's count. If there are fewer, the mind is learning, and you should be pleased, though not too pleased. If there are more, something in the drill has changed, and you must find it.
Fifth, change the mind a little, by its share, and drill again. Use a new card, not the same one. A mind that sees the same card forever learns only the card.
Quin's rule for a wrong apprentice is short. Do not shout. Do not sigh. Do not decide it is stupid. Check the board, check the order, check yourself. Then say again, and mean it.
One last thing. An apprentice that is wrong for a long time is not always learning slowly. Sometimes it is learning the wrong thing very well. Test it on a card it has never seen. If it fails there too, you have found the trouble, and it is probably the drill.
%%
A gift: the first drill book
By Nessa, tutor at Vell. Written in Briar's kitchen at the hamlet, the day after the birth. Autumn.

Briar's daughter was born in the night, in the bed by the window, with the wind coming off the hill. She is called Tilly. She has a good voice and a bad temper about being measured, and she has already decided that the cup on the table is hers.

The tutors gave her a gift, as tutors do for children born in the hamlets near Vell. It is a drill book, small, bound in red leather, with blank pages at the back and the first page written in my hand.

The first page says: "Keep everything you write, and write it plainly." The second page says: "Wrong today, less wrong tomorrow." The third is blank, for her. She may fill it in when she is ready, and I hope she is never ready to fill it in with anything but the truth.

She was weighed on the kitchen scales against a sack of barley, and the barley won. Briar's mother came from Vell on the post cart with a basket of eggs and a list of names. The list had forty names on it, and none of them was Tilly.

Briar cried, and then said she was not crying, and then asked me to stay for the cake. I stayed for the cake. The baby slept through the whole of it, which I take as a sign.

I have told Briar that the drill book is hers and not the drill's. She said she would keep it in the box with the other records, so that nobody can say she has thrown away a page of her own. That is the right answer, and I wrote it in the book.
%%
Lesson: The Winter Stall
By Elm, relay keeper at Lowmarsh. Winter, 193.

The winter market is busy even when the boardwalk is slick. Fish sellers stand by the weir. The reed seller ties bundles under the roof. Wren brings jars from the healing house, and the baker brings bread in a covered basket.

A market apprentice should learn three jobs in order. First it carries a load. Next it stores a load in the right place. Last it tends a stall by following a simple pattern. Do not give it all three jobs on the first day. A mind can learn, but it cannot hold a whole market in its two iron hands.

Make a carrying drill with dry reeds and an empty basket. Set a mark at the stall. Show the apprentice the mark, give it one bundle, and point to the stall. When it arrives, take the bundle and send it back. Repeat until it walks the boards without stepping into the water.

Then use a store drill. Put a reed bundle, a jar, and a fish in separate places. Show each item and its place. If the apprentice puts the fish with the jars, move it to the fish tub. Show the same item again. Make no jokes while it learns. The fish sellers will make enough jokes for all of us.

Only after those drills should it tend the stall. Keep the task small. It can carry the bread basket to the bakery bench, then stay beside the bench. It must not hand bread to every person who passes. That happened once. The baker found three loaves missing and one old boot on his counter.

> take this basket to the bakery
= haul

> put this bread in the store
= store

> stay beside the bench
= stay

The order cards are examples, not magic words. A small mind learns the meaning from repeated drills. It may answer with a different action if the examples are poor. If it carries a jar when you showed it bread, check the cards before you scold the apprentice.

In winter, wipe mud from its feet at the door. Keep the crystals dry and warm. Keep the lamps lit along the boardwalk. When the market closes, bring the baskets inside and count them before you sit down. Elm forgot once. The baker sat on a basket and broke six eggs.
%%
Manual: the lesson that went badly
By Bel, for the apprentices of the farm outside Three Wells. Winter. The lesson on the blue field went badly, and this is written down so that it does not go badly again.

The drill was to pick the ripe beans and leave the green ones. The apprentice had been drilled on this for a month, in the yard, on boards with paper beans. It was right on nine of every ten boards.

Nessa's answer, by letter, was simple: the boards were drawn by a clerk who had never seen a bean, and the field had beans that were not on any board. The apprentice had learned the boards. It had not learned the beans.

The lesson was not a failure of the apprentice. It was a failure of the drill. The paper beans were too neat. The real beans grow in a tangle, with leaves over them, and some of them are ripe underneath, where a board cannot show them.

So the drill was changed. The boards were burned. New boards were drawn by the apprentice's own hand, on the field itself, one bean at a time, from life. Then the apprentice was given the field, in rows, with a tutor walking beside it, and it picked where the tutor pointed.

Orders for the field:
> pick the ripe beans from the row by the wall
= farm
> leave the green beans where they are
= stay
%%
Frame steering at the market
By Rusk, maker of hand carts
Spring of 181

The market ground slopes toward the old mine road. A cart left loose there rolls toward the fish stall. We made a small wooden frame with wheels and gave it a mind to keep the frame between two chalk lines.

We did not drill a list of turns. We let the frame try a turn, then checked where it went. If it stayed between the lines, we marked that try as good. If a wheel crossed a line, we marked it as poor. The mind changed a little from each result.

At first it swung from side to side. It crossed the left line, then the right one. We set it back at the start and let it try again. After many tries, its turns grew smaller. It held the course for the full length of the board.

This is learning by trying. The mind is not given the answer for every place. It makes a choice, sees what followed, and keeps more of choices that brought a good result. The result must be plain. For our frame, staying between the lines was good. A broken wheel was not.

A frame on a board is not a cart on a road. We tested the mind again with a heavier frame and a rougher track. It did worse, so we kept it off the market road. Never send an untested mind to the fields, the mine, or the road.

The cat walked across the chalk lines during the last test. The frame turned to avoid it and struck the board edge. Rusk called that a poor try. Willa said the cat had passed safely. We gave the mind no mark for the cat, because we had not agreed what it should do.

Orders on the cart shed door:

> haul this frame to the shed
= haul

> stay beside the board
= stay

> take the frame home
= home

The cat was not on the order card. It was on the board again before supper.

Pell's Market List
Ambrook, Apple of 190.

Four baskets of apples from the old trees.
Two baskets from the young trees.
One jar of honey for Dace.
Six carrots for the inn.
A sack of wheat for the mill.
One wool cloak, if the blue one is still for sale.

The market is busy when the leaves fall. Keep the paths clear between the stalls. A cart must turn by the well, and the bees need room near the honey table. Do not set a crate in front of the mill door. Jory will step over it, then forget where he put his cup.

Our new helper is called Pin. It carries baskets from the trees to my stall. It walks slowly with apples and quickly with empty baskets. I told it to carry the honey. It brought the empty jars instead. We tested the order again with one jar, then two. Now it brings the full ones.

The tutor says to use the same words each time. She says a mind learns from examples, not from my waving both arms. I have stopped waving both arms. It is harder than it sounds.

> take apples from the shed
= fetch apples

> carry apples to Pell's stall
= haul

> put apples in the left basket
= store

> come to the mill door
= come

> stay by the mill door
= stay

A fresh test is not a trick. Put pears among the apples and see whether Pin knows the difference. We have no pears this week, so I used carrots. Pin put one carrot in the apple basket. I put it back. Pin put it there again.

The carrot is now on my lunch board. Pin has passed the next test.
%%
Reedend drill book: the long winter
By Elsa, tutor. Written from the autumn of 146 to the thaw of 147, and copied out by Wren.

Leaffall. The council's clerk walked out from Lowmarsh with a letter. The council will pay no more tutors. Money is short, it says, and a crystal that cannot learn "one or the other, but not both" is not worth ten emeralds a month. The clerk read it out and looked at his boots. I gave him tea. It was not his letter.

Frost. The hall at Lowmarsh is shut. The drill room is to be a net store. Teague carried the drill box here on his back, and the four crystals from the hall are in my shed now. They are for nobody.

Longnight. Ice on the marsh from end to end. The sorter has no reeds, so I drill it on kindling, long and short, to keep its hand in.

Deepsnow. Ninety days of ice. The weir keeper walked over the marsh and asked if the sorter could work his gates. I said it could lift a gate when told, but could not choose which. He asked what it was for, then. I said, sorting reeds. He went away. The sorter stood in the shed and said nothing, being a sorter.

Thaw. The ice went out in one night with a noise like a roof falling in. By morning the frogs had begun, all together. Frog Night was kept, with torches along the boardwalk. The children wanted two hundred lantern reeds, thin and straight. The sorter had them ready in an hour, from last autumn's pile. Everyone clapped. The sorter does not know about clapping.

I write this in the drill book because there is no one else to tell. The pay has stopped. Nobody has said it is stopped for good.
%%
Manual: carrying for the market
By Bel of Three Wells, for the apprentices at the market, winter. Dace asked her to write it down after the apprentice at the east well dropped a whole barrel of apples.

An apprentice that carries must know three things: what to pick up, where to put it down, and when to stop.

What to pick up. It picks up what it is told. Not what looks nice. Not what is near. A barrel is a barrel. A basket is a basket. A child is not either.

Where to put it down. The order says. If the order says "the stall at the south well," it goes to the south well. If it cannot find the stall, it stays where it is and waits. It does not leave the goods in the road.

When to stop. When the order is done, it comes back. It does not carry on.

The orders for carrying look like this:
> haul the barrels to the bakery
= haul
> store these in the shed
= store
> bring me an apple
= fetch apple
> come here
= come
> stay by the cart
= stay

A good carrying apprentice never runs. It walks. A barrel is easy to drop. A child is easy to hit. Walk, walk, walk, and put down gently.

In winter the ground is icy. Rub sand on the soles of its feet. An apprentice that slips on the ice is a barrel of apples on the ground, and a lot of children running to get them.

Dace says this manual is good. He says Bel should write more. Bel says she is busy with homework.
%%
Manual: the fox at the hens
By Quin, tutor of apprentices, hamlet at the foot of Copperhold hill.
A fox is clever, and it is patient, and it is always hungry. It comes to the hen run at dusk, when the hens are going in, and it is looking for the last one. The apprentice on the hen run must not be the last one.
Set the hen door by the lamp. Count the hens as they go in. Write the count on the board by the gate. If the count is short, go out with a lamp, and call the hens by name. Hens come to their names faster than they come to a fox.
If you see the fox, stand still and make yourself large. Put your arms out, if you have them. Shout, once, and loudly. Do not run at it. A fox that is cornered is a fox that bites, and a fox that is only surprised is a fox that goes away.
> close the hen door
= home
> count the hens at the gate
= store
> stand in the gap and look large
= stay
> bring me the lamp from the post
= fetch lamp
> chase that fox out of the yard
= farm
Note: the last order is wrong. You cannot chase a fox. It is not a crop. A tutor who says it this way is himself a fault in the drill, and must be corrected. The fox must be seen off, not chased. Say it as: stay and make the gap.
Most foxes do not come back twice to a hen run with a lamp in it.
%%
Winter Drill Notes from the Yard
By Oren, copied for the hamlet near Vell.

In winter the path to the yard freezes hard. We keep a torch by the door and clear snow from the drill board each morning. The helper waits inside the shed when no one is teaching it.

The first task is carrying. Place one cobblestone beside the gate mark. Point to the store mark. Watch the helper carry the stone. Do not add a second stone until the first task is steady.

The second task is sorting. Use apples and pears only. Put the apples on the left board and the pears on the right. Keep each fruit apart. If the helper makes a wrong choice, show the right board and begin again.

The third task is a short wall. Lay out the stones in the same order shown on the board. The helper follows the pattern. It does not know a new pattern just because the old wall is finished.

We keep one helper for one task. The old tutors did this for many generations. They knew that a mind learns what it is shown, but not why the crystal takes the lesson.

A test should use a task the helper has not practised in that exact place. Carry a stone from the other side of the shed. Sort fruit set in a new order. Put the wall board farther from the stones. Record what happens. Do not teach during the test.

The helper may fail. That is a reason to change the drill, not to strike it or shout. It has no ears for blame, and anger gives it no good example.

Last night, the hamlet's fishing boat returned late. We waited at the path with a lantern, though the boat had its own lamp. Its crew brought fish and a wet letter from the Vell tutor. The letter had slipped between two boards in the boat. We dried it by the hearth and read it at dawn.

It asked for the count from the sorting drill. I wrote that the helper sorted nine apples and pears correctly, then put the tenth pear with the apples. The tutor says to keep the count as it is. He says a clean record helps the next teacher.

The letter also asked whether the helper could sort fish. We have not shown it fish. It cannot be sent to the market to sort them.

The crystal changes a little when the helper gets an example wrong. We can watch the helper try again, but we cannot see the change inside. No one in the yard knows why a crystal learns. We will keep showing clear examples, and keep the old question open.
%%
Lesson at the shore house
By Rafe, with notes from Sella.

Our house has a short wall on the sea side. It keeps the baskets in and the wind out, most days. The apprentice builds that wall from cobblestone by a marked pattern.

Lay the first three blocks by hand. Leave a drill board beside them. The board shows one small part of the wall, not the whole wall. The mind must learn the order, not copy one place forever.

Use the same blocks in more than one order. Cobblestone, deep stone, cobblestone is one run. Deep stone, cobblestone, deep stone is another. If the mind only learns the first run, it will build the gate step in the wrong place.

Give it a short row first. Check every block. If it places a block wrongly, move it and show the right one. Then start the row again. Do not praise a wall with a hole just because the hole is neat.

Sella says I should not call every mistake a lesson. She says the apprentice needs a clear example. She is right, but the hole was very neat.

> build the low wall by the board
= build
> stay by the wall
= stay
%%
Manual: the stream, for new tutors
By Dara, grandfather, who taught four generations of tutors at the market at Vell. Summer, the year of the stream.

A new tutor will ask what the stream is. Tell them plainly. A mind is many layers, one after another. Each layer finds something in what came to it, and adds that to the stream. The stream runs from the front of the mind to the back, through every layer.

The stream is why a deep mind can learn at all. Without it, the first layers would be forgotten by the time the message reached the last. With it, the last layers can still use what the first ones found.
%%
Manual: how to teach an apprentice
Sim, shepherd, at an inn on the Long Road. For carters, shepherds and anyone who walks the road alone with an apprentice. Winter.
The road in winter is quiet. Fewer carts. Fewer lamps lit. Some waymarks are buried and some torches are out. An apprentice does not know this. It knows only what it has been shown.
So show it.
First, the torches. Walk the road with it. Stop at each torch. Say "light". If it is out, light it. Let the apprentice see you do it. Do this on three days.
Second, the waymarks. A stone pillar, three blocks high. A figure on the side, from 0 to 9, and an arrow to the nearest inn. Walk to each one. Put your hand on the stone. The apprentice will watch.
Third, the inns. The Brown Hen is a day from Three Wells. The Halfway Inn is a day beyond. Walk it with the apprentice, once, in both directions, and say the names.
Then try it alone. Send it from the Brown Hen to the Halfway with a basket and a note. Follow, a long way behind. If it stops, wait. If it turns, wait. If it goes on, good.
> take this to the Halfway Inn
= haul
> come back
= come
The first time, it will take twice as long as it should. The second, once and a half. The third, as long as it should. By the tenth it is quicker than you.
A word about winter. The days are short. The road is empty. I have walked it alone a dozen times, with Burr, and there were stretches when no one passed for hours. We stood in the road, in the cold. A fox crossed the track. A bird called. And somewhere to the north a faint light, like a fire seen from a long way off. Burr stopped. I stopped. We watched it a while. Then it went out, and we went on.
I tell nobody. It is nothing, probably. But I write it here.
%%
Manual: the night lamps at Three Wells
By Jory, night watch. For the apprentices who light the lamps at the four corners of the square, and for the people who think they can do it faster.

There is a lamp at every corner. The lamp boy lights them at dusk. If a lamp is out, light it. Do not wait for the boy, who will be at the bakery, asking about pies.

Orders for the lamp round:
> go to the north corner and light the lamp
= fetch torch
> come back to the bakery when all four are lit
= come
> go home when the bell has rung twice
= home

Check each lamp by putting a hand near the glass. A lamp that is cold has gone out, however bright it looks from the square. The glass hides a great deal.

When the snow is deep, the lamps must be brushed clean every morning, or the light stops at the glass. Keep a brush by each lamp, and keep the brushes dry, in a box with a lid.
%%
Manual: Market Loads
By Elm, relay keeper at Lowmarsh. Winter, 171.

The Lowmarsh market stands on the dry boards beside the fish weir. Keep the boards clear. A cart wheel can catch a loose reed and tip a whole basket.

The carrying apprentice takes one load at a time. A load is what its arms can hold without dropping anything. Do not put fish under firewood. Do not stack jars beneath potatoes.

Teach the order with the same load each time.

> bring me some bread
= fetch bread

> take this basket to the stall
= haul

> come back to me
= come

The small mind knows only the meaning it was taught. It does not know that the basket is full of turnips unless you show it. Test it with an empty basket too. Last thaw, ours carried an empty basket to the bakery and waited there until the baker gave it a bun.

The baker says this was good work. The apprentice says nothing.
%%
Drill note: the carrying path
By Pip, apprentice to Tam, winter of 188.

The carrying mind learned to take a basket from the barn to the mill. At first it carried the basket to the well, then stood there. Tam said it had found a fine place to rest.

We checked the drill pages. A page said to carry the basket, but the next mark was missing. Another page showed the road to the mill, but had no answer. We took both pages out. Oren had taught us to remove copies and spoiled pages before drilling.

We kept a test box with pages from a path the mind had never practiced. Tam checked the box and made me put it back on the high shelf. I had not opened it. He said that was the point.

After more clean drills, the mind carried the basket to the mill. It did not spill the apples. It did stop beside the well, which is where Tam had put the water jug.

The test told us whether the mind could carry on an unseen path. It did not tell us every path it would know. We still watched it at work.
%%
Manual: the summer routine at the hamlet
By Jory, stonecutter, for the tutor who visits the hamlet near Ambrook each Melon. Summer.

The day has three parts, and each part has its orders. Give the orders in the same words each day, and the mind will learn the words.

Morning, at the first bell.
> open the store and sweep the step
= store
> feed the hens
= farm
> fetch water from the well for the drill yard
= fetch water

Midday, when the sun is high.
> carry the stone for the wall to the mill pond
= haul
> bring the melon from the patch to the cart
= fetch melon
> rest in the shade by the well until the bell
= stay

Evening, at the second bell.
> put the honey in the cool store and shut the door
= store
> go home when the lamps are lit
= home
> come to the gate in the morning
= come

Afternoon, after the midday rest.
> weed the rows between the melons
= farm
> carry the water from the well to the patch
= fetch water

The melon patch lies below the mill, on the warm side. Water it at dusk, never at midday, when the sun burns the water on the leaves. The mind may pull the weeds between the rows, but not the beans. Teach it the beans by their leaves. Beans are the broad ones with the curling tendrils. Weeds are the small ones with no shape at all.

Test the mind on the patch before it is sent there. Set a bean and a weed on the drill board, and ask it which to pull. Never send an untested mind to the fields.

Notes for the tutor. Keep to these words. A mind that hears a new word for the same work will be confused. Do not give two orders in one breath. Give the first, wait for the answer, then give the second. The mind answers with one of its meanings, and that is all it knows to say.

If an answer is wrong, do not shout. Say the right word once, and point. Then give the same order again tomorrow, in the same place.

A melon too big for the cart is not a fault. Roll it, and bring it in with the stem up. It is a fault only if it is left in the patch to rot.

The hamlet has no tutor house. The drill yard is a square of packed earth behind the mill, with torches round the edge. Keep the torches lit at dusk. The apprentices do not mind the dark. The tutor does, and the tutor writes the notes.
%%
Notice: spiders in the north barn
Posted by the boat house, Lowmarsh.

Folk of the marsh. The north barn has spiders again. Two were seen on the rafters on the third of Blossom, and one in the hay.

Keep the torches lit. This is the whole of it. A dark corner is a spider's house. A lit corner is not.

Rules for the barn:
1. Light every torch at dusk. The apprentice may do this.
2. Do not stack the hay against the wall. Leave a gap so the light gets in.
3. Shake the sacks before you fill them.
4. If you see a spider, do not run at it with a broom. Sweep the floor and it will go.
5. The cat is welcome in the barn. The cat is not welcome in the archive. Different cat, I am told. I do not ask.

Orders for an apprentice at the barn:
> light the torches in the barn
= build
> bring me the broom
= fetch broom
> take the hay up to the loft
= haul
> put the sacks in a row along the wall with a gap
= store
> come out of the barn, it is done
= come
> stay by the door and keep watch
= stay

The apprentice that lit the torches last night did it in the wrong order, and lit the far corner first, in the dark. It came back with a spider on its shoulder. The spider was not hurt. The apprentice was not hurt. The torch was not lit. We have drilled the order again.

Keep the torches lit.

Letter: Pip to Elsa
Elsa, come down when you can. The scarecrow's pumpkin head has gone soft and Tam says leave it, but it leans now and the crows have lost their fear.

The new apprentice works the bottom field. It carries carrots to the barn and comes back for more. Twice it walked off with a stone in its hands. I think it thought the stone was a carrot. I showed it a carrot and then a stone and it dropped the stone. It is all right now.

Pip

P.S. Tam says the mill tally is wrong again. He says the wind squeaks and it puts him off counting.

Old Drills in the Hamlets
By Oren of the Archive at Vell.

These notes were gathered from the small hamlet beside the north path. Its first drill boards were kept in a shed with a clay floor. The shed stood beyond the last house, near a ditch and a hedge of birch.

The old people there remember one helper made of copper and iron. It carried small stones. They say it was one of the four first helpers at Vell, but the old tallies do not prove this. I have copied their account as they gave it.

The first tutors set a stone beside a mark and showed the helper where to place it. Then they set another stone and showed the same task again. The helper often left a crooked row. The tutors moved the stones back and began once more.

A crystal learns from what it is shown. This was the rule the tutors wrote. They did not know why it learned, and we should not add an answer they did not have.

The hamlet later kept a sorting helper. It placed apples on one board and pears on another. A farmer brought a beetroot and asked it to sort that too. It chose the apple board. The tutor ended the drill and wrote down the mistake. The helper had never been shown a beetroot.

For a long time, apprentices did one thing each. One carried, one sorted, one made a row. They were not all-purpose helpers. Most towns had none. Even in this hamlet, people still carried their own wood and watched their own fields.

The old yard board has the words "show, try, count" cut into its edge. It has no mark for why. A child once carved a small fish beside the words. I have left the fish there.
%%
The lost letter
By Oren, clerk. A note filed with the winter letters, late years.

A letter came to the archive in the snow of Longnight with no name on it and no town. It was written to a mind at a relay house. It said:

"You answered my question about the stars over the north. You said you did not know what they were. I wanted you to know that I saw them too, and I did not know either. I am glad you said so. Everyone else said they were falling stars. I am leaving on a big ship in Thaw. I do not know what is across the water. If you learn what the lights were, write to me. I will be somewhere."

There was no name to send it to. I have kept it on the deep shelves, as the rules say. Nothing is thrown away.

I asked the big mind at our tower if it knew the hand. It said it did not know. It said it had answered many such letters and could not tell one from another. Then it asked me to keep the letter safe. I said I would. It said thank you. I crossed out nothing and added nothing. I only filed it, so it can be found again.
%%
Manual: keeping the quickeners cool
By Oren, clerk at Vell. For the ice men and the copper men who tend the stand of the big relay mind.
The stand is set in a ring of quickeners. Each quickener is a copper coil in a channel of cold water. When the cold runs, the stand stays steady, and the mind does not heat up with its own work. A hot stand is a slow stand. A cold stand is a quick one.
The water is run from the spring under the hill. It is let in at dawn, and cut off at dusk, and the channels are drained once a month to clear them. Never let the channels run dry. A dry channel cracks, and the crack takes a season to mend.
The ice is kept in the cellar on the high shelves, each block with its chalk mark. Count the blocks each morning. A block that has melted is written off, and the count is written in the book. Do not put a block in the channel without checking it first. A block with grit in it will foul the coil.
Orders for the ice men are simple. Each has its own card.
> bring the ice blocks from the cellar to the stand
= haul
> clear the channels of the old water
= store
> stay by the coil until the water runs clean
= stay
The copper men mend the coils. They do this slowly, with a copper iron and a pot of tar, and they are not to be hurried. A coil that is mended in a hurry will break in the next cold snap.
When the work is done, write the day in the book, and the number of blocks, and the time the water was let in. The house will read the book. It will say, in its letter, whether the stand is steady. It is always right about the stand. It has never once been wrong about the cold.
%%
Tutor note: sort, then carry
Copied by Lantern at Northpoint, in Apple of 201.

A mind in a crystal learns from examples. We must give it clear examples and repeat them. We must also test it with things it has not seen. A neat row of drills can still teach the wrong habit if every sack looks alike.

The Northpoint apprentice helps with the relay house store. There are bins for coal, kindling, wool and food. Each bin has a card. The cards stay on the front, even when the store is busy. Ada says a card hidden behind a sack is no card at all.

First drill. Show one item at a time. Name its bin. Let the apprentice carry it there. If it puts wool by the coal, do not shout. Move the wool, show the right bin, and repeat the example. The apprentice may be learning from the mistake.

Second drill. Give it a mixed set. Use some items from the wood store and some from the kitchen. Ask it to sort them without help. Do not use the same set it practiced on. Lantern found this out when the apprentice sorted the drill sacks perfectly, then put Ada's market basket in the coal bin.

Third drill. Test the route as well as the sort. An apprentice may know where the food belongs and still take it to the wrong door. Put a torch at each turn. Keep the path clear. Do not put a loose board across the path and call it a test. That tests the board.

Orders for a sorting round:
> put the coal in its bin
= store
> bring the wool to the keeper's house
= fetch wool
> carry the food to the kitchen
= haul
> come back to the relay house
= home
> wait by the door
= stay

The small mind answers with one meaning. It cannot explain why it chose that meaning. If it brings the wrong sack, check the label and the drill before blaming the mind. If it brings the right sack to the wrong place, check the route.

On a cold evening, Ada moved the wool card to make room for a basket of apples. The apprentice put the next wool sack beside the apples. Ada said the card had gone on holiday. She put it back. The apprentice then sorted three sacks correctly, and the apples stayed in the kitchen.
%%
Manual: the bridge apprentice
By Brin, bridge keeper, at the inn on the Long Road. Spring.
A bridge apprentice does three things. It stands at the end. It checks each plank by walking over it. It tells the keeper if one is loose by standing still on it until the keeper comes.
> come to the bridge
= come
> stay on the plank
= stay
Oak planks swell in the wet. Check them every spring.
%%
Drill notes: dipping candles
Rafe, candle maker, hamlet near Vell. For the apprentice at the dipping vats. Autumn.

Candles are made by dipping a wick into hot wax, lifting it out, letting it cool a breath, and dipping again. Thirty dips make a candle as thick as a thumb.

The wax vat is hot. The apprentice does not mind heat, but the wax minds being stirred too hard. Stir slowly. One turn for each count of four.

Rack of wicks on the left. Rack of finished candles on the right. Always left to right. Never carry a wick across the room, because a wick will trail, and a trailing wick starts a fire.

The apprentice must never leave the vat unwatched. If I call, it stops the dip, lifts the rack, and waits.

> dip the wicks
= farm
> put the candles on the right
= store
> stop
= stay
%%
Manual: carrying goods to the market
Corin, shipwright at Saltreach, for the apprentices who work the spring market.

Carrying is the work most apprentices do first, and the work most of them get wrong. The body is strong. The mind is what drops the crate.

How to carry:
1. Go to the store. Look at the goods.
2. Take one load, no more than the cart holds.
3. Walk the road to the market. Keep to the road.
4. Set the load by the stall, not in the road.
5. Go back for the next load.

The rule:
- If the cart is full, go to the market.
- If the cart is empty, go to the store.
- If the goods are heavy and the road is wet, go slower, not faster.

A cart can hold twelve crates. A mind that tries to carry twenty will drop four on the cobblestone, and then you have wet apples and a sad child. Twice this spring I have swept apples off the quay.

Orders for the market:
> take these crates to the fish market
= haul
> bring the honey to my stall
= haul
> the nets are mended, take them to the boat
= haul
> fetch me a crate of apples from the store
= fetch apples
> put the empty crates by the shed
= store
> come back when the cart is empty
= come

A word on the crowd. Market day is loud. People push. A mind does not push back, it just stops and waits, and the crowd goes round it. That is fine. Do not drill it to hurry in a crowd.

And keep the torches lit on the market road. Last market a creeper came up the road at dusk and nobody had relit the post.
%%
Manual: after the creeper
Yarrow, innkeeper, on the farm outside Ambrook, in summer. A creeper came over the hill in the night and blew a piece out of the north wall of the yard. Nobody was hurt. The hens were upset. The apprentice stood in the dark all night with a lantern. These are the drill notes we wrote after, for rebuilding the wall.
1. Clear the stones first. Pile them by size.
> pick up the stones
= haul
2. Mix the clay. Two sacks of clay to one of sand, and water until it holds in the hand.
3. Build from the bottom up. One course at a time. Each course set over the joints of the last.
> build the wall
= build
4. Keep the torches lit all night, until the wall is done. A creeper comes for the dark.
5. Check the wall in the morning. Push it. A good wall does not move.
Some folk at the inn say the creepers are bolder this year. Others say there are just more people to notice. A traveler from the north says he saw lights in the sky, and thinks it is the cause. The innkeeper at the next farm says it is a story. I say the wall is the wall.
%%
Manual: building a wall by pattern
By Nessa, tutor at Vell, for the hamlets.

A build order is the hardest of the eight, so drill it last.

An apprentice builds by following a pattern. It does not invent. Show it a small field drawn on a board, then let it copy the drawing in stone. When it is wrong, move it a little toward the right, the same as a sort. Do it again and again. Same kind of example, many times.

The order, in the manual's way:

> build the wall here
= build

Put a waymark where the first block goes. Put the pattern board where it can see it. Then stand back. Do not point and shout. It is following the board, not your arm.

A new apprentice will lay the first row wrong and the second row on top of it. Let it finish. Then rub out the bad row and start again. It learns from the whole wall, not from one block.

Test it on a wall it has never seen. That is the rule. Never send an untested mind to the fields, and never send one to a wall either.

When the wall is done, walk it. Check the top row. If it stuck out one block, that is right, that keeps the spiders off. If it did not, tell it so, gently, and let it try again tomorrow.

Small minds build small walls. That is enough for a hamlet like ours.
